 FOREWORD

This is the seventy-eighth in a series of semiannual technical progress reports on fusion materials science
activity supported by the Fusion Energy Sciences Program of the U.S. Department of Energy. It covers
the period ending June 30th, 2025. This report focuses on research addressing the effects on materials
properties and performance of exposure to the neutronic, thermal and chemical environments anticipated
in the chambers of fusion experiments and energy systems. This research is a major element of the
national effort to establish the materials knowledge base for an economically and environmentally
attractive fusion energy source. Research activities on issues related to the interaction of materials with
plasmas are reported separately.

The results reported are the products of a national effort involving a number of national laboratories and
universities. A large fraction of this work, particularly in relation to fission reactor irradiations, is carried
out collaboratively with international partners, e.g., Japan and the UK. The purpose of this series of
reports is to provide a working technical record for the use of program participants, and to provide a
means of communicating the efforts of fusion materials scientists to the broader fusion community, both
nationally and worldwide.

This report has been compiled by Stephanie Melton, Oak Ridge National Laboratory. Her efforts, and the
efforts of the many persons who made technical contributions, are gratefully acknowledged.

 John Echols
 Research Division
 Office of Fusion Energy Sciences

 1. FERRITIC/MARTENSITIC STEEL DEVELOPMENT

 1

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

1.1 RADIATION INDUCED Cr SEGREGATION AT GRAIN BOUNDARIES IN Fe-3Cr AFTER PROTON
IRRADIATION — Y. Zhao (University of Tennessee), S.J. Zinkle (University of Tennessee and Oak Ridge
National Laboratory)

This study investigates radiation-induced solute segregation at grain boundaries in Fe-Cr binary alloys
under proton irradiation.

SUMMARY
Three materials, Fe-3Cr, Fe-5Cr and Fe-18Cr, were irradiated with 800 keV protons to a mid-range dose of
0.35 displacement per atom (dpa) at 300 and 500 °C. Following irradiation, electron backscatter diffraction
(EBSD) was performed to determine the misorientation angle of grain boundaries, after which site-specific
lift-outs were conducted to prepare thin-foil specimens. Scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM)
combined with energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS) mapping was then used to characterize the
spatial distribution of Cr at grain boundaries. The primary progress includes the successful completion of
the irradiation experiments and the acquisition and analysis of data for the irradiated Fe-3Cr.

Sample preparation
The as-received Fe-Cr specimens were sectioned into rectangular pieces with dimensions of 2 mm × 5 mm
× 1 mm or into disk-shaped samples with a diameter of 3 mm and a thickness of 1 mm using either electric
discharge machining (EDM) or a low-speed saw. The samples were ground to ~0.4 mm thickness using
SiC sandpaper, followed by sequential polishing with 3 to 0.5 μm diamond suspensions. Final surface
preparation was performed by vibrational polishing with 0.02 μm colloidal silica to produce a mirror-like
surface.
Ion irradiation
The polished specimens were mounted on a Cu plate with silver paste. Irradiation experiments were
conducted at the Michigan Ion Beam Laboratory (MIBL) using 800 keV protons at 300 and 500 °C. The ion
flux and total fluence were 1.75×1014 ions/cm2×s and 3.1×1018 ions/cm2, respectively, corresponding to a
dose rate of 2×10-5 dpa/s at the mid-range depth of 3 um and achieving a dose of 0.35 dpa after 5 h of
irradiation.
The displacement damage as a function of depth was predicted using SRIM, as plotted in Fig. 1.
Calculations used the full-cascade damage energy method [1] with the displacement energy of Fe set to
40 eV. During irradiation, specimen surface temperatures were monitored using four thermocouples and a
thermal imager with points of interest placed on the samples, indicating stable temperature histories with
fluctuations within ±5 °C.

Figure 1. SRIM prediction of the irradiation displacement damage as a function of depth.

 2

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

TEM sample preparation and characterization
TEM specimens were prepared using a FEI Helios Plasma FIB-SEM system. Following irradiation, EBSD
was performed to identify grain boundaries with selected misorientation angles. A thin Pt strip was first
deposited using the electron beam to mark the target location, followed by deposition of a ~3 μm thick W
layer using the ion beam to protect the surface from ion milling. Standard FIB lift-out procedures were then
used to prepare thin lamellae, followed by final cleaning at 2 kV and 20 pA for 10 min on each side to
remove damage from high-voltage ion milling.
The thin-foil specimens were examined using a Thermo Fisher Spectra 300 aberration-corrected
TEM/STEM operated at 200 kV. Specimens were tilted to minimize the projected width of the grain
boundary, enabling near edge-on imaging. EDS maps were acquired with a resolution of 1024 × 1024 pixels
over a 3 μm depth, with the scan width adjusted to fully cover the grain boundary, corresponding to a spatial
resolution of ~3 nm per pixel. The probe current was ~500 pA, and the dwell time per pixel was adjusted to
maintain a frame acquisition time of ~10 s. Data acquisition continued until the X-ray counts satisfied the
quantification criteria of the Thermo Fisher Velox software. Multiple EDS maps were collected to cover both
the irradiated region and depths beyond the irradiation range, extending up to ~9 μm from the surface or to
the intersection of the grain boundary with the edge of the thin foil window.
The raw data were analyzed using Thermo Fisher Velox software. Absorption correction was applied using
specimen thickness measured by EELS and a density of 7.9 g/cm3. Quantification used the Schreiber–
Wims ionization cross-section model, which provides improved accuracy for transition metals. For each
EDS map, concentration line profiles were extracted perpendicular to the grain boundary and averaged
over a length of 500 nm. The grain boundary composition was defined as the peak or valley concentration
in the profile, or as the concentration at the grain boundary position when no clear enrichment or depletion
was observed.

PRELIMINARY RESULTS
Fig. 2 presents bright field (BF) images and Cr elemental maps for Fe-3Cr irradiated at 300 °C. Two
specimens were extracted from a low-angle grain boundary (LAGB, misorientation ~10°) and a high-angle
grain boundary (HAGB, misorientation ~50°). From the surface to mid-range depth, both boundaries exhibit
slight Cr segregation. In the BF images, a high density of dislocation loops appear at a depth of ~5.5 µm,
slightly deeper than the ~4.5 µm peak damage depth predicted by SRIM. At depths where the loop density
decreases sharply, the Cr enrichment at grain boundaries correspondingly becomes less pronounced.
Beyond the irradiated region, only isolated Cr-rich clusters are observed, likely Cr nitrides or carbides. No
clear difference in Cr enrichment or depletion behavior is observed between the low- and high-angle grain
boundaries.

 3

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Bright field images and Cr elemental maps at the low-angle and high-angle grain boundaries of
Fe-3Cr irradiated at 300 °C.
The quantified Cr concentration difference between the two grain boundaries and bulk as a function of
depth is shown in Fig. 3 (a, b). Both LAGB and HAGB exhibit nearly constant Cr enrichment, with a slight
increase up to ~5.5 μm, corresponding to the depth with the highest dislocation loop density in the BF
images. Beyond this depth, Cr enrichment gradually decreases toward zero. Depths showing distinct Cr-
rich clusters are indicated by empty symbols and were excluded from the trend lines. Within the irradiation
region, HAGB exhibits higher Cr enrichment (1-2%) compared to LAGB (0-1%).

 4

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. Quantified Cr concentration differences between grain boundaries and the bulk as a function of
depth for (a) LAGB and (b) HAGB after irradiation at 300 °C, and for (c) LAGB and (d) HAGB after irradiation
at 500 °C.
BF images and Cr EDS maps for specimens irradiated at 500 °C are shown in Fig. 4, with quantified Cr
concentrations plotted in Fig. 3(c, d). Similar to the 300 °C results, both LAGB and HAGB exhibit Cr
enrichment from mid-range to the end of the irradiation region. A notable difference is the presence of a Cr-
depletion region near the specimen surface. For the LAGB, surface Cr enrichment coincides with oxygen
signals. From mid-range to the end of the irradiation region, pronounced localized Cr-enrichment islands
are observed, appearing as extra dark contrast in BF images superimposed on smooth grain boundaries.
Fig. 5 provides zoomed views at ~2 μm depth, where dislocation overlap is minimal, and at the peak
damage depth, where significant overlap occurs. It can be observed grain boundaries exhibit Cr enrichment
even without dislocation overlap, while locations with overlapping dislocations show enhanced enrichment.
Cr enrichment associated with matrix dislocation loops is also visible. These observations indicate that
irradiation induces Cr segregation under these conditions, and the accumulation and overlap of dislocation
loops at grain boundaries contribute to additional enrichment. Beyond the irradiation depth, Cr enrichment
gradually diminishes and disappears beyond ~7 μm. For the HAGB, Cr nitrides or carbides, distinguishable
by elongated brighter contrast in the Cr maps and higher enrichment levels (empty symbols) in Fig. 4(d),
are present at multiple depths. At other locations, Cr depletion is observed near the surface (up to ~2 μm)
and beyond the damaged region. Compared with the LAGB 500 °C and HAGB 300 °C results, Cr
segregation behavior at the HAGB 500 °C does not follow a consistent trend, suggesting that the presence
of Cr nitrides or carbides may have a stronger influence on grain boundary Cr distribution at higher
temperatures.

 5

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 4. BF images and Cr elemental maps at the low-angle and high-angle grain boundaries of Fe-3Cr
irradiated at 500 °C.

Figure 5. BF images and Cr elemental maps at the low-angle grain boundaries of Fe-3Cr irradiated at 500
°C. (a, b) are from ~2 µm below surface and (c, d) are from ~5.5 µm below surface.

SUMMARY AND FUTURE WORK
STEM-EDS analysis revealed radiation-induced Cr enrichment at the majority of grain boundaries in Fe-
3Cr at 300 and 500 °C, regardless of grain boundary misorientation. Several factors were found to influence
the observed Cr segregation, including surface, Cr nitrides or carbides, and overlap with dislocation loops.
Surface effects produced a Cr-depletion region up to ~2 μm at 500 °C but had minimal impact at 300 °C.
Cr nitrides/carbides and overlap with dislocation loops enhanced local enrichment. Accounting for these

 6

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

factors, enrichment was generally more pronounced at HAGB and at the higher irradiation temperature of
500 °C.
 To obtain more quantitative results, the following work will be carried out:
 1. An additional lift-out will be extracted from the 500 °C HAGB specimen to reduce the effect of Cr
 carbides/nitrides.
 2. STEM-EDS characterization of Fe-5Cr and Fe-18Cr will be performed to examine the effect of Cr
 concentration on grain boundary segregation.
 3. Two repeated irradiations will be conducted on Fe-10Cr and Fe-14Cr.

[1] S. Agarwal, Y. Lin, C. Li, R. Stoller, S. Zinkle, On the use of SRIM for calculating vacancy production:
Quick calculation and full-cascade options, Nuclear Instruments and Methods in Physics Research Section
B: Beam Interactions with Materials and Atoms 503 (2021) 11-29.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

1.2 ATOMISTIC MECHANISMS GOVERNING HYDROGEN STABILITY AT HELIUM CAVITY IN
REDUCED ACTIVATION FERRITIC–MARTENSITIC STEELS UNDER IRRADIATION — Lihao Shi, Logan
N. Clowers, Gary S. Was, Fei Gao (University of Michigan, Nuclear Engineering and Radiological Sciences)

The objective of this project is to understand the stability of the transmutation gas hydrogen (H) on helium
(He) cavity surfaces at elevated temperatures under irradiation in reduced activation ferritic–martensitic
(RAFM) steels.

SUMMARY
Experimental observations from triple-ion-irradiated RAFM steels reveal segregation of alloying elements,
particularly Cr and Ta, at helium cavity interfaces. Density functional theory (DFT) calculations were
performed to quantify H-trapping energetics at Fe–He interfaces and to evaluate the influence of key
alloying elements. Our results demonstrate that solute segregation significantly enhances H-binding
stability. We identify a dual stabilization mechanism: an electronic effect, where d-band hybridization
between solutes and iron strengthens the local metal–H bonds, and a surface topology effect, where the
local lattice strain and site availability at the bubble periphery are modified by the presence of solute atoms.
Meanwhile, helium accumulation alters the thermodynamic stability of defect–solute configurations near the
interface and can facilitate vacancy-associated solute segregation during bubble growth. These findings
provide atomistic insight into the coupled influence of helium and alloy chemistry on hydrogen behavior at
irradiation-induced defect interfaces and offer a theoretical explanation for the experimentally observed
hydrogen retention near helium cavities in reduced activation ferritic–martensitic steels.

Experiment
To evaluate this hypothesis of solute segregation at the cavity-matrix interface, a series of experimental
measurements were conducted on a triple ion irradiated reduced activation ferritic/martensitic steel using
energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS). Descriptions of the sample preparation and ion irradiation
procedures used to produce the triple ion irradiated F82H-IEA alloy are provided in previous work [1–3] and
will only be covered here briefly. The composition and heat treatment condition of F82H-IEA is shown in
Table 1. The irradiated materials discussed in this work were created using the triple beam capabilities at
the Michigan Ion Beam Laboratory (MIBL) at the University of Michigan. Irradiation bars were cut from the
bulk material via wire-EDM and subsequently polished until a sub-micron finish was obtained using
polycrystalline diamond slurry. Irradiations were each conducted in the Multi-Beam Chamber (MBC) using
a triple ion beam (Fe+H+He) configuration of 5 MeV Fe2+, 390 keV H+ and 2.85 MeV He2+ impinging upon
the irradiation stage at 0° (orthogonal to the samples), 30° and 60°, respectively. The H and He ion beams
were passed through two rotating Al-foil beam energy degraders to tailor the profile of the implanted ions
to the damage profile for a constant appm/dpa ratio around the nominal damage region [4]. These beam
energies resulted in the depth-dependent damage and implantation curves as determined using SRIM-
2013 where the Quick Kinchin-Pease model was used to evaluate the displacements per atom (dpa) and
gas injection rates (appm/dpa) following the recommendations of ASTM-E521-23 [5].
DFT Calculation
Previous experiments show that helium bubble facets in bcc iron preferentially align with {100} planes [6],
while first-principles studies indicate that the Fe(110) surface exhibits the strongest hydrogen binding
among common facets [7–9]. Accordingly, the (100) and (110) surfaces were selected as representative
orientations. Because He–He interactions are largely non-directional and helium clusters display liquid-like
behavior governed primarily by atomic density [10,11], ordered simple cubic (sc) and body-centered cubic
(bcc) helium lattices were used to represent He/V ratios of 0.5 and 1. Surface slabs were constructed from
relaxed bcc Fe, with a vacuum region introduced along the surface normal and the bottom 5 Å fixed to
mimic bulk constraints. Helium assemblies were placed above the surface and fully relaxed to obtain stable
Fe–He interfacial structures. Substitutional solutes (Cr, W, and Ta) were introduced in the top Fe layer with
coverages of 0.1, 0.5, and 1 monolayer (ML). All calculations were performed using VASP with the PAW
method and the PBE functional, employing a 520 eV cutoff, a Γ-centered 5×5×1 k-point mesh, and

 8

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

convergence criteria of 10–5 eV in energy and 0.01 eV Å–1 in force. Hydrogen binding energies, diffusion
barriers (CI-NEB), and solute segregation energies were evaluated to quantify the influence of helium
accumulation and alloying elements at the Fe–He interface.
Results
Figure 1 shows two sets of qualitative EDS maps of cavities in F82H-IEA irradiated to 50 and 150 dpa at
500°C with 40/10 appm/dpa of H and He. Each set of images indicates clear evidence of segregation of
chromium and tantalum at cavities throughout the depth dependent damage profile while also showing
segregation of tungsten to a lesser extent. This segregation may be driven by the dissolution of the M23C6
and MX precipitates and changes in grain boundary segregation behavior as discussed in Clowers and
Was [1]. Depletion of Cr at the interface, accompanied by dissolution of carbides in the 500°C condition,
would result in a supersaturation of the constituent Cr, Ta and W metal solutes under irradiation, promoting
segregation at interfaces, including cavities which are among the primary components of sink strength at
these conditions [1].
Hydrogen binding at the Fe–He interface is significantly influenced by substitutional solute atoms. Figure 2
summarizes the hydrogen binding energies as functions of solute species (Cr, Ta, and W), alloy surface
coverage, and He density for the (100) and (110) interfaces, with hydrogen occupying the most stable
adsorption sites. Overall, the degree of enhancement depends on the solute element, surface coverage,
and crystallographic orientation. On the (100) interface, the introduction of a single Ta or W atom increases
hydrogen binding relative to the pure Fe surface. However, as coverage increases, these solutes tend to
act as localized trapping centers rather than continuously strengthening the overall binding. In contrast, Cr
shows little effect at low coverage, with noticeable enhancement only at full monolayer coverage. The
maximum binding energy on (100), 1.311 eV, occurs for 1 ML Ta at the short-bridge site. Increasing He
density generally weakens hydrogen stability; at He/V = 1 the binding energy decreases for all cases,
reflecting the destabilizing effect of an over-pressurized He bubble. In contrast, at He/V = 0.5 the binding
energies remain comparable to those on He-free interfaces. The (110) interface exhibits intrinsically
stronger binding than (100), with further enhancement at higher solute coverage, particularly for Cr and Ta.
To quantify the kinetic stability of hydrogen at the Fe–He interface, climbing-image nudged elastic band (CI-
NEB) calculations were performed to determine the minimum energy pathway for H migration from the
interfacial adsorption site toward the subsurface region along the surface normal. The reaction coordinate
was normalized from 0 (interfacial ground state) to 1, corresponding to the subsurface Fe bulk solution
position. As shown in Figure 3(a,c), substitutional alloying elements (W, Cr, and Ta) at 0.5 ML significantly
increase the hydrogen diffusion barrier. On the (100) surface, Ta produces the largest barrier enhancement,
followed by W and Cr, consistent with the strengthened H–metal interactions that deepen the interfacial
potential well and hinder hydrogen migration. A similar trend is observed on the (110) surface, although the
overall barriers are higher due to the denser atomic packing and stronger geometric confinement. The effect
of helium density is shown by comparing He/V = 0 and He/V = 1 conditions. On the (100) interface, helium
slightly increases the diffusion barrier (~0.1 eV) by modifying the local stress state and migration pathway.
In contrast, the (110) interface shows only minor sensitivity to helium-induced confinement.

 9

 Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

dpa, each at 40/10 appm/dpa of H/He

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

element for (a) Fe(100) and (b) Fe (110) surfaces.

Figure 3. CI-NEB calculated hydrogen diffusion energy profiles. (a) (100) surface at He/V = 0. (b) (100)
interface at He/V = 1. (c) (110) surface at He/V = 0. (d) (110) interface at He/V = 1.

 11

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Future studies should further integrate advanced experimental characterization and atomistic modeling to
establish a quantitative description of hydrogen stability at helium cavity interfaces in RAFM steels. On the
experimental side, a key priority is the quantitative determination of irradiation-induced solute segregation
around helium cavities. While the present EDS observations reveal enrichment of alloying elements such
as Cr, Ta, and W at cavity interfaces, future work should resolve the atomic-scale composition and spatial
distribution of these segregated species. On the computational side, the present DFT calculations capture
static energetics and migration barriers but do not describe the dynamic evolution of H–He–solute
interactions. Future work should incorporate large-scale molecular dynamics or machine-learning
interatomic potential simulations to investigate hydrogen trapping, diffusion, and detrapping during helium
bubble growth under irradiation conditions, thereby linking atomistic mechanisms to experimentally
observed hydrogen retention behavior.

1.3 ANALYSIS OF THE EFFECTS OF QUENCHING AND TEMPERING ON THE MICROTENSILE
BEHAVIOR OF FERRITIC/MARTENSITIC STEEL: QUANTIFICATION OF TENSILE STRENGTH OF
QUENCHED AND TEMPERED F82H STEEL — Peter Hosemann (University of California, Berkeley)

Reduced activation ferritic martensitic (RAFM) steels, particularly F82H, are leading candidates for fusion
reactor structural components due to their favorable combination of high-temperature strength, toughness,
weldability, and low neutron-induced activation. The mechanical behavior of F82H is governed by its ferritic–
martensitic microstructure, which forms during rapid quenching and is characterized by high dislocation
density and supersaturated carbon, resulting in high strength but limited ductility. Subsequent tempering
provides better mechanical properties through carbon diffusion and dislocation recovery, reducing strength
while enhancing ductility and fracture toughness. During tempering, carbon redistribution promotes the
formation and coarsening of carbide precipitates, which play a critical role in stabilizing the microstructure
and influencing strength and creep resistance. Although prior studies have established macroscopic
differences between quenched and tempered states, they often obscure localized deformation mechanisms
inherent to the material’s hierarchical structure. To address this, small-scale mechanical testing (SSMT)
provides a means to probe micron-scale volumes and reveal interactions between dislocations and
microstructural features, albeit with considerations of size-dependent strengthening effects. This study
employs microtensile testing, a SSMT technique, to systematically evaluate the influence of heat treatment
on the mechanical response of F82H, correlating stress–strain behavior with in situ observations and
microstructural characterization to identify the mechanisms governing plasticity.

Methods

Figure 1. Dimensions for micro and mesoscale tensile bars with SEM image of example fabricated samples
Pieces of F82H were sectioned into thin foils and subjected to controlled heat treatments to produce
quenched and tempered martensitic microstructures, with quenching performed from 1040 °C and
tempering at 750 °C under high vacuum to minimize oxidation. The foils were mechanically polished,
followed by fabrication of microtensile specimens using a combination of femtosecond laser machining and
focused ion beam (FIB) milling to achieve precise geometries and surface quality. Microstructural
characterization was conducted via electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD), and a total of 25 microtensile
samples (quenched and tempered) were tested using a picoindenter system under displacement-controlled
loading to obtain stress–strain behavior. To bridge length scales, complementary mesoscale tensile
specimens were fabricated using femtosecond laser machining and tested under comparable strain rates
with digital image correlation to account for compliance effects. This integrated approach enabled direct
comparison of heat treatment–dependent mechanical behavior across micro- and mesoscale regimes with
accurate geometric and microstructural characterization. In the prior research update, samples were
fabricated and tested, however an extensive analysis correlating the mechanical data with microstructural
findings was performed yet. Here, a comprehensive analysis of all the tensile bars is completed and the
main highlights are shown. Publication of the data is planned for early 2026.
Results

 13

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Stress-strain curves measured for all tested microtensile (left) and mesoscale tensile bars (right)
Microtensile testing of single-crystal F82H specimens revealed that plastic deformation is governed by
dislocation glide on {110}<111> and {112}<111> slip systems, with comparable critical resolved shear stress
(CRSS) values for both systems at room temperature. Quenched samples exhibited substantially higher
yield strengths (835–910 MPa) than tempered samples (388–592 MPa), consistent with their suspected
dislocation density, supersaturated carbon, and residual stresses that impede dislocation motion.
Correspondingly, CRSS values decreased upon tempering, reflecting reduced barriers to slip activation due
to dislocation recovery and carbon redistribution. Notably, microscale strength values for tempered F82H
were comparable to bulk measurements, indicating a diminished size effect and suggesting that
conventional strengthening mechanisms, such as grain boundary carbide reinforcement, do not fully explain
the observed behavior.

Figure 3. Comparison of mechanical properties of F82H microtensile bars between samples with single
and multiple crystals within gauge section.
Comparisons between single- and polycrystalline microtensile specimens further demonstrated that grain
boundaries do not provide a net strengthening effect at this scale, as single crystals consistently exhibited
higher strength. Instead, low-angle grain boundaries (LAGBs) acted as preferential sites for failure initiation
in both heat treatment conditions, while tempering promoted more stable plastic flow characterized by
frequent strain bursts associated with dislocation–carbide interactions. High-angle grain boundaries
(HAGBs) displayed contrasting behavior, providing weak points in the quenched state but became
comparatively strengthened after tempering, likely due to carbide precipitation and associated boundary
pinning effects. Across length scales, mesoscale and microscale tensile results showed remarkably
consistent strength values, further indicating that traditional size-dependent strengthening is suppressed in
F82H.
Conclusion
In this study, 25 F82H microtensile specimens were fabricated using a combined femtosecond laser and
focused ion beam approach and tested under uniaxial tension to compare quenched and tempered
conditions. The results show that tempering reduces critical resolved shear stress and strength while
significantly improving ductility, without altering slip system preference. Microscale strength was
comparable to bulk values, indicating suppressed size effects likely due to the fine martensitic lath structure.
Grain boundaries generally reduced strength, with low-angle grain boundaries serving as preferred fracture
initiation sites in both conditions. In contrast, high-angle grain boundaries were weakened in the quenched

 14

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

state but strengthened after tempering, attributed to carbide precipitation and enhanced boundary stability,
highlighting the key role of microstructural evolution in controlling deformation and failure. These findings
highlight the role of microstructure including dislocation density, carbon content, and carbide distribution in
governing deformation mechanisms, slip activation, and failure behavior in RAFM steels at the microscale
and provide an intricate look into RAFM strengthening and deformation mechanisms.

applications, Journal of Nuclear Materials 523 (2019) 510–537.
https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jnucmat.2019.05.058.

on the deformation behavior of a HT-9 alloy using microtensile testing, Materials Characterization 174
(2021) 110972. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.matchar.2021.110972.

Energy 9 (2016) 331–337. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.nme.2016.09.017.

1.4 IN SITU AND EX SITU DISSOLUTION BEHAVIOR OF HEAVY ION IRRADIATED MX-TiC
PRECIPITATES IN CASTABLE NANOSTRUCTURED ALLOYS — Ethan Polselli (University of Michigan),
Hangyu Li (University of Michigan), Ying Yang (ORNL), Kai Sun (University of Michigan), Kevin Field
(University of Michigan)

Castable nanostructured alloys (CNAs) are engineered to have a high density of small (<10-20 nm) and
finely dispersed secondary phases to improve their high-temperature creep strength and radiation
performance. Ex situ heavy-ion irradiation of CNAs has demonstrated that nanometric MX-type TiC
precipitates are susceptible to dissolution at high doses, potentially limiting their ability to support long-term
in-service performance [1,2]. However, ex situ studies provide only a snapshot of radiation behavior. This
work seeks to understand the similarities and variances between existing ex situ ion irradiation data with in
situ heavy-ion irradiations to verify in situ studies can be used to elucidate, with high fidelity, the temperature
and dose effects on precipitate dissolution in CNA9, which contains otherwise abundant, thermally stable
MX-TiC precipitates.

SUMMARY
In situ TEM ion irradiation experiments were performed to determine the dose dependency for MX-TiC
dissolution in laboratory-scale CNA9 under heavy-ion irradiation at 300, 400, and 500°C. In situ irradiation
with Kr3+ at a dose rate of 1×10-3 dpa/s at 300°C showed MX-TiC precipitate stability up to ~50 dpa with a
final precipitate density of 2.36 × 1021 m-3. Conversely, previous ex situ work on CNA9 irradiated to 50 dpa
using Fe3+ with a dose rate of 7×10-4 dpa/s at 300°C demonstrated complete dissolution of MX-TiC [1,2]. At
15 dpa, the difference in density between the two irradiation techniques increased from 300°C up to 500°C
with the in situ samples having a lower precipitate density for all temperatures at 15 dpa. The highest density
of MX-TiC for each method was found at 500 °C with 14.2 and 17.3 × 1021 m-3 for in situ and ex situ irradiated
samples, respectively. The results studied to date present discrepancies between previous ion irradiations
at low doses (≤ 15 dpa) with increasing disagreement at higher doses (> 15 dpa).

In situ ion-beam irradiations were conducted in CNA9, a low-carbon (0.049 ± 0.0049 wt% [3]) laboratory-
scale CNA containing numerous MX-type TiC precipitates. Initially, experiments were expected to run until
complete dissolution of the precipitates was observed. Total dissolution was not observed by ~50 dpa at
300°C, despite this being predicted by single-beam ex situ irradiation. Subsequent in situ irradiations were
conducted to 15 dpa, where precipitates were observed ex situ by Green et al. at 300, 400, and 500°C
using Fe3+ single ion irradiation to a dose rate of 7×10-4 dpa/s [2]. Post-irradiation characterization of in situ-
irradiated samples using STEM-EDS spectrum imaging was used to compare the remaining MX-TiC
carbide distribution following irradiation up to 15 dpa with the corresponding ex situ distribution of the same
irradiation dose and temperatures.

Unirradiated TEM lamellae were prepared at the Michigan Center for Materials Characterization using a
Thermo Fisher Helios 650 Nanolab SEM and FIB system. The lamellae were targeted to be near the [100]
zone axis using an EDAX Hikari EBSD detector and were machined in the shape of 8 by 10 μm windows
with un-thinned pillars supporting the sides and bottom of the window to reduce sample bending. The
thickness of each lamella was measured before irradiation using Energy Filtered TEM (EFTEM). In situ
irradiations were conducted in a Thermo Fisher Tecnai G2 F30 microscope at the Michigan Ion Beam
Laboratory with 1.17 MeV Kr3+ ions to a dose rate of about 1×10-3 dpa/s. Damage rates were calculated
using the “quick” Kinchin-Pease (K-P) calculation method using SRIM-2013. A custom Gatan 652 heating
double tilt holder was used to heat samples during irradiation to temperatures of 300, 400, and 500°C to
compare against previously characterized ex situ irradiated CNA9. Precipitates were observed during
irradiation through a strong darkfield imaging condition near the [100] zone axis with a g = 002 condition in
Figure 1(left) where the Baker-Nutting FCC to BCC orientation relationship between the MX-TiC and the
matrix enabled visible precipitate contrast [4,5]. Figure 1 (right) shows an example of STEM-BF and STEM-
EDS performed in the same region as the dark field in situ video before irradiation. STEM-EDS spectrum
maps constructed with the Ti-kα peak were used to count the number and size of precipitates. Images were

 16

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

taken within the same region of interest before and after irradiation when possible, but a greater area is
imaged and counted using STEM-EDS to improve sampling. The precipitates were sized using an ellipse
fit in ImageJ and equivalent diameters were calculated as the square root of the product of the major and
minor axis of each ellipse.

Figure 1. TEM darkfield image of CNA9 near the [100] zone axis with a g = 002 imaging condition, with a
STEM-EDS Ti spectrum image map overlaid to confirm white features are MX-TiC precipitates. TEM
darkfield with corresponding STEM-EDS Ti chemical map for the pre (left) and post (right).
Results
The quantified results in Figure 2a and Figure 3 show no consistent trend in precipitate size with increasing
irradiation temperature between the in situ and ex situ irradiated samples at 15 dpa. The mean size of MX-
TiC varied after ex situ irradiation but did not change consistently with increasing temperature. Unlike the
ex situ irradiated samples, the mean precipitate size remained very stable under in situ irradiation and did
not deviate significantly from 9.3 nm in diameter across all temperatures. STEM-EDS results on MX-TiC
precipitate density demonstrated a consistent trend of increasing precipitate density with increasing
irradiation temperature in both experiment types, indicated by the precipitate density distribution at 15 dpa
across the three temperatures tested and shown in Figure 3b. The density reported at 300°C shows strong
agreement between the results of the two methods with an in situ measured density of 0.79 ± 0.18 × 1021
m-3, well within error of the measured density of 0.88 ± 0.18 × 1021 from after the ex situ experiment. Across
all temperatures, the density measured in the in situ-irradiated samples remained slightly lower than that of
the corresponding ex situ-irradiated samples at the same temperature. Systematic deviations between the
two methods at 15 dpa could arise from an increased dose rate during in situ irradiation and/or from the
free surface inherent to the TEM lamella specimen, which introduces a disproportionately large surface-
defect sink effect. Both the increased dose rate and the free-surface effect could shift the balance of
precipitate stability toward dissolution by increasing solute ejection from precipitates and decreasing back
diffusion, respectively. Taken together, these changes may produce the variances between the two
irradiation techniques shown in Figure 2b.
The minor variances between the in situ and ex situ irradiation at 15 dpa are in strong contrast with the
results found for the high-dose responses near 50 dpa. For example, at ~50 dpa, 300°C, precipitates were
found at a density of 2.36 × 1021 m-3 after in situ irradiation, while none were found in the previous ex situ
irradiations at the same dose and temperature. To date, it remains unclear what mechanisms drive this
strong change in behavior between the two irradiation environments at elevated doses. Based on these
results, in situ irradiations seem to replicate ex situ irradiations well at low doses and may still provide

 17

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

insight into the mechanics of dissolution. Significant disagreement at higher doses between methods may
limit the utility of in situ experiments for replicating ex situ or bulk-like responses in this dose regime but
may also indicate greater MX-TiC stability than predicted using ex situ methods while enabling detailed
dynamical information.

Figure 2. a) Mean MX-TiC precipitate density measured using STEM-EDS spectrum imaging post
irradiation for in situ and ex situ irradiated samples at 300, 400, and 500°C. b) mean size of precipitates for
each temperature, similarly measured using STEM-EDS, where the number of precipitates counted for
each condition is given as n. Ex situ irradiated results courtesy of Green et al. [2].

Figure 3. Size distribution comparison of MX-TiC precipitates pre- and post-irradiation measured via STEM-
EDS for both in situ and ex situ irradiation methods. Inset lines on each violin plot indicate the mean, first,
and third quartile ranges.

Precipitate stability is observed at higher doses during in situ experimentation than expected from the same
conditions during ex situ irradiation. To better capture the process of dissolution during in situ irradiation,

 18

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

lower-temperature experiments may reduce back diffusion of ejected solute to precipitates, thereby
reducing precipitate stability and allowing dissolution to be captured within reasonable doses for in situ
experiments. Moreover, the dynamic behavior of precipitates captured via TEM darkfield is not addressed
here and requires further analysis. Future testing at higher doses (>50 dpa) could confirm increased stability
of MX-TiC precipitates under in situ irradiation, reduce any potential experimental error, and better
understand the influence of temperature on stability.

[1] T.M.K. Green, T. Gräning, W. Zhong, Y. Yang, K.G. Field, MX precipitate behavior in an irradiated

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

1.5 ALLOY MODIFICATION OF PWHT-FREE REDUCED-ACTIVATION 3Cr BAINITIC-FERRITIC
STEELS — Y. Yamamoto, T. Graening-Seibert (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

This work aims to evaluate the advantages in the mechanical performance of newly proposed, modified
bainitic-ferritic steel based on 3Cr-3WVTa steels developed at ORNL which eliminated the requirement of
post-weld heat treatment (PWHT). There are two major objectives in the present study: one is to identify
the microstructural factors of embrittlement observed in recently developed modified 3Cr-3WVTa steel after
tempering or exposing at the potential service temperatures, and the other is to summarize the property
screenings of newly proposed 5Cr bainitic steels. In addition, the effect of nitrogen addition in the 5Cr
bainitic steels on property improvement is also discussed.

SUMMARY
The source of embrittlement in modified 3Cr-3WVTa steel after tempering or exposing at service
temperatures could not be identified through detailed microstructure characterization. However, the
characterization revealed that there were no obvious microstructural factors that might lead to
embrittlement, such as retained austenite, residual δ-ferrite, compositional segregation at prior austenite
grain boundaries (PAGs), as well as formation of brittle secondary-phase precipitates during thermal
exposure. Because of the results, it was hypothetically considered that relatively high Mn content might
attribute promoting the observed embrittlement. Property screenings of newly proposed 5Cr bainitic steels
(Mod. 5Cr) with minimized Mn content (~0.2 wt.) indicated that tensile strengths and ductility, together with
their temperature dependence, were comparable to those of modified 3Cr-3WVTa steel (Mod. 3Cr),
whereas a reduction of impact toughness after tempering was still significant like that of Mod. 3Cr. It was
found that a nitrogen addition to Mod. 5Cr increased the impact toughness after tempering.

Table 1 summarizes the nominal compositions of the materials evaluated in the present study. Mod. 3Cr
steel was proposed in the earlier stage of the alloy development which targeted eliminating the requirement
of PWHT process via three combined property characteristics; (1) minimized hardness variation across the
weldment, (2) sufficiently high hardenability comparable to the original steel, and (3) high impact toughness
in the as-welded (or as-normalized) condition. All of them were achieved by compositional modification with
increased Mn and decreased C applied to the original 3Cr-3WVTa steel. However, Mod. 3Cr suffered from
significantly low impact toughness after tempering or exposing at the potential service temperatures
(~500°C). Because of the issue, two research activities have been brought up; one is to identify the
mechanism of why such embrittlement would occur, and the other is to propose new alloy design to maintain
the PWHT-free design while avoiding the reduction of impact toughness after tempering or exposing at the
service temperatures.
Table 1. Nominal compositions of bainitic steels studied.
 Nominal composition, wt.% (balanced Fe)
ID Remarks
 Cr Mn Si W V Ta C Ni N
Original 3Cr-
              3     0.4    0.16 3       0.2    0.1    0.10 -       -      Proposed in 1990’s [1]
3WVTa
Mod. 3Cr     3      2      0.16 3       0.2    0.1    0.03 -       -      Modified 3Cr steel
Mod. 5Cr     5      0.2    0.16 3       0.2    0.1    0.03 1       -      5Cr
5Cr + N      5      0.2    0.16 3       0.2    0.1    0.03 1       0.02 5Cr + 0.02N

Detailed microstructure characterization of Mod. 3Cr in the as-normalized condition and after isothermal
aging at 500°C for up to 10,000h was conducted using SEM-EBSD and -EDS analyses. Figure 1 shows a
summary of the EBSD analysis of the as-normalized Mod. 3Cr, representing fully bainitic-ferritic

 20

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

microstructure, with no retained austenite or δ (or even α) -ferrite formation. The EDS analysis also revealed
no significant elemental segregation at the PAGs or brittle secondary-phase precipitation in both as-
normalized and aged samples, indicating that the characterization with SEM could not detect any source
of embrittlement in Mod. 3Cr after normalizing or exposing at service temperatures. Therefore, it was
hypothetically considered that relatively high Mn content in Mod. 3Cr contributed to promoting the observed
embrittlement, aka temper embrittlement.

Figure 1. SEM-EBSD color maps of Mod. 3Cr steel in the as-normalized condition: (a) inverse pole figure
color map, (b) kernel average misorientation map, and (c) phase identification overlapped with image quality
map.
The Mn addition plays an important role of stabilizing austenite phase at elevated temperature and
maintaining high hardenability, so that new alloy design has been proposed replacing Mn to the other
elements for stabilization of austenite phase. Since the Cr addition up to 7 wt.% is known to increase the
stability of austenite phase and retard the ferrite transformation during continuous cooling process, the
addition of 5 wt.% of Cr was selected with expectation of improved hardenability even without the Mn
addition. The reduced C addition (0.03 wt.%) combining with a limited amount of the Ni addition (1.0 wt.%)
were also applied at the same time for balancing low hardness and high hardenability. Mod. 5Cr with newly
proposed alloying additions successfully achieved fully bainitic microstructure with relatively low hardness,
as reported previously.
The tensile properties of Mod. 5Cr were evaluated, as summarized in Figure 2. The tensile strengths and
ductility, together with their temperature dependence, were comparable to those of Mod. 3Cr. This result
suggests that newly proposed compositional modification would not negatively impact on forming fully
bainitic microstructure because of maintaining high hardenability as expected from the design strategy,
which successfully preserve the high-temperature strength as well as decent ductility like those observed
in Mod. 3Cr.
The Charpy impact toughness (absorbed energy) and micro-Vickers hardness at room temperature before
and after tempering are shown in Figure 3. As described earlier, Mod. 3Cr showed relatively low hardness
and small reduction of the hardness after tempering, which was good, while the absorbed energy
significantly dropped after tempering. Mod. 5Cr also showed a similar trend in the hardness before and
after tempering, which was also good. The absorbed energy in the as-normalized condition was decently
high compared to the original steel, while it dropped again after tempering like Mod. 3Cr, indicating that the
elimination of Mn did not fully resolve the embrittlement issue after tempering.

 21

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Tensile properties of the Mn-free 5Cr bainitic-ferritic steel (Mod. 5Cr) in as-normalized condition,
compared with the original and Mod. 2Cr steels: (a) yield strength, and (b) total plastic elongation.
During evaluation of the other combinations of alloying elements, it was found that the nitrogen addition to
Mod. 5Cr (called “5Cr + N”) improved the impact toughness significantly. With the addition of nominally 0.02
wt.% nitrogen, the absorbed energy in the as-normalized condition did not change from Mod. 5Cr, while the
absorbed energy after tempering increased drastically, as shown in Figure 3a. Unfortunately, the as-
normalized hardness was relatively high compared to the others, and it dropped significantly after
tempering, as shown in Figure 3b, so that the alloy composition would not be a good candidate to be
downselected. However, the improvement of the impact toughness in both as-normalized and tempered
conditions are highly attractive. The mechanism is unknown, so that the detailed characterization as well
as the compositional optimization to improve the hardness characteristics is currently in progress.

Figure 3. Comparison of Charpy impact toughness (a) and micro-Vickers hardness (b) in Mod. 3Cr, 5Cr,
and 5Cr +N steels before and after tempering.

Property evaluation of modified 5Cr steels with nitrogen additions, together with microstructure
characterization, will be performed to comprehensively investigate the effect of minor alloying additions on
improving the properties satisfying PWHT-free design.

[1] R.L. Klueh et al., Int. J. Pressure Vessels and Piping, 84 (2007) 29-36.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

1.6 IRRADIATION EFFECT ON THE STABILITY OF MX PRECIPITATE IN FERRITIC MODEL ALLOYS—
Weicheng Zhong, Ying Yang, Josina (Wilna) Geringer, TS Byun (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Castable Nanostructured Alloys (CNAs) were designed to have high number density of nanoscale MX types
of precipitates, which provide the high temperature mechanical strengthening and irradiation sink strength.
The goal of this work is to understand the microstructural evolution in CNAs under neutron irradiation,
including the stability of MX precipitates.

SUMMARY
Neutron irradiation induces significant evolution in the morphology, distribution, and composition of TiC
precipitates in CNA4 and CNA5 alloys. In the as-received condition, both alloys contain spherical TiC and
elongated Cr-rich M23C6 precipitates. These precipitates remain largely unchanged after irradiation to ~6
dpa. At higher dose (~20 dpa), TiC precipitates undergo pronounced changes, particularly in CNA4, where
they become elongated and preferentially located at boundaries. Compositional analysis reveals that
spherical TiC contains Ti, W, Ta, and V, whereas elongated TiC is primarily Ti with minor V and reduced W.

CNA4 and CNA5 were irradiated in High Flux Isotope Reactor (HFIR) to ~6 dpa and ~20 dpa at the
temperature of ~500-560°C. M23C6 and TiC precipitates are present in both CNAs, and their stability under
neutron irradiation were investigated using transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and energy dispersive
spectroscopy (EDS).
Results
Figure 1 shows the EDS Cr and Ti maps of the as-received samples and those subjected to neutron
irradiation to ~6 dpa and ~20 dpa for both CNA4 and CNA5. In these maps, the blue precipitates correspond
to Cr-rich M23C6 precipitates distributed along boundaries, while the yellow particles represent TiC
precipitates.
In the as-received condition, both alloys exhibit spherical TiC in the matrix and elongated M23C6 precipitates
at the boundary. In addition, very fine TiC precipitates appear to be distributed along lath boundaries. After
neutron irradiation to 6 dpa, both spherical TiC and M23C6 precipitates remain present in CNA4 and CNA5,
and their distribution is largely unchanged compared to the as-received condition.
After irradiation to 20 dpa, notable changes in the TiC precipitates are observed. In CNA5, most of the
coarse TiC precipitates remain spherical and are distributed within the matrix, although one particle exhibits
an elongated morphology at a boundary. This effect is more pronounced in CNA4, where most of the TiC
precipitates are located at boundaries and display an elongated morphology.
In addition to differences in morphology and distribution between spherical and elongated TiC precipitates
at 20 dpa, further characterization of their composition reveals distinct differences. Figure 2 shows EDS
intensity line profiles of the metallic elements associated with the TiC precipitates. The spherical TiC
precipitates contain Ti, W, Ta, and V. In contrast, the elongated TiC precipitates at boundaries consist
primarily of Ti with minor V, while the W content appears lower than that in the surrounding matrix.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. EDS intensity line profiles of the metallic elements associated with the spherical and elongated
TiC precipitates in CNA5 after neutron irradiation to 20 dpa.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Future work will focus on characterizing irradiation-induced defects and correlating them with the resulting
mechanical properties.

1.7 PNNL HARDNESS TESTING OF Ni-CLADDED STEEL SAMPLES — Peter Hosemann (University of
California, Berkeley)

Six irradiated steel specimens, each featuring a nickel cladding layer along one face, were received from
Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL) for nanoindentation hardness characterization. The objective
of the study was to establish hardness profiles across the bulk steel/nickel interface by conducting spatially
resolved indentation arrays across both constituent regions.

Indentation testing was performed using a Berkovich tip geometry under displacement-controlled
conditions, targeting a maximum depth of 250 nm at a constant loading rate of 12.5 nm/s. Indents were
spaced 4 µm apart. All measurements were conducted quasi-statically; neither continuous stiffness
measurement (CSM) nor nanoDMA techniques were used.
To date, five of the six received specimens have been evaluated. Testing of each specimen has revealed a
consistent geometric impediment that precludes valid data collection on the samples in their current as-
received condition. Specifically, the material edge — the intended region of interest for interface
characterization — exhibits appreciable rounding, most likely introduced during the polishing stage of
sample preparation. This curvature prevents the Berkovich tip from making proper, well-defined contact
with the specimen surface in the near-edge region, rendering hardness measurements at and across the
interface unreliable. It is highly likely the sixth (albeit untested) sample would show the same geometric
issues as the other five.

Figure 1. Testing of sample ZS52 (UCB RSIS#72898).

 26

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Closeup of interface in sample ZS52 (UCB RSIS#72898)
Compounding this geometric limitation, it has been difficult to confidently identify the true specimen edge
under optical microscopy of our Bruker TI990 nanoindentation system. Edge rounding, combined with
optical artifacts such as reflections, saturation of the optics, and diffraction effects inherent to imaging near
a material boundary, makes precise positioning of indents relative to the interface ambiguous. Further
obscuring the edge is the translucent mounting adhesive — either Crystalbond or a comparable epoxy —
used to affix the specimens to their stainless steel puck holders. The change in contrast between the
adhesive and the specimen material at the edge makes it difficult to distinguish where the sample terminates
and the adhesive begins. When optical microscopy is required, it’s often helpful to dope the adhesive with
graphite powder, or something else that makes the adhesive opaque.

Figure 3. Sample R56 (UCB RSIS#72897) at cladding edge after testing.
In summary, reliable nanoindentation characterization across the bulk steel/nickel interface requires that
each specimen have a well-defined, flat cross-sectional surface. It is recommended that the samples
undergo re-preparation — specifically, re-polishing — with particular attention to preserving edge geometry
and minimizing rounding artifacts prior to any further testing.

 27

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

1.8 ANALYSIS OF THE EFFECTS OF QUENCHING AND TEMPERING ON THE ENERGY DISSIPATION
OF FERRITIC/MARTENSITIC STEEL QUANTIFICATION OF TENSILE STRENGTH OF QUENCHED
AND TEMPERED F82H STEEL — Peter Hosemann (University of California, Berkeley)

F82H is a key structural material being developed for fusion applications, including the ITER breeding
blanket, where understanding its mechanical response under reactor-relevant conditions is critical. Its
tempered martensitic microstructure forms through rapid quenching followed by tempering, producing a
hierarchical structure of laths, blocks, packets, and prior austenite grains (PAGs), with tempering promoting
carbide precipitation, dislocation annealing, and lattice relaxation, thereby increasing ductility and
toughness while reducing strength. Small-scale mechanical testing (SSMT) offers a means to probe these
features at micron scales, enabling insight into localized defect interactions that are difficult to resolve in
bulk samples, particularly in nuclear materials with handling constraints. Building on foundational work in
internal friction, micromechanical spectroscopy (μMS) allows nondestructive measurement of resonance
and damping in FIB-fabricated microcantilevers, providing quantitative assessment of defect-mediated
energy dissipation. In this study, μMS was applied to eight quenched and tempered F82H microcantilevers
with varying grain structures to gain an intricate look into the interactions of dislocations, grain boundaries,
and carbide precipitates on mechanical damping and defect behavior, offering a detailed perspective on
the microstructural mechanisms governing deformation in RAFM steels.

SUMMARY
A quenched F82H specimen was polished and used to fabricate ~3×3×15 μm microcantilevers via
femtosecond laser milling followed by focused ion beam (FIB) cleaning. Grain orientations were
characterized using electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) prior to testing. Micromechanical spectroscopy
(μMS) was performed on the cantilevers in a Hysitron PI-88 picoindenter with nanoDMA capabilities,
applying small elastic forces via a diamond wedge tip while sweeping frequencies from 200–300 Hz to
capture resonance peaks and dynamic compliance at multiple points along each cantilever. Following the
initial measurements, the cantilevers were tempered at 750 °C for 1 h under vacuum, and μMS testing was
repeated to assess changes in resonance behavior due to tempering, providing a nondestructive,
quantitative evaluation of defect-mediated mechanical damping and elastic response in F82H.

Figure 1. All numbered cantilevers with EBSD IPF-Z map overlaid

PROGRESS AND STATUS ______
 5μm

 28

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Calculated elastic modulus and Q-1 for each tested cantilever
μMS testing of eight F82H cantilevers in both quenched and tempered states revealed that damping
increased following tempering, with average Q⁻¹ rising from 3.79×10⁻³ to 7.60×10⁻³. Analysis of damping
mechanisms suggests this increase arises primarily from precipitate-dislocation interactions, including
Granato-Lücke pinning and Schöek-type precipitate interface relaxation, rather than interstitial carbon
(Snoek) or grain boundary effects. Orientation of grains relative to the applied stress influenced damping
through dislocation motion, while cantilevers with higher densities of HAGBs and precipitated carbides
showed smaller damping increases, indicating that carbides at grain boundaries constrain dislocation
activity. Overall, it is theorized that tempering redistributed carbon into carbides, modifying defect
interactions and enhancing energy dissipation in the microstructure.

Figure 3. Normalized compliance vs normalized frequency measured from the μMS experiment on
Cantilever 4 showing measured resonance peak. Analyzed trendline shown with raw data points.
Elastic modulus measurements showed an increase from 146.4 GPa (quenched) to 155.8 GPa (tempered),
still below the bulk F82H value of 218.8 GPa, primarily due to crystallographic anisotropy in BCC martensite.
Using Voigt-Reuss bounds derived from single-crystal elastic constants, most cantilever measurements fell
within theoretical predictions. The modulus increase after tempering is attributed to reduced dislocation
density, lower interstitial carbon content, decreased internal stresses, and the slightly higher stiffness of
ferrite compared to martensite. Larger-grained cantilevers showed the greatest modulus gains, while those
with finer grains and more HAGBs exhibited smaller improvements, likely due to the influence of carbide
precipitates at grain boundaries increasing incoherent interface area.

Figure 4. Theoretical values for elastic modulus using Voigt and Reuss bounds compared with measured
elastic modulus
Eight F82H micro-cantilevers were tested using the novel μMS technique to probe carbide evolution and
its effect on mechanical behavior between quenched and tempered martensite. Using picoindenter
measurements of the cantilevers’ resonance peaks, quantitative values for damping and elastic modulus

 29

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

were extracted. Damping increased by a factor of ~2 following tempering, primarily due to carbide
nucleation strongly pinning dislocation lines, while grain orientation played a key role, with grains having
close-packed planes aligned with the applied stress showing the largest damping increases. The elastic
modulus rose by 6.4% after tempering, with larger-grained cantilevers showing the greatest gains, while
fine grains with high-angle grain boundaries (HAGBs) exhibited smaller increases, likely due to carbide
precipitation at boundaries constraining modulus improvement. These findings complement microtensile
studies showing that tempering reduces the critical resolved shear stress (CRSS) and enhances ductility,
while carbides at HAGBs stabilize grain boundaries, mitigate sudden fracture, and influence strain burst
behavior, collectively linking microstructural carbide evolution to both energy dissipation and mechanical
performance in F82H.

applications, Journal of Nuclear Materials 523 (2019) 510–537.

on the deformation behavior of a HT-9 alloy using microtensile testing, Materials Characterization 174

 2. ODS AND NANOCOMPOSITED ALLOY DEVELOPMENT

 31

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

2.1 EVALUATE THERMOMECHANICAL TREATMENT STRATEGY OF CNAs TO BALANCE THE HIGH
TEMPERATURE STRENGTH AND ROOM TEMPERATURE CHARPY ENERGY — Ying Yang, Weicheng
Zhong, Jack Carpenter, Xiang Chen and Yanli Wang (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Evaluate thermomechanical treatment strategies for castable nanostructured alloys (CNAs) to improve the
balance between room-temperature toughness and high-temperature strength, with emphasis on
identifying processing windows that reduce ductile-to-brittle transition behavior while retaining the hardness
and creep capability required for fusion structural applications.

SUMMARY
This reporting period focused on thermomechanical treatment development for CNA plates. The current
CNA products, including both rod (CNAR) and plate (CNAP), show attractive creep strength relative to
Eurofer97, but this advantage is accompanied by higher ductile-to-brittle transition temperature and
reduced room-temperature Charpy energy. To address this tradeoff, a matrix of rolling, reheating, and
tempering conditions was applied to CNA plate material. The results show a strong inverse relationship
between hardness and Charpy energy, confirming that toughness recovery requires careful control of
deformation temperature and tempering schedule. Among the conditions examined, lower-temperature
rolling followed by reheating and tempering at 750°C/1 h provided the best overall toughness response,
with TMT10 reaching 70.72 J and 100% shear while reducing hardness to 200.8 HV10. These results define
a practical processing window for improving CNA toughness while preserving the strength basis needed for
fusion-relevant service.

The major effort during this period was to assess how thermomechanical treatment can tune the strength-
toughness balance of CNA plates. Figure 1 summarizes the motivation for this work: compared with
Eurofer97, CNAR and CNAP exhibit improved creep performance, but their impact properties indicate a
higher DBTT. Because nanoprecipitate-strengthened alloys derive their strength from a complex
microstructure that is highly sensitive to processing history, optimization of hot working and tempering is
essential for future scale-up and qualification. The present study therefore focused on establishing
processing conditions that recover room-temperature Charpy energy without fully sacrificing the hardness
as an indicator for high-temperature strength.

Figure 1. CNAR and CNAP show improved creep performance but higher DBTT than Eurofer97.

CNA plate material was first normalized at 1180°C for 30 min and then subjected to selected rolling,
reheating, and tempering treatments. The processing matrix included no rolling, hot rolling at 1180°C or
1050°C with 30% or 60% reduction, and warm rolling at 700°C with 30% reduction. Reheating was
performed either at the rolling temperature or at 1050°C, followed by tempering at 710°C for 1 h or 4 h, or
at 750°C for 1 h. Room-temperature Charpy impact energy, percent shear fracture, HV10 hardness, and

 32

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

prior-austenite grain size were used to compare conditions. This dataset was designed to identify how
deformation temperature, strain level, and tempering schedule influence the tradeoff between toughness
recovery and strength retention.
Results
The TMT results show a clear processing-property trend. In the reference condition without rolling, Charpy
energy remained very low (1.57-3.43 J) and hardness stayed relatively high (235-272 HV10). Rolling at
1180°C produced only limited improvement, with Charpy values below about 17 J. A much larger increase
in impact energy was obtained when rolling temperature was reduced to 1050°C or 700°C and the material
was subsequently reheated and tempered. For example, TMT8 reached 40.04 J at 217.4 HV10, TMT9+3h
reached 49 J at 205 HV10, and TMT10 achieved the highest toughness at 70.72 J with 100% shear and
200.8 HV10. These results indicate that lower-temperature deformation combined with a sufficiently soft
temper is effective for reducing brittleness in CNA plate.
The grain-size data further suggest that thermomechanical processing contributes to toughness recovery
through refinement of the transformed microstructure. Conditions processed at 1050°C or below reduced
grain size substantially relative to the reference state. However, grain refinement alone does not explain
the full trend, because hardness remains a strong indicator of the resulting Charpy response. As shown in
Figure 2, higher hardness is generally associated with lower impact energy, consistent with the expectation
that the microstructural state providing maximum precipitation and matrix strengthening also increases the
resistance to stable crack initiation and propagation at room temperature. The data therefore highlights the
need to optimize nanoprecipitate stability, lath structure, and carbide distribution together rather than
pursuing hardness alone.

                                             /30min            30%                 /1h

Figure 2. The TMT results show that hardness is inversely proportional to Charpy energy.

Next work will focus on evaluating short term creep-rupture results and 600 and 650°C and identifying the
effect of TMT on creep performance. The correlation between the mechanical-property trends with detailed
microstructural characterization, including prior-austenite grain structure, lath morphology, and precipitate
evolution after thermomechanical treatment will be also studied. Additional optimization is needed to identify
a processing window that preserves more high-temperature strength while maintaining acceptable room-
temperature toughness. The most promising conditions from this screening study will be used to guide

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

follow-on heat-treatment development, plate scale-up, and future qualification-oriented property evaluation
for fusion structural components.

2.2 EFFECT OF HEAT TREATMENT ON FRACTURE TOUGHNESS PROPERTIES OF THE CNA STEEL
— Xiang (Frank) Chen, Weicheng Zhong, Qingqiang Ren, Ying Yang, Mikhail A Sokolov, Tim Graening,
Yutai Katoh (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

The work aims to evaluate the effect of different heat treatments on the fracture toughness properties of
the Castable Nanostructured Alloy (CNA) steel rationalized by the grain structure after heat treatments.

SUMMARY
CNA is being developed as the new reference reduced activation ferritic-martensitic (RAFM) steel in the
U.S. It aims for higher creep strength and radiation resistance than existing RAFM steels, such as F82H
and EUROFER97, by formation of a higher volume fraction and number density of MX nanoprecipitates in
the material. In this work, we studied a range of heat treatments including conventional normalization-
tempering (NT) heat treatments and cyclic heat treatments as well as their effect on the fracture toughness
of a CNA rod material. We discovered that the conventional NT heat treatments faced challenge to meet
both requirements for Vickers hardness (HV: 235-250) and Charpy ductile-to-brittle transition temperature
(DBTT) (≤ -30°C) due to coarse grain structure formed after the heat treatment. In contrast, adding
intermediate cyclic heat treatment between normalization and tempering heat treatment significantly refined
grain size resulting in good combination of Vickers hardness and Charpy properties.

During fusion plant operation, neutron irradiation and transmutation helium cause embrittlement and reduce
the fracture toughness of RAFM steels. It is critical for RAFM steels to possess superior fracture toughness
for fusion reactor applications. This study aims for evaluation of the effect of heat treatment on fracture
toughness properties of CNA steel and optimizing the heat treatment to find a good balance between
hardness/strength and fracture toughness.

Ф25.4 mm (1”) CNA rod material with its composition shown in Table 1 was used in this study. Initially, five
different NT heat treatments (HT7, HT9 thru HT12) highlighted in Table 2 were applied to the material. In
addition, one additional heat treatment called HT-cyclic, which introduced one intermediate cyclic heat
treatment between normalization and tempering heat treatment, was evaluated. After heat treatment,
Charpy impact and Vickers hardness testing were performed on the material.

Results
Table 3 summarizes the Charpy impact results of the CNA rod after NT heat treatments with comparison of
literature data on F82H, EUROFER97, Grade 91 steels. In general, CNA demonstrated higher DBTT and
upper shelf energy (USE) than other 9Cr-type FM steels. In addition, within the evaluated NT heat treatment
conditions, lower normalization temperature, shorter normalization time, higher tempering temperature, and
longer tempering time would result in lower DBTT and higher USE. However, none of the evaluated NT
heat treatments would meet both design requirements for CNA, i.e., HV: 235-250 and Charpy DBTT ≤ -
30°C as summarized in Table 4. Further evaluation of selected specimens revealed coarse prior austenite
grain size as shown in Figure 1 and it is known that such coarse grain size was responsible for higher
Master Curve reference temperature in laboratory heats of EURPFER97 [5]. Based on an earlier work in
[6], one intermediate cyclic heat treatment was added between normalization and tempering heat treatment.
The following testing as shown in Figure 2 showed promising combination of the room temperature (RT)
Charpy impact energy from half-size Charpy and Vickers hardness of the CNA material. Additional testing
will be performed to determine the improvement in Charpy DBTT after the new heat treatment condition.

2.3 IN-SITU ALLOYING OF 0.3 WT.% Y2O3 WITH GAS-ATOMIZED 14WT POWDER — Shubhrodev
Bhowmik, Nilesh Kumar (Department of Metallurgical and Materials Engineering, The University of
Alabama), Osman El Atwani, Stuart A. Maloy (Pacific Northwest National Lab), Pankaj Kulkarni, and Kumar
Kandasamy (Enabled Engineering)

The work in this report was a part of a project funded by DOE-SBIR Phase II. One of the objectives of the
project is to manufacture oxide dispersion strengthened (ODS) 14YWT fuel cladding tubes in a fast, cost-
effective, and environmentally friendly manner. This report summarizes the results of the study evaluating
the effectiveness of Friction Stir Consolidation (FSC) in in-situ synthesis of ODS 14YWT alloy. This
knowledge will further be utilized in directly manufacturing ODS tubes without involving ball milling process
using SolidStir® Extrusion. SolidStir® Extrusion is a shear deformation-based consolidation and extrusion
method without needing external heating. To explore the feasibility of in-situ alloying with gas-atomized
(GA) powder and Y2O3 to generate 14YWT alloy, FSC successfully synthesized ODS 14YWT alloy with
desired microstructure and hardness values, suggesting SolidStir® Extrusion can utilize a mixture of GA
14WT and Y2O3 powders to extrude fuel cladding tubes.

SUMMARY
Conventionally, the production of feedstock powder for 14YWT fuel cladding tubes involves ball milling gas-
atomized (GA) particles with the target composition of Fe-14Cr-3W-0.4Ti (wt%) and 0.3 wt% Y₂O₃ for
approximately 40 hours. This prolonged mechanical alloying step ensures uniform elemental distribution
and alloying. A subsequent multistep extrusion process is typically required to achieve a uniform dispersion
of nano-oxides. As an alternative, friction stir-based techniques, such as SolidStir® Extrusion, offer the
potential to integrate in-situ alloying of GA powders with Y₂O₃ and tube extrusion, thereby eliminating the
need for ball milling and multistep extrusion. Among these, friction stir consolidation (FSC) presents a rapid
and efficient route for parameter optimization, utilizing Inconel 625 dies to consolidate powder within
machined dies. In this study, 0.3 wt% Y₂O₃ (30–50 nm) was in-situ alloyed during FSC using GA 14WT
powder to assess the feasibility of bypassing the conventional ball milling stage. The resulting
macrostructure, EBSD-based grain size analysis, and microindentation hardness measurements are
presented to evaluate the effectiveness of FSC-based in-situ alloying in streamlining the feedstock
preparation process. The microhardness results were compared with SolidStir® extruded tube using ball
milled powder in as processed and heat-treated condition.

UPDATE AND STATUS
For FSC, the feed powder was prepared by gas atomization (GA) using the gas atomizer at the University
of Alabama (UA) with a nominal composition of Fe-14Cr-0.4Ti-3W (in wt%). Fe, Cr, and Ti were procured
from Atlantic Equipment Engineers in New Jersey (with 99.9% purity), while the W rod was purchased from
McMaster Carr with 99% purity. The W rod was then cut by Wire EDM in appropriate dimensions to maintain
the nominal composition. For this study, the gas atomized powder in the size range of 20-200 µm was
chosen. Yttria (Y2O3) powder with a size of 30-50 nm (from SSnano, with 99.99% purity) was used. The
14WT GA and Y2O3 powders were mixed with a LabRAM II from ResodynTM Acoustic Mixer using the
following parameter: 30 seconds 60g, 30 minutes 70g, 10-minute rest, 30 seconds 50g, 30 seconds 60g,
30 minutes 70g to ensure proper mixing (denoted as L and LR in the later sections).
To process the feed powder (a mixture of 14WT GA powder pre-mixed with 30-50 nm sized Y2O3), several
dies were machined by drilling blind holes (diameter ~9.57 mm and depth ~5.60 mm) into an Inconel 625
plate (Fig. 1(b)). The die was clamped onto the anvil of the FSW machine and filled with powder. To achieve
a defect-free consolidation of the powder, the processing parameters chosen were as follows: tool rotational
rate: 400 rpm; forge velocity: 1 mm/min for 14YWT powder and 0.5 mm/min for 14WT powder; dwell time:
10 s, and plunge depth (PD): ~75% of the die depth. The side view shows the W-Re tool used in the
processing of 14YWT and 14WT powders (Fig. 1(a)). Ar gas was used to prevent the oxidation of the alloy
during consolidation. After consolidation, the samples were cut by wire EDM, and the characterization was
performed on the longitudinal cross-section.

 40

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Heat treatment of the extruded tube and consolidated powder was performed in a benchtop muffle furnace
from Thermo Scientific (Type 48000) at 1000 °C for 15 minutes to investigate its effect on the precipitation
of Y-Ti-O-based precipitates.
The Vickers micro-indentation hardness test with an Alpha MHT-2000Z hardness tester from Pace
Technologies was performed on powders and consolidated 14YWT in both as-processed and heat-treated
conditions to observe the mechanical property in terms of hardness. A force of 100 gf (HV 0.1) with a dwell
time of 10 s was used during the hardness measurement.

Figure 1. (a) Tool; (b) die (hole) machine into Inconel 625 plate for friction stir consolidation.
Fig. 2(a) presents the FSCed region for 14WT + 0.3 wt.% Y₂O₃ (30-50 nm) consolidated at 400 rpm.
Longitudinal cross-sectional image, captured using a Keyence microscope, is also shown. The results
indicate minimal back extrusion and no visible surface defects. However, an unbonded region is observed
at the bottom of the die. EBSD analysis was conducted at various positions in the consolidated region. The
EBSD result included in Fig. 2(c) corresponds to a region approximately 500 μm from the surface (indicated
by the circular dot in Fig. 2(b)).
The EBSD results for the consolidated powder sample are shown in Figs. 2(c) and 2(d). The IPF map (Fig.
2(c)) display multiple colors, indicating no preferred crystallographic orientation. The grain size follows a
lognormal distribution, with an average size of 1.52±0.6 μm. From the cumulative distribution, approximately
26.4% of grains fall within the ultrafine grain (UFG) region. Additional parameters, including LAGB fraction
and average KAM value, were computed from the EBSD results and are summarized in Table 1. It can be
observed that the fraction of LAGB is 34.1%, and the average KAM value is 1.63°.
Microindentation hardness measurements on the longitudinal cross-section, shown in Fig. 3, indicate that
the sample consolidated at 400 rpm in AP condition by in-situ alloying exhibits higher hardness compared
to tube extruded by SolidStir® extrusion technology at 500 rpm with 40-hour ball-milled powder. After HT,
the hardness of the sample processed at 400 rpm further increased. Additionally, the hardness of FSCed
14YWT powders was compared with FSCed 14WT (without Y2O3). The presence of Y2O3 in FSCed 14YWT
results in better hardness.

CONCLUSIONS
Based on the results presented, it can be concluded that in-situ alloying offers a promising alternative for
processing 14YWT powder, indicating the direct fabrication of fuel cladding tubes with comparable
microstructure and better microhardness. It also indicates that the shear-based consolidation and tube
extrusion method may not require ball-milled powder as feedstock. This approach has the potential to
significantly reduce the production time associated with traditional feedstock preparation, eliminating the
need for prolonged mechanical alloying via 40-hour ball milling employed in conventional methods.
