Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

2.4 FABRICATION OF NANOSTRUCTURED Fe-9Cr STEELS WITH OXIDE–CARBIDE HYBRID
STRENGTHENING — T.S. Byun, Y. Lin (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

The objective of this work is to design, fabricate, and characterize novel nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys
incorporating oxide–carbide hybrid strengthening, enabling sustained strengthening and effective defect-
sink functionality over a broad temperature range, thereby extending the high-temperature operating
window for fusion energy systems.

SUMMARY
This research aims to develop advanced fusion structural materials that integrate the complementary
advantages of carbide-strengthened reduced-activation ferritic–martensitic (RAFM) steels and oxide
dispersion-strengthened (ODS) ferritic alloys. Both alloy classes exhibit temperature-dependent
degradation mechanisms that limit their individual applicability under fusion conditions. To address this
limitation, a nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloy concept incorporating oxide–carbide hybrid nanoparticle
dispersions is investigated. This approach leverages the distinct thermodynamic and irradiation stability
regimes of oxides and carbides, enabling complementary strengthening and defect-sink functionality and
thereby promoting sustained microstructural stability across a broader range of fusion-relevant
temperatures. Two Fe–9Cr-based alloys with controlled oxygen and carbon additions were fabricated via
consolidation followed by tailored multistep thermomechanical processing to achieve refined grain
structures and controlled precipitation of oxide and carbide nanoparticles. The resulting oxide–carbide
hybrid-strengthened steels, exhibiting varied nanostructures, were systematically characterized to enable
performance comparison and down-selection.

This research developed and applied streamlined powder metallurgy (PM) and thermomechanical
processing (TMP) routes to fabricate nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys with oxide–carbide hybrid
strengthening. The target composition, Fe–9Cr–2W–0.4Ti–0.2V (in wt.%), was designed with controlled low
(0.05 wt.%) and high (0.11 wt.%) carbon levels to promote carbide formation while maintaining compatibility
with oxide dispersion. Two alloy powders were produced via vacuum induction melting followed by argon
gas atomization, ensuring tight control of impurity elements and particle size distribution.
Mechanical alloying was performed by blending the powders with 0.3 wt.% Y2O3 powder to introduce a
uniform oxide dispersion. The milled powders were then consolidated by hot extrusion after heating to 850
°C, producing fully dense bulk materials. Subsequent TMP routes were designed to refine the
microstructure and control nanoparticle precipitation. These treatments involved repeated hot-rolling cycles
at 900 °C with intermediate annealing, introducing total strains of 50% or 120%. The processes used the
deformation and associated dislocation networks to provide effective nucleation sites for both oxide and
carbide particles, promoting their uniform and fine distribution. To further tailor the microstructure, a subset
of samples underwent tempering at 750 °C for 2 hours, a condition known to enhance carbide precipitation
in ferritic–martensitic steels while maintaining the stability of oxide particles. In total, eight processing
conditions (two compositions × four TMP routes) were evaluated, as summarized in Table 1. This integrated
PM–TMP approach enabled the controlled development and mass-production option of nanograined
microstructures with hybrid oxide–carbide dispersions, providing a new platform to investigate the
synergistic strengthening and defect-sink behavior under fusion-relevant conditions.
Table 1. Thermomechanical treatment matrix for the two alloys after mechanical alloying and extrusion
 Material ID Processing ID TMT-A: Hot-rolling TMT-B: Tempering
 9YWTV-H1 PM1-HR Repeated (4 times) hot rolling after 900 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 120% total strain
 PM1-HRT Repeated (4 times) hot rolling after 900 °C Annealing at 750 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 120% total strain for 2 hours

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 PM1-LR Repeated (2 times) hot rolling after 900 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 50% total strain
 PM1-LRT Repeated (2 times) hot rolling after 900 °C Annealing at 750 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 50% total strain for 2 hours
 9YWTV-H2 PM2-HR Repeated (4 times) hot rolling after 900 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 120% total strain
 PM2-HRT Repeated (4 times) hot rolling after 900 °C Annealing at 750 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 120% total strain for 2 hours
 PM2-LR Repeated (2 times) hot rolling after 900 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 50% total strain
 PM2-LRT Repeated (2 times) hot rolling after 900 °C Annealing at 750 °C
 annealing for 30 minutes for 50% total strain for 2 hours

Microstructural and mechanical characterizations have been conducted for the newly produced materials.
The SEM–EBSD maps presented here confirm the formation of nanograined structures resulting from the
applied processing routes; additional results are reported separately. Figures 1 and 2 show EBSD maps of
the 9YWTV-H1 and 9YWTV-H2 alloys following four thermomechanical treatments: (a) HR, (b) HRT, (c) LR,
and (d) LRT. These maps were obtained from cross-sectional planes normal to the rolling direction, which
also coincide with the tensile axis used in mechanical testing. Consequently, the grain structures in these
images exhibit minimal crystallographic texture and appear nearly equiaxed. It should be noted, however,
that EBSD analysis performed on planes parallel to the rolling direction would likely reveal elongated grains
aligned with the deformation direction, reflecting the imposed hot-rolling strain.
Overall, qualitative comparison of the EBSD maps indicates that the 9YWTV-H1 alloy generally exhibits a
finer grain structure across all processing routes. In particular, the PM1-LR condition shows a notably
refined microstructure relative to the other conditions. Carbon content emerges as the dominant factor
governing microstructural evolution in these Fe–9Cr alloys. Across all thermomechanical conditions, the
high-carbon alloy (9YWTV-H1) consistently exhibits finer and more uniform grain structures than the low-
carbon counterpart (9YWTV-H2), which shows larger average grain sizes and greater variability. For
example, the as-rolled PM2-HR condition (456 nm) is clearly coarser than PM1-HR (382 nm). This
refinement in the high-carbon alloy should be attributed to a higher number density of carbide and/or
carbonitride particles that effectively pin grain boundaries, suppress grain growth, and enhance stability
during hot rolling at 900 °C and tempering at 750 °C.
Rolling intensity also influences grain size evolution. Contrary to the expectations, the higher strain
condition (HR, 120%) produces coarser grains than the lower strain condition (LR, 50%). For instance,
PM1-LR achieves the finest grain size (~286 nm), whereas PM1-HR reaches ~382 nm. This trend is likely
due to increased thermal exposure in HR, where additional rolling passes promote recovery and grain
growth. Post-rolling annealing at 750 °C generally leads to further coarsening, particularly in LR conditions,
while HR conditions show greater thermal stability. Additionally, the low-carbon alloy exhibits significantly
higher grain size variability, indicating a more heterogeneous microstructure compared to the stable and
uniform structure of the high-carbon alloy.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

(c) LR, and (d) LRT

2.5 TENSILE PROPERTIES OF NANOSTRUCTURED Fe-9Cr STEELS WITH OXIDE–CARBIDE
HYBRID STRENGTHENING — T.S. Byun, D.A. Collins, K.M. Krogh, Y. Lin (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

This research aims to develop nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys engineered with oxide–carbide hybrid
nanoparticles to achieve thermally stable strengthening and high defect-sink efficiency under irradiation
over a broad temperature range. The primary objective of this testing task is to evaluate temperature-
dependent mechanical performance to support alloy downselection.

SUMMARY
This study focuses on the development and systematic evaluation of nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys
incorporating oxide–carbide hybrid strengthening. Two Fe–9Cr-based alloys with controlled additions of
oxygen (Y2O3 ~0.3 wt.%) and carbon (0.05 and 0.11 wt.%) were fabricated via mechanical milling, powder
consolidation by extrusion, and tailored multistep thermomechanical processing to promote grain
refinement and controlled nanoscale precipitation. Uniaxial tensile testing was conducted over a
temperature range from room temperature to 700 °C. Comparison of tensile properties reveals that a few
selected conditions exhibit exceptional strength–ductility combinations across the tested temperature
range. This finding provides a foundation for downselecting optimal alloy compositions and processing
routes for subsequent comprehensive qualification, including irradiation performance evaluation.

In the mechanical performance evaluation, uniaxial tensile and fracture toughness tests were conducted
over a wide temperature range. Eight variants of nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys with a nominal composition
of Fe–9Cr–2W–0.4Ti–0.2V (wt.%) were fabricated with low (0.05 wt.%) and high (0.11 wt.%) carbon levels
and a constant 0.3 wt.% Y2O3 addition. Processing combined streamlined powder metallurgy, including
mechanical milling and extrusion, with subsequent thermomechanical treatments. The as-extruded
materials were designated PM1 (high carbon) and PM2 (low carbon). Post-extrusion processing involved
hot rolling at two strain levels: 120% via four passes (HR) and 50% via two passes (LR), each following
annealing at 900 °C for 30 minutes. Tempering at 750 °C for 2 hours was applied to both conditions (HRT
and LRT).
Uniaxial tensile testing of SS-J2 specimens was performed at room temperature, 200 °C, 300 °C, 500 °C,
600 °C, and 700 °C at a nominal strain rate of 1 × 10⁻³ s⁻¹ using an electromagnetic mechanical testing
system. Load–displacement data were continuously recorded to failure and used to derive engineering
strength and ductility parameters. Figure 1 summarizes representative strength and ductility data—yield
strength and total elongation—for the eight processing conditions. Overall, the high-carbon alloys exhibit
significantly higher strength, while maintaining comparable ductility, albeit with greater variability, relative to
the low-carbon counterparts. The strength trends suggest that hybrid oxide–carbide precipitation effectively
stabilizes nanograin and dislocation substructures, thereby enhancing high-temperature strength–
toughness synergy. Although ductility generally increases with temperature, Figure 1(b) reveals a
pronounced reduction in the intermediate temperature range of 300–400 °C, likely associated with dynamic
strain aging. A significant decrease in ductility is observed at 700 °C, in particular in high-carbon materials,
indicating high-temperature degradation. These findings will inform the downselection of optimal alloy
compositions and processing routes upon the completion of fracture behavior analysis, supporting the
identification of a lead candidate for future comprehensive qualification, including irradiation performance
evaluation.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Temperature dependence of tensile properties for various alloy–processing combinations: (a)
yield strength and (b) total elongation.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

2.6 MICROSTRUCTURAL CHARACTERIZATION OF NANOSTRUCTURED Fe-9Cr ALLOYS WITH
OXIDE–CARBIDE HYBRID STRENGTHENING — Y. Lin, T.S. Byun (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

The objective of this work is to investigate and quantify nanoparticles in nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys with
oxide–carbide hybrid strengthening, aiming to extend the high-temperature operating window for fusion
applications.

SUMMARY
Transmission electron microscopy (TEM) characterization confirms the formation of a nanograined
microstructure containing both carbide and oxide particles in a new class of nanostructured Fe–9Cr ferritic
alloys with oxide–carbide hybrid strengthening. The average grain size of the four selected alloys ranges
from 364 to 517 nm. The diameter and number density of the Y–Ti–O and MX nanoparticles range from 2.9
to 5.7 nm and 2.1 × 10²¹ to 7.9 × 10²² m⁻³, respectively.

Nanostructured Fe–9Cr ferritic alloys were synthesized via an integrated processing route combining
powder metallurgy and thermomechanical treatment (TMT). Four representative alloy-process
combinations (PM1-HR, PM1-HRT, PM2-HR, and PM2-HRT), exhibiting favorable tensile properties and
fracture toughness, were selected for detailed TEM characterization to elucidate microstructure–property
relationships. PM1 and PM2 refer to two distinct mixtures of alloy and oxide powders, whereas HR and
HRT correspond to the 120% hot rolling at 900 °C and the same hot rolling followed by 750 °C tempering,
respectively. Detailed powder compositions and TMT parameters (temperature and strain) are reported
elsewhere.
STEM bright-field (BF) and annular dark-field (ADF) images reveal well-developed fine-grained
microstructures in the nanostructured Fe–9Cr alloys (Fig. 1), with PM1-HR exhibiting the smallest average
grain size (382 ± 184 nm) and PM2-HRT the largest (517 ± 287 nm). STEM–energy-dispersive X-ray
spectroscopy (EDS) further confirms the presence of fine Y–Ti–O and/or MX nanoparticles (Fig. 2), coarse
MX precipitates (Ti/V/W–C/N, >10 nm), and coarse Cr-rich oxide particles. The nanostructured Fe–9Cr
alloys exhibit a high density of fine Y–Ti–O and MX nanoparticles, with diameters of ~2.9–5.7 nm and
number densities on the order of 10²¹–10²² m⁻³, indicating effective nanoscale dispersion strengthening. In
contrast, coarse MX particles (>10 nm) show larger sizes (~14–23 nm) with lower number densities (~10²¹
m⁻³). Coarse Cr-O particles are the largest (~36–49 nm) and least dense (~10¹⁹ m⁻³). Comparatively, HR
conditions generally maintain higher nanoparticle number densities than HRT conditions, while tempering
(HRT) tends to increase particle size, particularly for coarse MX and Cr-rich precipitates. Notably, PM2-
HRT exhibits significant coarsening and a pronounced reduction in fine particle density, suggesting reduced
dispersion strengthening relative to other conditions. Overall, the microstructure is dominated by a high
density of ultrafine oxide and carbide nanoparticles, with secondary contributions from coarser MX and Cr-
O particles, and clear processing-dependent evolution in particle size and density. These hybrid
dispersions, in both size and type, consisting of very fine (several nanometers) oxide and carbide particles
and coarser particles, may exhibit improved high-temperature mechanical performance, as the coarser
particles can provide increased resistance to dislocation glide and boundary migration in creep conditions.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9Cr alloys.

 3. CERAMIC COMPOSITE STRUCTURAL MATERIAL DEVELOPMENT

 52

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

3.1 FORMATION OF COMPOSITE SiC/SiC JOINTS BY EMBEDDED WIRE CHEMICAL VAPOR
DEPOSITION — B.W. Lamm, E. Cakmak, W. Zhong, T. Koyanagi (ORNL), J. Pegna (Free Form Fibers,
LLC)

ABSTRACT
The joining of ceramic monoliths or composites to date has primarily been limited to the formation of brittle
monolithic joints using heterogeneous (dissimilar) materials, similar to brazing in metals. The development
of a damage-tolerant joint layer by SiC ﬁber reinforcements is demonstrated here. Tube workpieces made
of SiC ﬁber-SiC matrix composite are joined using a nonwoven SiC ﬁber mat densiﬁed by embedded wire
chemical vapor deposition (EWCVD), creating a ﬁber-reinforced weld-like joint by homogeneous joining.
EWCVD uses a localized heating method to target deposition and growth to the joint region speciﬁcally,
while minimizing thermal damage to the surrounding composite tube material. X-ray computed tomography
(XCT) is used to nondestructively characterize as-made joints for relative density, adhesion, and
composition. In situ XCT analysis during mechanical testing revealed crack deﬂections in the bonding layer,
which indicates a toughening mechanism typical of ceramic matrix composite phase. Gas permeation
testing of these proof-of-concept composite joints identiﬁed relatively high leak rates in comparison to fully
coated SiC/SiC composite tube workpieces. The novelty of the composite joining method and current
technology challenges, including gas permeability, are discussed in comparison with traditional ceramic
joints and materials.
Abstract of the manuscript: Adv. Eng. Mater. 2025, 27 (21), e202501581.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

3.2 ELECTRON MICROSCOPY DATA ON IRRADIATION EFFECTS IN GLASSY CARBON, NUCLEAR
GRAPHITE, PYROLYTIC CARBON, AND CARBON FIBERS — J. David Arregui-Mena, Takaaki Koyanagi,
David A. Cullen, Michael J. Zachman, Yan-Ru Lin, Kyle Everett, Sabrina Gonzalez-Calzada, Phillip D.
Edmondson, Tyler J. Gerczak, Yutai Katoh, Nidia C. Gallego (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Abstract of a manuscript in Data in Brief (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.dib.2025.111918)

Carbon materials have become increasingly diverse, finding applications in high-temperature and high-
radiation environments. Glassy carbon, a relevant material of interphase in SiC fiber composites for fusion
applications, an allotrope known for its exceptional chemical inertness and desirable mechanical properties.
This article presents comprehensive electron microscopy data revealing the effects of neutron and electron
irradiation on glassy carbon. For comparison, additional data are provided for pyrolytic graphite and carbon
fibers, materials that exhibit similar structural behavior under irradiation. In situ electron irradiation
experiments further illustrate the real-time microstructural evolution of glassy carbon during exposure. The
dataset is organized into five parts: (1) transmission electron microscopy (TEM) micrographs of as-received
and neutron irradiated glassy carbon; (2) TEM micrographs of neutron irradiated graphite; (3) TEM
micrographs of unirradiated and irradiated carbon–carbon composites; (4) TEM micrographs.

 4. PLASMA INTERACTIONS, HIGH HEAT FLUX MATERIALS, AND COMPONENT TESTING

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

4.1 OPTIMIZING THERMAL DESORPTION SPECTROSCOPY FOR HIGH THROUGHPUT, HIGH
ACCURACY DEUTERIUM MEASUREMENTS IN FUSION MATERIALS — Masashi Shimada (Idaho
National Laboratory)

The objective of this work is to develop and demonstrate a high-throughput Thermal Desorption
Spectroscopy (TDS) system capable of high-accuracy deuterium quantification in fusion-relevant materials.
This includes establishing a robust, reproducible calibration method using molecular-flow method and
implementing instrumentation and analytical improvements that significantly reduce measurement
uncertainty. The overarching goal is to provide a standardized TDS platform to support fusion materials
research, fuel retention assessments, and multi-institutional data comparability.

SUMMARY
Precise quantification of hydrogen isotopes in plasma-facing component (PFC) materials remains a
challenge in fusion materials and fusion nuclear science, where accurate measurements of deuterium (D)
and tritium (T) retention are essential for predicting tritium inventory and evaluating material performance.
Conventional TDS systems often show large uncertainties due to short-lifetime certified leaks, variable
system geometries, inconsistent temperature profiles, and spectral interference among hydrogen isotopes
and helium [1].
A new TDS platform developed at Idaho National Laboratory (INL) addresses these limitations through an
optimized system architecture and a reliable molecular-flow calibration method using a standard
conductance element (SCE). Dual pressure controllers and high-accuracy capacitance manometers
provide stable, reproducible leak generation with uncertainties approaching 0.5%, an order-of-magnitude
improvement over traditional calibration methods. Additional upgrades reduce background hydrogen
outgassing, enhance mass-spectrometric discrimination among D₂, HD, and He, and incorporate gate
valves and an independent vacuum subsystem to support high-throughput measurements.
Overall, this work establishes a foundation for high-throughput, low-uncertainty TDS measurements and
provides a clear pathway toward standardized deuterium quantification across the fusion materials
community.

The high-throughput TDS system was designed for high sensitivity in the 1–6 amu range and incorporates
high-conductance, low-volume stainless-steel gas pathways to minimize residence time and reduce
hydrogen outgassing by limiting metal in the heated zone. Background suppression has been further
improved by redesigning the heated region with low-solubility materials, resulting in a more stable baseline.
A key advancement is the implementation of a molecular-flow calibration method using a standard
conductance element (SCE), dual pressure controllers, and high-accuracy capacitance manometers (26,
1.3k, 133k Pa). This approach provides stable, reproducible leak rates from 1×10⁻⁹ to 1×10⁻⁶ Pa·m³/s,
eliminating reliance on short-lifetime certified leaks and enabling a long-term, low-uncertainty calibration
framework. A high-resolution quadrupole mass spectrometer was also installed to clearly resolve D2 (4.028
amu) from He (4.003 amu), and automated control software now synchronizes temperature ramping,
pressure regulation, and QMS acquisition to enhance repeatability and reduce operator involvement.
Analytical improvements—including Gaussian deconvolution for separating D2 and He peaks and
drift-correction routines for QMS sensitivity changes—further support high-accuracy deuterium
quantification, with processing tools converting ion currents into absolute D inventories based on the
molecular-flow calibration constants.
Results
The molecular-flow calibration approach has markedly reduced measurement uncertainty. Conventional
certified-leak methods often produce uncertainties above 10%, whereas the new technique achieves values
near 0.5% with week-to-week variability below 1%. The system supports leak rates spanning four orders of
magnitude, providing flexible calibration for both low-inventory and high-retention specimens. Background
levels were also reduced through optimized material selection, component preparation, and thermal
conditioning, improving sensitivity for low-retention materials.

 56

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. INL’s high-throughput TDS system, incorporating molecular-flow calibration and low-background
design to support low-uncertainty deuterium measurements.
Performance improvements extend to both instrumentation and throughput. The upgraded high-resolution
mass spectrometer now resolves the He/D2 mass overlap, while Gaussian deconvolution and
drift-correction routines ensure accurate separation of D2 and He during complex desorption events.
Enhanced temperature-ramp control and a simplified sample-mount design have reduced total
measurement time by roughly 50%, enabling processing of approximately five samples per day compared
with two in traditional workflows. Planned inter-laboratory comparisons will further validate system
performance and support broader adoption of standardized TDS practices within the fusion materials
community. Figure 1 shows INL’s high-throughput TDS system, which incorporates molecular-flow
calibration and a low-background design to support low-uncertainty deuterium measurements.

Future work will focus on completing system validation, expanding analytical capabilities, and advancing
standardized TDS practices for fusion materials research. Immediate priorities include comprehensive
repeatability testing across a range of deuterium inventories and heating profiles, followed by
inter-laboratory benchmarking to establish comparability and promote adoption of molecular-flow calibration
as a shared standard. Additional development will enhance the analytical software suite through automated
uncertainty propagation, improved deconvolution tools for mixed-gas environments, and streamlined
reporting functions to support high-volume measurement campaigns.

(2017), https://inis.iaea.org/records/kg4g4-1w705/files/49059864.pdf

4.2 DEFECT FORMATION AND TRANSMUTATION BEHAVIORS IN IRRADIATED TUNGSTEN UNDER
THERMAL-NEUTRON SHIELDED AND MIXED SPECTRUM CONDITIONS — Weicheng Zhong, Yan-Ru
Lin, Yutai Katoh (ORNL), Akira Hasegawa (Tohoku University).

This work investigates the microstructural evolution of tungsten irradiated under a thermal-neutron shielded
condition and compares it with irradiation in a mixed spectrum irradiation condition. The insight gained from
this work is essential for extrapolating existing fission reactor data to fusion conditions for improving
predictive models of tungsten’s behavior in a fusion environment.

SUMMARY
Polycrystalline W was irradiated in the High Flux Isotope Reactor (HFIR) at two different conditions: thermal-
neutron shielded and mixed spectrum (unshielded). Under thermal-neutron shielded conditions, voids are
uniformly distributed in the matrix, with transmutation product Re and Os segregation around voids. In
contrast, under mixed spectrum, Re/Os-rich precipitates were observed, and large voids are present
adjacent to the precipitates.

Table 1 lists the irradiated tungsten samples, their irradiation conditions and their estimated Re+Os
transmutation content. Transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and Energy Dispersive Spectroscopy
(EDS) were used to characterize the voids and the transmutation products in irradiated W,

Results
Under the thermal-neutron shielded conditions, void formation was observed in all tungsten samples (AT07,
AT04, and AT02), the void size increased with irradiation temperature. Faceted void morphologies were
most prominent in AT07, as shown in the bright-field inset image of Figure 1a, suggesting that these voids
are under-pressurized. At lower irradiation temperatures, the voids are significantly smaller. To improve their
visibility, under-focused and over-focused Fresnel imaging techniques were employed. The voids in AT04
and AT02 are shown in Figure 1b and Figure 1c, where smaller voids but with higher number density were
observed.

4.3 DEVELOPMENT OF A MICROCANTILEVER BENDING PROTOCOL FOR RADIATION-
EMBRITTLEMENT EVALUATION OF BCC ALLOYS — G. R. Khan, A. Garcia-Caraveo, T. Chen (Oregon
State University), W. Setyawan (Pacific Northwest National Laboratory)

To develop a microcantilever bending protocol specifically tailored for evaluating radiation embrittlement
bcc alloys, where shallow ion-damage layer constrains specimen geometry. By systematically selecting
crystal orientation and loading rate, applying J-integral analysis, this protocol aims to isolate irradiation-
induced changes in fracture behavior within the limited damage volume while preserving physically
meaningful toughness measurements.

SUMMARY
Evaluating simulated radiation embrittlement in bcc alloys via microcantilever (MC) bending is challenging
because of the shallow ion-damage layer (~1 μm) severely limiting specimen dimensions, which may be
subjected to strong size effects. This work develops a dedicated protocol to regulate the otherwise
overwhelming plasticity in small specimens. A literature survey on cleavage plane and loading orientation
effects in bcc metals informed the selection of the {100} plane and <100> loading direction for the MCs
produced in tungsten particles of the DPT-W alloy, chosen to promote brittle cleavage and minimize
ambiguity in fracture mode identification. Key findings from orientation experiments confirms this as lowest
toughness value of Kinit = 10.36 MPa√m of the MCs is found in this plane and loading direction. A rapid
loading rate of 500 nm/s (contact point displacement rate) was adopted to suppress plastic zone growth
relative to the small specimen size while keeping in-situ scanning electron microscopy (SEM) observations
feasible. And lastly J-integral analysis was applied to estimate toughness, avoiding the reliance on small-
scale yielding assumptions. Together, these selections form a coherent protocol framework for future testing
of irradiated bcc microstructures at elevated temperatures for brittle-to-ductile transition (BDT) studies.

Introduction
Ion irradiation offers a rapid and cost-effective means to emulate radiation damage in candidate plasma-
facing materials (PFMs), avoiding the need for prolonged neutron irradiation. This acceleration comes with
a key trade-off: damage is confined to a shallow micron-scale depth, limiting specimen geometry and
requiring small-scale testing. Despite this, it enables efficient screening of radiation-induced embrittlement
and deformation, accelerating PFM development and down-selection.
BDT provides a direct measurement of radiation embrittlement in bcc alloys. In tungsten, the transition
temperature is controlled primarily by the onset of effective crack-tip plasticity controlled by the mobility of
1/2⟨111⟩ screw dislocations [1]. The crack-tip dislocation behavior is size-dependent, with dislocation
starvation and absence of plasticity at the smallest scales (toward the Griffith limit fracture toughness) or
with the formation of geometrical necessary dislocations under strong strain gradient at intermediate sizes
(giving higher than bulk fracture toughness) [2]. It is also important to note that the BDT temperature has
been shown to be rate-dependent and to shift to lower values at the microscale [3]. Higher loading rates
suppress thermally activated dislocation mobility, thereby confining the plastic zone and reducing stable
crack growth.
The fracture mode of the cantilevers depends on the balance between the creation of new fracture surfaces
and energy dissipation through crack tip plasticity. For a microcantilever that is essentially a single crystal,
both the cleavage plane and the crystallographic orientation of the applied force are important because the
former determines the fracture surface energy and the latter influences the process of dislocation activation.
Riedle et al. [4] established cleavage anisotropy in tungsten, and Gumbsch et al. [5] further showed that
{100} planes exhibit significantly lower room-temperature toughness (for crack system: {100}<010>, K =
8.7 MPa√m, for {100}<011>, K = 6.2 MPa√m) than {110} planes (for {110}<001>, K = 20.2 MPa√m and for
{110}<11� 0>, K = 12.9 MPa√m) in three-point bending tests. As the loading orientation dictates the
activations of dislocations of different systems, the {100}<010> crack system has been shown to be
controlled by the mobility of screw dislocations [5,6].

 61

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

To evaluate the fracture toughness, linear elastic fracture mechanics (LEFM) systematically underestimates
fracture toughness when the plastic zone around the crack-tip no longer obeys the small-scale yielding. For
microscale fracture toughness testing, the J-integral analysis is recommended to quantify the strain energy
release rate at the crack tip, encompassing both elastic and plastic contributions to the driving force for
fracture beyond the small-scale-yielding regime [2,6,7].

Our previous work adopts rectangular microcantilevers, which can only be fabricated at the edge of a
sample using FIB technique [8]. The present study employs pentagonal-shaped microcantilevers which can
be fabricated in the central area of a sample surface, allowing more possible sites to fabricate
microcantilevers of specific crack systems. Pentagonal-shaped microcantilevers with a 1×1 µm rectangular
cross-section are fabricated using a focused ion beam (FIB) such that the cross-section of interest remains
within the ~1 µm irradiation depth. The cantilever length is set to 5 µm. A flat notch is introduced near the
base, with a depth of 0.4 µm.
In-situ scanning electron microscope (SEM) microcantilever bending experiments were conducted. The J-
integral was estimated following the ASTM standard [7] using the following equations [7,9]. From the
resulting J value at fracture initiation, the corresponding initiation toughness (combined Kinit) can be
calculated.

Here ν is the Poisson’s ratio, taking 0.28; E the Young’s modulus estimated using nanoindentation
technique; P the Applied load; L the Distance between notch and loading; a the notch depth; h the beam
height; I the second moment of area of the beam cross-section; y the vertical distance from top surface to
neutral plane; F(a/h) the dimensionless shape factor based on notch and beam geometry for pentagonal
microcantilever obtained from finite element analysis [9].
In this protocol, only fracture initiation is considered, and the fracture initiation toughness is evaluated. This
approach is adopted as, beyond initiation, the combination of small specimen size, the pentagonal / house
shaped geometry and a relatively large plastic zone to sample size leads to significant plastic deformation,
particularly near the base, resulting in toughness overestimation. By restricting the evaluation to fracture
initiation, more consistent and representative toughness values can be obtained.
Crack initiation identification is made possible using continuous stiffness measurement (CSM) indentation
method and in-situ SEM recording. In CSM mode, a low amplitude (0.02 μm) sinusoidal oscillation (100 Hz)
is superimposed on the bending load to introduce rapid loading-unloading cycles, enabling continuous
stiffness measurement during deformation. The initiation of fracture is marked by a clear and sustained
drop in stiffness following the peak stiffness. This transition is further correlated with simultaneous in-situ
SEM recording to accurately determine the crack initiation point in time visually. The corresponding load-
displacement data, combined with geometric measurements obtained from SEM and relevant material
property inputs, are then used to calculate the fracture initiation toughness using Eqn. (1) - (6).
Results

 62

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Higher loading rates were progressively evaluated to identify a suitable value for the protocol. A rate of 500
nm/s was selected to limit crack tip plasticity development while remaining sufficiently moderate to permit
meaningful in situ SEM video observation. Figure 1 shows the load-displacement curves of three different
crack systems with crystallographic configurations of {100}<001>, {100}<011>, and {110}<111>. The
crystallographic plane denotes the dedicated fracture plane, and the crystallographic direction denotes the
loading direction.

Figure 1. Load–displacement curves in unirradiated W at room temperature, for orientation-dependent
microcantilever bending tests of {100}⟨001⟩ (black), {100}⟨011⟩ (red), and {110}⟨111⟩ (blue) crack systems.
Crack initiations are indicated by dotted lines.
The {100}<001> crack system (black curve) exhibits the least plastic yielding, with an early and sharp load
drop at relatively low displacement. The estimated fracture initiation point, indicated by the black dashed
line, is followed by a short regime of continued load increase attributed to strain hardening. Subsequently,
rapid crack propagation occurs, leading to a relatively brittle failure characterized by a steep load drop. The
initiation toughness for this configuration is estimated as Kinit = 10.36 MPa√m. It is further anticipated that
screw dislocation mobility controls DBT of this crack system, presenting a mechanistic similarity to bulk-
scale behavior [5]. The {100}<011> configuration (red curve) shows increased plastic yielding compared to
{100}<100>, along with a higher maximum load and a more gradual post-peak load reduction, indicating a
more ductile response under the same loading rate. The fracture imitates at a greater displacement than
the {100}<100> crack system does, yielding a toughness of 11.17 MPa√m. In contrast, the {110}<111>
configuration exhibits the highest peak load and a fully plasticity-dominated response. No distinct fracture
initiation can be identified; instead, the load decreases gradually after the peak with continued plastic
deformation. As such, only qualitative assessment is possible, indicating that this configuration
accommodates the greatest extent of plasticity among the three configurations.

CONCLUSION
A microcantilever bending protocol was established to evaluate ion-irradiation induced embrittlement in
BCC alloys within shallow ion-damage layers. This protocol minimizes crack-tip plasticity by targeting the
{100} cleavage plane, loading along the <001> direction, and employing a rapid rate of 500 nm/s. Using J-
integral analysis, the derived initiation toughness of Kinit = 10.36 MPa√m for the {100}<001> crack system,
agreeing with literature and our prior square cross-section microcantilever measurements, as shown in
Table 1. In the future, we plan to apply this approach to ion-irradiated BCC metals at evaluated temperatures
to evaluate irradiation effects on BDT.

temperature.

4.4 EFFECT OF SECONDARY PHASES AND SOLUTE ADDITIONS ON BONDING AND THERMAL
TRANSPORT AT W/Cu INTERFACES — Alice Perrin, Tim Lach, Hsin Wang (ORNL), Mingxi Ouyang,
Lance Snead, Jason Trelewicz (Stony Brook University)

This task focused on analyzing the thermal diffusivity across several W/Cu interfaces for the purpose of
understanding how bonding methods as well as alloy selection affect the functionality of the interface for
active cooling.

SUMMARY
Fusion reactor components such as divertors rely on strong bonds between dissimilar materials to maintain
structural integrity as well as desired functional properties such as efficient heat exchange [1]. The plasma
facing components, typically made up of W or W alloys, must be bonded to Cu-based high heat flux
components; these Cu alloy cooling channels facilitate water flow for continuous active cooling. This task
has examined several bilayer samples of W and Cu or Cu alloys to determine how the alloying choices and
bonding processes used affect the mechanical integrity and thermal transport efficiency of each bond.

W/Cu specimens were produced via a non-destructive bonding technique by Nippon Tungsten. W/CCNZ-
HP and W/CCNZ-HZ specimens were produced by a two-step sintering process in which W disks were first
sintered separately, and then CCNZ additions were pressed and sintered onto them at lower temperatures
up to 900°C until fully densified. Thermal diffusivity measurements were performed on polished disks of
W/Cu alloys using an LFA Netzch system. All LFA measurements were obtained such that the laser pulse
hit the W side of the bilayer samples so that the heat diffused through the W and into the Cu sample (Figure
1a). The heat signature on the Cu side of the sample was measured and an effective diffusivity value for
the bilayer stack was obtained. The thermal diffusivities of single-layer specimens of sintered W, CCNZ-HZ
and CCNZ-HP were also measured (Figure 1b). LFA measurements were performed with laser voltages of
250V and pulse times ranging from 0.2ms to 0.5ms. Measurements were performed at 50°C intervals from
50°C up to 700°C as well as at room temperature (~25°C).

 65

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. a) Schematic of laser flash diffusivity experimental setup on bilayer samples; resistance diagram
to the right corresponds to resistance of individual layers as well as the bonded interface resistance, RC, b)
thermal diffusivity data for single layer samples and bilayer W/Cu or W/CCNZ specimens.
Transmission electron microscopy samples were produced using a focused ion beam to obtain liftouts from
metallographically polished interfaces; liftouts were mounted to V-notch Mo grids and oriented such that
the interface between layers was parallel to the top of the sample with the Cu layer on top and W layer on
the bottom to reduce curtaining effects. High resolution microscopy and energy-dispersive x-ray
spectroscopy (EDX) was performed with a Themis high-resolution scanning transmission electron
microscope (STEM) system with a 200kV beam. Selected area electron diffraction (SAED) was performed
on secondary phases to confirm crystal structures.
Results
Thermal diffusivity measurements found that the effective diffusivity (αeff) of the W/CCNZ-HP sintered stack
was incredibly similar to the W/Cu NDB stack (Figure 1b) across all temperatures, though more variability
of the W/CCNZ-HP stack was observed. Interestingly, the measured thermal diffusivity of the CCNZ-HP
single layer specimen was also very similar to literature measurements of pure Cu despite having a 28%
lower electrical conductivity (which scales linearly with thermal conductivity in most metallic materials).
Meanwhile, both the single-layer CCNZ-HZ and bilayer W/CCNZ-HZ samples showed lower thermal
diffusivity across all temperatures, though the diffusivity of CCNZ-HZ trends towards the CCNZ-HP values
at higher temperatures. Given the expected conductivity of CCNZ-HZ is 12% higher than CCNZ-HP, this
result suggests significant difference in the sintered CCNZ-HZ compared with literature values of rolled and
aged bulk CCNZ-HZ, likely due to microstructural differences as well as potential sintering defects and
imperfections leading to lack of fusion.

 66

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2: a) TEM micrograph of CCNZ-HP/W interface; b) micrograph and EDX scans of red inset region
in (a) showing fine Cr precipitates near W interface; c) micrograph and EDX scans of green inset region in
(a) showing similar fine Cr precipitates throughout CCNZ-HP microstructure within grains and at grain
boundaries. Green arrows indicate some Cr precipitates visible in micrographs for clarity.
TEM analysis of the W/CCNZ interfaces found that despite the similar compositions and phases present in
each alloy, the character of the microstructures formed in both the bulk and at interfaces differed in key
aspects. TEM analysis of the W/CCNZ-HP specimen (Figure 2) found that the microstructure of the CCNZ-
HP bulk was comprised of nanoscale Cr precipitates at grain boundaries; the micron-scale NbCr2 phase
was not captured in the TEM specimen. Some Cr precipitates were observed at the interface with W, but
the interface primarily showed a direct bond between the Cu matrix and the W layer.
In contrast, in the W/CCNZ-HZ specimen, a coarser grain of (Cu,Cr)5Zr was captured, while little evidence
of Cr precipitates was observed (Figure 3). Nanoscale NbCr2 phase was found primarily along the CCNZ/W
interface. As the design of these alloys is intended to produce only micron-scale NbCr2 the microstructure
of these alloys is evidently significantly different than their cast and rolled counterparts. It is also unclear if
the excessive formation of NbCr2 at the interface is due to uneven mixing of the sintered powders leading
to higher concentrations of this phase at the interface, or if this formation is due to a thermodynamic driving
force causing preferential diffusion of these elements towards the interface.
The contrast between the W/CCNZ-HZ and W/CCNZ-HP microstructures highlights the importance of
specific materials selection for complex thermal transport across the bonded interface; meanwhile, the
similarity of the thermal diffusivity of W/CCNZ-HP and W/Cu demonstrates that multiple processing/bonding
techniques and materials can be used to produce functional W/Cu based divertor components.

 67

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3: a) TEM micrograph of CCNZ-HZ/W interface with blue arrows denoting NbCr2 Laves phase
formation along interface; b) micrograph and EDX scans of blue inset region in (a) showing NbCr2 Laves
phase particle; c) micrograph and EDX scans of cyan inset region in (a) showing (Cu,Cr)5Zr phase.

Future work on this project will focus on characterization of the interface bond’s resistance to assess the
efficacy of the sinter-bonding technique used to produce the W/CCNZ samples for comparison with the
non-destructive bonding technique used for W/Cu specimens. Micro-mechanical bond testing will further
assess overall bond strength of all bilayer specimens.

[1] Zinkle, Applicability of copper alloys for high heat flux components, Phys. Scr. T167(014004) (2016), doi:
10.1088/0031-8949/2015/T167/014004

 68

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

4.5 ALLOY DESIGN OF LOW-ACTIVATION DPT-W COMPOSITES  Mohan Nartu and Wahyu Setyawan
(Pacific Northwest National Laboratory)

The objective of this project is to develop low-activation ductile-phase-toughened tungsten (DPT-W)
composites to improve the irradiation resistance of pure W as materials for plasma facing components, in
particular against irradiation-induced embrittlement under fusion environment.

SUMMARY
Compatibility of candidate ductile phases with W is studied to assess their applicability to form a dual-phase
DPT-W composite. For fcc Fe-based ductile phases, despite their broad single-phase fcc stability, they tend
to form Fe–W intermetallics even at low W contents, making them unsuitable for DPT-W integration. In
contrast, bcc Ti-, V-, and Cr-based ductile phases exhibit good compatibility with W and form a stable dual
phase across a wide temperature range. Furthermore, addition of Mn provides beneficial solid-solution
strengthening and ductility without destabilizing the bcc phase. Here, we report the eight down-selected
DPT-W composite systems based on Ti- and Cr-rich ductile matrix phases for fabrication.

In the previous reporting period, we presented the initial assessment for selecting low-activation elements
for the candidate ductile phases of the DPT-W composites. The ductile phase candidates are based on bcc
Ti, V, and Cr, and fccFe. Minor alloying elements may include Mn, Fe, Zn, Sn, Si, C, and B. The principal
consideration is the thermodynamics stability to form a single-phase in the temperature range of
approximately 700 - 1200 ℃. However, unanticipated thermal fluctuations may occur, potentially causing
the temperature to drop below 700℃ during operation. Therefore, an additional temperature window of 200-
300℃ has been factored in for alloy development. Thermodynamic parameters for a total of 508 binary and
ternary bcc alloys comprising of Ti, V< Cr, and Mn were determined using Therm-Calc software. Phase
stability plots for Ti-V-Cr, Ti-V-Mn, Ti-Cr-Mn, and Cr-V-Mn were presented. For fcc Fe, it was found that Mn
and Cr showed potential in stabilizing fcc Fe single phase. Addition of Ti and V was also studied. Several
Fe-Mn-Cr, Fe-Mn-Ti, and Fe-Mn-V demonstrated excellent single-phase fcc stability and exhibited solidus
start temperatures higher than 1400 ℃.
In the current reporting period, we study the compatibility of the candidate ductile phases with W to form a
dual-phase composite. Although Fe-based fcc ductile phase alloys were found to have promosing
single-phase stability, compatilibity assessments with W revealed that many of these compositions form
undesirable intermetallics, even for low atomic fractions of W. Figure 1 shows the calculated phase fraction
versus temperature plots for Fe-40Mn and Fe-36Mn-10W (all in at.%). The ductile matrix composition Fe-
40Mn clearly exhibits a single-phase fcc across a wide temperature range, 300-1300 °C (Figure 1(a)).
However, with the addition of 10 at.% W (keeping the same 60/40 atomic fraction for Fe/Mn), the composite
alloy (Fe-36Mn-10W) reveals a significant fraction of the Fe-W-based intermetallic phase, the μ phase,
below 600 °C, rendering the Fe-based fcc ductile matrix phase undesirable for the DPT-W composite.
Consequently, our focus shifted primarily to Ti-, V-, and Cr-based bcc systems, beginning with Ti- and Cr-
based low-activation ductile matrices and subsequently expanding to V-containing variants to ensure
microstructural stability and W compatibility across the targeted operating temperature range.

 69

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. CALPHAD-predicted phase fraction versus temperature plots for Fe-40Mn and Fe-36Mn-10W.
One important property of BCC alloys containing V, Cr, and Ti is their lower vacancy migration energy. This
characteristic allows vacancies to be mobile, thereby enhancing the material's resistance to void swelling
[1, 2]. In addition to improving the void-swelling resistance of alloys, Mn could enhance solid-solution
strengthening and ductility in the ductile matrix phase [3, 4]. Due to this dual benefit, Mn has been
incorporated into alloy design, though only in moderate quantities to not impact the bcc phase stability or
compromise other material properties, such as lowering of the melting point.
Thermo-Calc-predicted binary phase diagrams for W–Ti and W–Cr, shown in Figure 2, demonstrate that
both systems possess a substantial temperature range in which a dual-phase bcc (bcc ductile phase matrix
and bcc W) microstructure remains stable, which is an essential requirement for DPT-W composites. The
maximum dual-phase stability temperatures occur at approximately 30 wt.% Ti and 20 wt.% Cr, with
corresponding transition temperatures of 1221 °C and 1636 °C, respectively. In both alloy systems, the
upper stability temperature (Tmax) initially increases with the addition of Ti or Cr, reaches a peak, and then
decreases with further alloying. To ensure a wide dual-phase BCC stability regime, W–30 wt.% Ti and W–
20 wt.% Cr were selected as the baseline DPT-W composite compositions for the Ti-rich and Cr-rich
categories. All additional alloying strategies are developed as modifications to these two baseline systems.

 70

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Thermo-Calc predicted binary phase diagram of W-Ti and W-Cr alloys.
For the Ti-based DPT-W composites, the 70/30 W/matrix ratio was maintained, with alloying additions
replacing Ti within the 30 wt.% fraction of the ductile matrix. Thermo-Calc (using TCFE13 database)
evaluations were performed by adding V, Cr, and Mn in 2 or 4 wt.% increments to assess their influence on
the dual-phase bcc stability regime, particularly the maximum dual-phase stability temperature, Tmax. The
effects of these alloying additions are shown in Figure 3(a). Both V and Cr, which strongly stabilize the bcc
phase, shift the system toward a single-phase bcc field and therefore reduce Tmax. Cr exhibits a more
pronounced destabilizing effect on the dual-phase bcc than V; for example, a 4 wt.% V addition results in a
Tmax of 1118 °C, whereas 4 wt.% Cr lowers it to 1071 °C. Because the baseline W–30Ti composite exhibits
a Tmax of 1221 °C, only slightly above the intended maximum operating temperature of 1200 °C. Thus,
Ti-based systems offer limited flexibility for alloying with elements such as V or Cr that strongly promote a
single-phase BCC structure. Accordingly, a working threshold of 1100 °C was adopted to guide composition
down-selection.
As illustrated in Figure 3(a), W–26Ti–4V meets the Tmax>1100 °C criterion and was therefore selected,
whereas W–26Ti–4Cr falls slightly below the threshold and was excluded. However, W–28Ti–2Cr remains
above the threshold and has been included for further evaluation. Mn exhibits a different trend: moderate
Mn additions initially increase Tmax, but higher concentrations eventually reduce it below the threshold.
Although W–26Ti–4Mn achieves the peak Tmax, Thermo-Calc phase-fraction predictions reveal the
formation of a significant C14 Laves (intermetallic) phase, producing an undesired three-phase
(BCC#1 + BCC#2 + C14) microstructure at all relevant temperatures. Consequently, this composition was
not selected. In contrast, W–28Ti–2Mn maintains a strictly dual-phase bcc microstructure up to 1341 °C,
which is well above the 1100 °C threshold, and has been down-selected for experimental assessment.
Based on these criteria, the Ti-based DPT-W compositions selected for PM-HIP fabrication are W–30Ti,
W–26Ti–4V, W–28Ti–2Cr, and W–28Ti–2Mn (all in wt.%).

 71

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. Thermo-Calc-predicted variation of the maximum dual-phase bcc stability temperature (Tmax) with
alloying additions for Ti-based and Cr-based DPT-W composites. The dashed line indicates the 1100 °C
threshold used for composition down-selection.
A similar Thermo-Calc analysis (using the TCHEA7 database) was performed for the Cr-based DPT-W
composites, and the resulting Tmax versus alloying-element content trends are shown in Figure 3(b). In
contrast to the Ti-based systems, all three alloying additions, V, Mn, and Ti, exhibited a consistent
destabilizing influence on the dual-phase BCC regime, as indicated by the monotonic decrease in Tmax with
increasing solute content. Although Mn and Ti additions produced Tmax values well above the 1100 °C
threshold up to approximately 7 wt.% addition, even a small Ti addition (2 wt.%) resulted in the formation of
a C14 Laves phase that remains stable up to 830 °C. This reduces the strictly dual-phase BCC region to
temperatures above 830 °C, which is unacceptable for DPT-W service. Consequently, Ti addition was
eliminated from further consideration in the Cr-based system.
Similarly, an 8 wt.% Mn addition led to the prediction of a Cr3Mn5 intermetallic phase stable up to 750 °C,
which also lies within the desired DPT-W operational range, rendering this composition unsuitable.
However, when Mn was limited to 6 wt.%, the Cr3Mn5 phase was predicted to be stable only up to 546 °C,
ensuring a strictly dual-phase bcc microstructure within the operational window. Thus, W–14Cr–6Mn was
selected for experimental evaluation. In contrast, V additions showed no intermetallic phases and preserved
a wide dual-phase bcc stability range, with Tmax remaining well above 1100 °C up to 10 wt.% V. To maintain
consistency with the Mn-modified system and to enable a clear comparison of the independent effects of V
and Mn on mechanical and irradiation behavior, a 6 wt.% V addition was chosen, leading to the selection
of W–14Cr–6V.
Finally, to explore the combined influence of V and Mn, a modification containing 3 wt.% V and 3 wt.% Mn
was evaluated. This composition retained a robust dual-phase bcc stability regime and did not exhibit
intermetallic phase formation within the operational temperature range. Therefore, W–14Cr–3Mn–3V was
included among the down-selected Cr-based DPT-W candidates. Together with the four Ti-based alloys,
these Cr-based compositions complete the eight DPT-W composites listed in Table 1, all of which will be
fabricated via PM-HIP and subsequently evaluated for microstructural stability, irradiation tolerance, and
mechanical performance.
Table 1. The down-selected DPT-W composites along with their dual-phase BCC stability temperature
regimes.

 Sample # Composition (wt.%) Tmin (°C) Tmax (°C)

                       1           W-30 Ti                     743         1221

                       2           W-26Ti-4V                   745         1118

                       3            W-28Ti-2Cr                  662         1137

                       4            W-28Ti-2Mn                  645         1341

                       5            W-20Cr                      0           1636

                       6            W-14Cr-6Mn                  546         1389

                       7            W-14Cr-6V                   0           1389

                       8            W-14Cr-3Mn-3V               0           1399

4.6 DUCTILITY MODELING OF W-Ti-Cr-V MULTI-PRINCIPAL-ELEMENT ALLOYS  Krishna Chaitanya
Pitike and Wahyu Setyawan (Pacific Northwest National Laboratory)

The objective of this project is to develop ductile low-activation W-Ti-Cr-V refractory multi-principal-element
alloys (rMPEAs) to improve the irradiation resistance of pure W as materials for plasma facing components,
in particular against irradiation-induced embrittlement under fusion environment.

SUMMARY
Neuro-evolution potential (NEP), a form of machine learning interatomic potential, is employed to study the
property of a ½[111] screw dislocation in W-Ti-Cr-V bcc refractory multiprincipal element alloys. First, the
Peierls barrier in pure W and the effect of a solute (Ti, V, or Cr) on the barrier is calculated with NEP and
DFT to validate the potential. Good agreement of the barrier in pure W and a consistent trend of solute
effect is observed. The validation supports the use of this potential for further dislocation studies in
concentrated compositions. The energy landscape of the screw dislocation from near a solute to a location
far from the solute is calculated. The results show that while the dislocation moves faster around a solute,
compared to pure W, the dislocation must overcome a higher overall barrier to effectively detrap from the
solutes.

Refractory multiprincipal element alloys (rMPEAs) are a promising class of materials for plasma-facing
applications in fusion energy systems due to their high melting temperatures, potential radiation tolerance,
and thermal stability. However, designing rMPEAs that simultaneously meet high-temperature performance
and mechanical reliability criteria remains a challenge. Recent studies have demonstrated the potential of
rMPEAs to address some of these challenges. RMPEAs have shown exceptional thermal stability,
resistance to softening at high temperatures, and remarkable damage tolerance under irradiation due to
sluggish defect diffusion kinetics and complex chemical environments.1,2 For instance, Cr-Ta-V-W rMPEAs
demonstrated superior irradiation damage tolerance against dislocation loop formation compared to pure
W under ion-beam exposure.3 However, the high afterheat associated with Ta poses limitations for long-
term deployment in fusion environments.4 Hence, in this study, we replace Ta with low-activation Ti and aim
to design W-Ti-V-Cr rMPEAs with improved ductility.
In our previous study, we developed ductility model for W-Ti-V-Cr alloys based on electronic structure
descriptor, bonding-state depletion (BSD).5 We also developed machine-learned models to predict the bcc
phase stability maps for W-Ti-V-Cr rMPEAs based on CALPHAD calculations. Using these models, we
screened alloy compositions that satisfy target conditions for ductility (peak true strain 𝜀𝜀𝑝𝑝 ≥ 5%), melting
temperature (𝑇𝑇𝑚𝑚 ≥ 2000 ℃), and beta-transus temperature (𝑇𝑇𝛽𝛽 ≤ 800 ℃). The electronic ductility
descriptors do not consider any properties of dislocation. Ductility is ultimately governed by the ability of the
alloy to dissipate plastic deformation through the formation and motion of dislocations. Here, we explore
possible different descriptors derived from dislocation property, i.e. the structure of the dislocation core (i.e.
compact vs non-compact, symmetry of the slip system), Peirels barrier and stress, kink-pair formation
energy, and dislocation mobility.
For dislocation study, we use molecular dynamics (MD) simulations with a neuro-evolution potential (NEP)
trained to accurately describe rMPEAs.6 We first validate the NEP by comparing the Peierls barrier
predicted by NEP with those obtained from density functional theory (DFT).

 74

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Periodic quadrupolar array of dislocation cores in a parallelepiped cell used to calculate the
migration barriers in pure W. The blue atom is replaced with a solute to calculate the effect of a solute (in
the dilute limit) on the Peierls barrier. There are two screw dislocations with opposite Burgers vectors (red
and green dots) initially placed at sites 0. Both dislocations are then moved simultaneously along the path
from site 0 to site 4 to study the migration energy landscape.

Figure 2 compares the migration barriers between positions 0 and 1 (from Figure 1), as calculated by both
DFT and NEP. The figure illustrates this comparison for systems containing Cr (a), Ti (b), and V (c) solutes,
as well as for pure W (d). The migration barriers for pure W calculated using Siesta and PWSCF DFT
codes7 agree well with our DFT calculations done with the VASP code. The migration barrier trends as a
function of solute predicted by NEP are consistent with the DFT predicted values.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Peierls barrier calculated using the climbing nudged elastic band (cNEB) method in bcc W crystal
near a solute atom (a) Cr, (b) Ti, (c) V, and (d) pure W, calculated with DFT and NEP. (e) presents the
comparison between all migration barriers calculated using DFT and NEP. In e), the barriers in pure W
calculated with Siesta and PWSCF codes are from published literature.7

Figure 3. Migration energy barriers calculated employing NEP between positions 0 and 4, as shown in
Figure 1.

Figure 3 presents the Peierls barrier of a screw dislocation near a solute (Cr, Ti, V) as the dislocation
migrates from site 0 to site 1, 2, 3, and finally site 4. We find that the Peierls barrier between 0 and 1 is
lower compared to pure W. However, the Peierls barrier along 1 to 2 path is higher compared to pure W.
Note that potential energy at site 2 is higher than at site 1. The potential energy further increases from site
2 to site 3 and an additional barrier. Finally, sites 3 and 4 show the same potential energy, thus these sites
represent an atomic environment of a pure W. Thus, the barriers from site 3 to 4 are the same as that of

 76

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

pure W. Overall, the detrapping barrier of a screw dislocation from Ti, Cr and V solutes (i.e. to move from
site 0 to 4) are 0.31, 0.26, and 0.2 eV, respectively.
These results show that Cr, Ti, and V solutes act as trapping sites for a screw dislocation in W. Future
research will study the following:
 • The effect of solutes on the structure of the dislocation core and on altering the slip system
 • The effect of solutes on the kink-pair formation energy
 • The effect of solutes on dislocation mobility

4.7 ROLE OF INTERFACE ELEMENT INTERMIXING IN HELIUM BUBBLE FORMATION IN
IRRADIATED DISPERSION-STRENGTHENDED TUNGSTEN — Ashrakat Saefan, Chase Hargrove, Xing
Wang (Pennsylvania State University)

Dispersion-strengthened tungsten (DSW) is a promising candidate for plasma-facing materials (PFMs) in
divertor regions. The microstructure and alloy chemistry play key roles in determining DSW performance
under reactor-relevant conditions. After identifying the major source of oxygen impurities in as-fabricated
DSW produced by spark plasma sintering (SPS), we performed hydrogen reduction of tungsten powders
for sintering and investigated its impact on the resulting DSW microstructure. We also examined the role of
interface chemistry in helium bubble formation, a key degradation mechanism in divertor PFMs.

SUMMARY
Hydrogen reduction of tungsten powders for SPS effectively reduced oxygen impurities in the fabricated
DSW and helped preserve the nanoscale grain morphology. Helium ion irradiation was performed on the
DSW samples. Consistent with previous studies, tungsten–dispersoid interfaces act as efficient defect sinks
that suppress helium bubble formation. However, detailed analysis revealed that metal element intermixing
may occur near the interfaces, producing local regions with reduced resistance to helium ion irradiation.

1. Effects of hydrogen reduction on fabricated DSW
In our last semi-annual report, we showed that nano tungsten powders from several widely used vendors
contained thick oxide layers, which may contribute substantially to oxygen impurities in SPS-fabricated
DSW. To remove the intrinsic oxide layers, hydrogen gas reduction was performed at 1000 °C under flowing
H2 gas for 4 hours, and the processed powders were subsequently used to fabricate DW. Figure 1 presents
the representative grain morphology and element distribution in the newly fabricated samples. As shown in
the scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) image in Fig. 1a, the average grain size of tungsten
is approximately 400 nm, while the TiC dispersion phase has an average size of about 100 nm. Compared
with DSW fabricated without hydrogen reduction, the grain structure is significantly finer [1].

 78

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. STEM image and EDS element mapping of as-fabricated DSW containing 1 wt% TiC.
Element mapping using energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS) in Fig. 1b–f also confirmed clear
carbon signals in the dispersion phase, which were not observed in previous DSW samples [1]. Although
oxygen signals remain detectable in the dispersion phase, these results demonstrate that hydrogen
reduction is an effective approach for reducing oxygen impurities. Future studies will focus on optimizing
the reduction time, temperature, and gas flow rate to further improve the alloy chemistry of as-fabricated
DSW.
2. Impact of element intermixing on helium bubble formation
Helium bubble formation is an important degradation mechanism of tungsten as PFCs in divertors. Here
we irradiated DSW samples using 200 keV He ions at 850°C to a fluence of 4.5×1017 ions/cm2. Fresnel
contrast in transmission electron microscopy (TEM) was employed to image helium bubbles. Consistent
with previous findings [1], most W–TiC interface regions exhibited strong resistance to bubble formation,
with no bubbles observed at or near the interfaces. As discussed in the literature, this behavior is likely
attributed to effective point defect recombination and annihilation near the interfaces.

Figure 2. (a) Under-focused TEM image showing He bubble formation in irradiated DSW; (b,c) EDS
element mapping and 1D concentration profile near the interface with bubble formation; (d,e) EDS element
mapping and 1D concentration profile near the interface without bubble formation
However, regions containing dense helium bubbles, as shown in Fig. 2a, were also observed near some
W–TiC interfaces. These inconsistent behaviors are likely associated with metal element intermixing. As
shown in Fig. 2b–c, clear tungsten ingress into the TiC phase occurred in regions where dense bubbles
formed. In contrast, when tungsten ingress into the TiC phase was not observed (e.g., Fig. 2d–e), the
interface maintained its role as an effective defect sink and suppressed bubble formation. One possible
explanation is that element intermixing results in the formation of a W–Ti–C–O mixed phase with a reduced
melting point compared with non-mixed regions, which may effectively enhance vacancy mobility at a given
temperature and promote vacancy clustering locally. Tungsten ingress into TiC dispersion phases has been
reported previously [2]. As this intermixing may degrade the radiation resistance of DSW materials, further
research is needed to understand the underlying mechanisms and identify methods to minimize this effect.

4.8 FUNCTIONALLY GRADED CU-W INTERLAYERS FOR ENHANCED DIVERTOR/HEATSINK
BONDING — Katherine Lee, Timothy Hall (Faraday Technology)

The high temperature performance of direct joints between tungsten plasma facing materials and CuCrZr
heatsinks is compromised by thermal stresses resulting from the mismatch in the coefficient of thermal
expansion (CTE). This mismatch can be mitigated by incorporating a functionally graded material with
intermediate CTE values as an interlayer between tungsten and CuCrZr. The objective of this program is
to develop a new method for manufacturing functionally graded tungsten alloys and demonstrate their
performance as CTE-matching interlayers in tungsten/CuCrZr braze joints.

SUMMARY
A novel method for manufacturing tungsten/CuCrZr joints with functionally graded iron/tungsten (Fe/W)
alloy interlayers has been developed. The key innovation is the use of electrochemical deposition to apply
the functionally graded tungsten alloy to a tungsten substrate; the tungsten alloy is then joined to CuCrZr
via brazing. Electrochemical deposition has been shown to produce well-adhered, low porosity (< 0.1%)
tungsten alloys under mild conditions (T < 60°C, ambient pressures) with wide range of tungsten content
(10-65% W w/w). The ability to manufacture tungsten/CuCrZr braze joints incorporating these tungsten
alloy interlayers was first demonstrated on the coupon-scale. Flat-tile plasma facing component mock-ups
were then manufactured and used for high heat flux testing. This testing showed that braze joints with
tungsten alloy interlayers were stable under fusion-relevant thermal loads (10 MW/m2).

During electrochemical deposition, a tungsten substrate is submerged in an electrolyte containing dissolved
iron and tungsten cations. A current applied between the tungsten and a counter electrode reduces the
cations, depositing a metallic Fe/W alloy on the tungsten. First, the ability to deposit Fe/W alloys with a
constant tungsten wt% (ranging from 10–65% W w/w) was demonstrated. The tungsten content of the alloy
was controlled by varying user-defined parameters such as electrolyte composition, temperature, and
applied current. Figure 1 shows how the alloy microstructure can be controlled by modulating the electric
field. Fe/W alloys with identical tungsten content (40% W w/w) were prepared using a direct current electric
field (where a constant cathodic current is applied) and a pulsed current electric field (where the cathodic
pulse is periodically interrupted by an off-time). Despite having identical tungsten content, the alloy
deposited with a direct current electric field had a high degree of porosity and dendritic growth (Figure 1A)
while the alloy deposited with a pulsed current electric field had negligible porosity or voids (Figure 1B).

 A B

                        (40% W w/w)                                    (40% W w/w)

 Tungsten
Figure 1. SEM of Fe/W alloy (40% W w/w) electrochemically deposited with a (A) direct current and (B)
pulsed current electric field. This shows how the electric field can be used to control alloy microstructure.

Next, the ability to manufacture functionally graded Fe/W alloys with two or more layers (where each layer
has a different tungsten content) by sequencing different electrochemical deposition parameters was
demonstrated. Figure 2 shows an example of a functionally graded Fe/W alloy. In this example, two
electrochemical deposition parameters were sequenced, with Parameter 1 applying a tungsten-rich (> 50%

 81

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

W w/w) alloy to the tungsten substrate and Parameter 2 applying an iron-rich (< 50% W w/w) alloy to the
tungsten-rich layer. The resulting SEM/EDS (Figure 2) shows a three-layer stack consisting of the tungsten
substrate, tungsten-rich alloy (60% W w/w), and iron-rich alloy (40% W w/w).

Figure 2. SEM/EDS data for a functionally graded Fe/W alloy generated by sequencing different
electrochemical deposition parameters. Plot of the signal intensity of tungsten (yellow) and iron (green) are
overlaid with the cross-sectional image. Line scan location is denoted in red line.
Braze joint manufacturing was first demonstrated on the coupon-scale. Tungsten/CuCrZr braze joints with
and without interlayers were fabricated using a 0.002ʺ-thick 50/50 Cu/Au braze alloy. Figure 3 shows an
example SEM/EDS of a braze joint prepared using an iron-rich alloy (35‒40% W w/w, 200-230 µm, porosity
< 0.1%). Four distinct layers are visible corresponding to the CuCrZr coupon, Cu/Au braze alloy, Fe/W alloy,
and tungsten coupon. High-resolution SEM/EDS collected at the interface between the Cu/Au braze alloy
and the Fe/W alloy interlayer (Figure 3B) shows a diffusion zone with an increased Fe/W content and
decreased Cu/Au content (relative to the Cu/Au braze alloy layer). The interface is free of potentially weak
intermetallics and other defects (e.g., porosity, voids) that occur when Kirkendall effects are present.

Figure 3. Representative SEM/EDS of a braze joint with an Fe/W alloy interlayer showing the (A) CuCrZr

braze alloy and Fe/W alloy. Signal intensity of copper (yellow), gold (orange), iron (green), and tungsten
(pink) are overlaid. Red line denotes line scan location.
Flat-tile PFC mock-ups with and without Fe/W alloy braze joints were then manufactured and tested under
steady-state cyclic loading at 10 MW/m2 (Figure 4). Due to time constraints, high heat flux testing was
limited to 40 cycles. Both braze joints with and without Fe/W alloy interlayers were found to be stable under
these limited test conditions making it impossible to extrapolate what (if any) improvements in braze joint
lifetime might be achievable with the Fe/W alloy interlayers.
