4.9 MICROSTRUCTURE AND MECHANICAL PROPERTIES OF 1D-ROD SHAPED WTiCrV MULTI-
PRINCIPAL-ELEMENT-ALLOY PROCESSED THROUGH LASER-POWDER-BED-FUSION ADDITIVE
MANUFACTURING  Ishtiaque Robin, Osman El-Atwani, and Wahyu Setyawan (Pacific Northwest
National Laboratory), Matthew Vigil, Caleb Hatler, Dan Thoma (University of Wisconsin)

The objective of this research is to optimize the process parameters for laser power bed fusion (LPBF)
additive manufacturing (AM) to produce W–Ti–Cr–V multi-principal element alloy (MPEA) with targeted
composition and improved mechanical properties. The previously down-selected composition is predicted
to retain a stable single-phase body-centered cubic (BCC) structure under fusion-relevant extreme
conditions, a low β-transus temperature, and a high melting point. Different process parameters including
laser power, velocity, and liner energy density (LED) were varied in AM process to manufacture four
samples in rod shape with 3 mm diameter. Evaluated microstructural characterization and mechanical
properties of these samples revealed insights on effectiveness of different process parameters for LBPF
AM.

SUMMARY
Manufacturing refractory high entropy alloys for a target composition is an extremely challenging task.
Utilizing thermophysical parameters and phase diagrams through high-throughput calculation using Pandat
software, a few compositions for WTiCrV were down-selected which are expected to retain a BCC zone in
large temperature range and maintain a high melting temperature. One such composition was chosen for
fabrication through LPBF additive manufacturing. Process parameters were varied to produce four different
samples with same composition. Mechanical properties of these samples were evaluated using 3-point
bend test and microstructural characterization of these samples were conducted utilizing scanning electron
microscopy (SEM), energy-dispersive spectroscopy (EDS), electron back-scattered diffraction (EBSD). The
findings from microstructural and mechanical properties provide us with insights into how desired properties
can be achieved through fine tuning the process parameters during manufacturing.

From large compositional space for WTiCrV multi-principal elemental alloys (MPEAs), a few compositions
were down-selected using thermo-mechanical parameters and phase diagram analysis utilizing high
throughput calculations in Pandat software. One composition with 55W-35Ti-5Cr-5V in at% were chosen to
be manufactured using laser powder bed fusion (LPBF), an additive manufacturing technique suitable for
producing complex refractory alloy components. Four samples were manufactured, all of which are built as
1D rods with a diameter of 3 mm to eliminate the microstructural complexity arising from overlapping raster
during AM process. Power and velocity were changed to control liner energy density (LED) of AM process
to produce fully dense samples with good mechanical properties. Four samples, referred to as sample 5,
sample 6, sample 8, and sample 9 were produced with different process parameters, which are presented
in the Table 1 below. Yield strength from 3-point bend test for four samples were also presented in the same
table. The LPBF process was conducted using pre-alloyed WTiCrV powder, ensuring compositional
uniformity. However, challenges such as incomplete powder melting and chemical inhomogeneity were
observed, which are being addressed through ongoing process optimization.
Microstructural analysis was performed using SEM, EDS, and EBSD to evaluate the phase composition,
and grain morphology of the fabricated samples. SEM imaging provided insights into grain morphology and
fracture surfaces of ruptured samples from mechanical testing, while EDS analysis identified regions of
unmelted powder through elemental distribution. EBSD was used to quantify grain size and orientation,
critical for understanding the mechanical performance of the alloy. 3-point bend tests were used to
determine tensile yield strength (YS) of the samples.

 84

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Table 1: Process parameters and yield strength from 3-point bend test for samples 5, 6, 8, and 9.

 Samples LED (J/mm) Power (W) Velocity (mm/min) Yield strength (MPa)

 5           885.8                       375                  25.4                  327
 6           1181.1                      500                  25.4                  706
 8           644.2                       300                  27.9                  560
 9           644.2                       300                  27.9                  885

The change of process parameters directly influenced microstructural properties as can be seen in Figure
1 (a). The back scattering electron (BSE) images for samples 5, 6, 8, and 9 are shown here. Microstructural
morphology was found similar for sample 5 & 6, and sample 8 & 9. The longitudinal cross section of the
rods revealed that the center part of the rod has different morphology compared to the sides for both sample
5 & 6. For sample 8 & 9, morphology is similar throughout the samples. Additional EDS maps are included
in Figure 1 (b) & (c) for sample 5 and 8. Sample 5 has a large region in the middle with mostly unmelted W,
which is also depleted from Ti, Cr, and V. On the other hand, sample 8 (Figure 1 (c)) shows pockets of
round shaped unmeltted W, surrounded by enriched Ti, Cr, and V regions, a clear indication that only Ti,
Cr, and V are mixed in sample 8 and 9, while W was separated during fast cooling in LPBF AM process.

Figure 1: (a) BSE images are shown for samples 5, 6, 8, and 9; (b) shows EDS area maps for W, Ti, Cr,
and V for sample 5; (c) shows EDS area maps for W, Ti, Cr, and V for sample 8.

Different grain morphology is observed in different parts of sample 5. To further analyze it, additional EDS
are done in different locations, the results of which are shown in Figure 2(a) with an overlay of EDS maps.
Different locations within the samples are marked in white boxes and elemental compositions within these
boxes are presented in Figure 2(c). Within the center line of the sample, the location represented by box 1

 85

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

contains mostly enriched W and highly depleted Ti, Cr, and V. Within box 2 and 5, morphology is similar to
Sample 8 & 9 and has a composition close to target composition and overall average composition of sample
5. On the other hand, box 3 and 4 contain a darker morphology within the sample, EDS chemical
composition indicates depletion of W and enrichment of Ti, Cr, and V in these regions.

Figure 2: (a) shows EDS overlay map for sample 5; (b) shows the chemical composition of W, Ti, Cr, V for
sample 5; (c) shows how chemical composition is varied in different parts of the sample marked in (a).

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3: (a) SEM back-scattering images, (b) inverse pole figure (IPF) maps, and (c) Kernal average
misorientation (KAM) maps of samples 5, 6, 8 and 9.

Grain morphology was further scrutinized using SEM and EBSD analysis and the results are shown in
Figure 3, where BSE images, inverse pole figure (IPF) maps, and Kernel Average Misorientation (KAM)
maps are presented for sample 5, 6, 8, and 9. No particular grain orientation is observed for sample 8 and
9. However, along the center line of sample 5, a somewhat elongated morphology is observed. This
particular location is enriched in W and depleted in Ti, Cr, and V as we observed in Figure 2 (b). Grain size
analysis performed using EBSD data and processed through Oxford Aztec Crystal software provided the
equivalent grain diameter of 12.6 ± 12.73 µm, 14.58 ± 12.69 µm, 22.24 ± 12.16 µm, and 21.14 ± 11.58 µm
for Samples 5, 6, 8, and 9, respectively. From KAM maps, presence of different amounts of misorientation
and therefore, internal strains were observed in different parts of all four samples without showing any
specific trends and KAM distribution is largely nonuniform.
Mechanical data based on 3-point bend test are presented in Figure 4 (a) where engineering stress-strain
curve is shown for sample 5, 6, 8, and 9. All samples were tested at room temperature in the as-printed
state with no additional surface polish and notch. The associated fractured surfaces were analyzed using
SEM and are presented in Figure 4 (b), (c), (d), (e). Sample 9 showed the highest yield YS at 885 MPa.
However, unmelted powder throughout the cross section of sample 9 was observed as shown by Figure 4
(e). It is important to note that sample 8 and 9 have same LPBF parameters, but sample 8 fractured at
lower strain compared to sample 9. It is due to contamination during AM process, the surface of sample 8
showed signs of oxidation evident by darkening of the surface. For sample 9, oxidation on the surface was
reduced during AM process which resulted in higher YS compared to sample 8. The oxygen pickup during
AM process, therefore, remained to be a very important factor in determining the mechanical properties of
the builds. Sample 5 and 6 had regions with fully melted cross-section and fracture surface showed ductile
morphology evident from the dimpled surface (Figure 4 (b), (c)). The diameter for melt pool is ~600 µm for
sample 5 and 6 and for both samples the melted regions are characterized by dimpled surfaces. Note that
the 600 µm spot size is a machine constant in the AM device but the actual melt pool geometry varies
widely based on process parameters. High power makes the melt pool wider and deeper while faster
velocities make it shallower and more circular. These 1D rods are produced using one single laser scan
moving only along the Z-direction, without any rastering along the X and Y-directions and process

 87

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

parameters are controlled to produce rods with 3 mm diameter. For both samples 8 and 9, unmelted W was
observed even though sample 9 showed higher YS. Based on the results, the upper and lower bound of
LED is predicted for good fabricability and YS, which is defined to be 644 <LED<1181 J/mm. The process
parameters including power and scanning velocity should be controlled so that LED is within the defined
range for desired properties in the AM build samples.

Figure 4: (a) stress-strain curve from 3-point bend test for sample 5, 6, 8, and 9; fractography of broken
regions are shown in (b), (c), (d), and (e) for samples 5, 6, 8, and 9, respectively.

Additional work is ongoing for lamella preparation using focused ion beam and electropolishing to observe
these samples under transmission electron microscopy. Additionally, high throughput manufacturing is
ongoing to produce twenty-five compositionally different samples via directed energy deposition (DED)
additive-manufacturing based on down-selected compositions. In addition to that, plasma arc-casting is
also utilized to produce similar compositions which would help us to evaluate the effect of different
manufacturing techniques on these MPEAs.

4.10 RECENT EXPERIMENTS CONDUCTED WITH THE DIII-D DIVERTOR MATERIALS EVALUATION
SYSTEM (DIMES) AND MIDPLANE MATERIALS EVALUATION SYSTEM (MiMES) — Tyler Abrams,
Gregory Sinclair, Shawn Zamperini (General Atomics), Dmitry Rudakov (University of California San Diego),
Luca Cappelli (Oak Ridge Associated Universities), David Elder (University of Toronto)

The objective of this project is to use DiMES and MiMES systems on DIII-D to investigate material plasma
interaction, solid wall erosion, advanced plasma-facing component (PFC) materials, deuterium uptake and
particle transport for normal operation and transient events. In addition, the OEDGE, Flan, and GITR codes
are used for modeling DiMES and MiMES experiments and supporting corresponding plasma-materials
interactions (PMI) modeling on DIII-D.

SUMMARY
Significant progress in PMI research was accomplished using the DiMES and MiMES systems on DIII-D
during this reporting period. The program successfully executed four days of DiMES experiments in June
2025, exposing a total of 77 individual samples. These samples included novel materials from public users
and private fusion companies. Key advances were made in understanding deuterium retention in lithium
systems, tungsten transport modeling, and runaway electron impacts on plasma-facing components. The
Flan impurity transport code was substantially upgraded with a 100x performance improvement and new
kinetic collision models, enabling first-of-their-kind simulations of tungsten transport in negative triangularity
plasmas. Four peer-reviewed publications were produced, including articles in Nuclear Fusion, Plasma
Physics and Controlled Fusion, and Nuclear Materials and Energy, as well as invited and oral presentations
at major international conferences.

The spring 2025 DIII-D campaign featured extensive DiMES experimental activities under the Fusion Pilot
Plant (FPP) Materials Thrust. Four days of experiments were conducted June 9-12, 2025, including a
Director’s Reserve Industry Day that enabled three private fusion companies to test innovative materials in
the plasma environment. A total of 14 DiMES heads housing 77 individual samples were exposed,
representing both more mature refractory alloys and some diverse, novel concepts.
Notable experiments included the exposure of a boron pebble rod 1 cm in diameter, which demonstrated
the feasibility of testing three-dimensional pebble-based concepts in the tokamak divertor environment.
Another significant experiment involved ceramic samples supplied by Helion Energy, including angled and
flush samples of silicon carbide (SiC), boron carbide (B₄C), and silicon nitride (Si3N4). This experiment
marked a milestone as one of the first neutron-irradiated samples (SiC) to be tested in DiMES. While the
angled B₄C sample shattered from edge localized mode (ELM) impact, all other samples survived to varying
degrees, providing valuable data for private industry material down-selection efforts. All experiments
successfully achieved their stated goals in terms of exposure time and diagnostic characterization. The
DiMES system performed well throughout the campaign, with no evidence of leaks.
Significant progress was made in understanding deuterium retention mechanisms in lithium-coated plasma-
facing components (Figure 1). Analysis of DiMES experiments comparing pre-lithiated samples with lithium-
deuterium (Li-D) co-deposits revealed that below the lithium melting point, deuterium retention is primarily
driven by Li-D co-deposits rather than pre-deposited layers. Both pre-deposited and in-situ deposited lithium
exhibited lower erosion than predicted by sputtering models. These findings suggest that for achieving low
recycling regimes, in-operando lithium injection is more effective than pre-deposited films at temperatures
below the lithium melting point.

 89

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. (a) Measured deuterium areal density as a function of total Li areal density. (b) Deuterium areal
density as a function of Li areal density deposited during plasma exposure, with a least-squares fit to the
data overlaid.
Analysis of tungsten spectroscopic signals revealed a continuous rise in the WI line at 255.14 nm during
the plasma plateau in recent DiMES experiments. This observation prompted the design of startup
experiments to assess temperature effects on tungsten coating erosion and emissivity. A comprehensive
database of DiMES experiments, involving tungsten net erosion measured via Rutherford backscattering
spectrometry (RBS) from 2012 to 2024, is being compiled. The database reveals that in the absence of a
metal interlayer between the DiMES head and carbon coating, approximately 8 nm of W erosion is required
to achieve statistically significant results. Efforts are ongoing to correlate net erosion measurements (via
RBS) with gross erosion estimates (via spectroscopic diagnostics). A potential workflow is being evaluated
to estimate tungsten net erosion from gross erosion alone for impact energies above 300 eV, which would
remove reliance on WII and WIII measurements and simplify the diagnostic approach.
The Flan edge impurity transport code was completely rewritten from Python to C++ to increase
performance, resulting in over 100x speed improvement. Following the rewrite, a novel kinetic collision
model was derived and implemented. Random deflection angles are now calculated at every time step
based on the momentum slowing down time from collisions with background plasma species. The
anomalous transport coefficients extracted from Flan simulations continue to match previous experimental
data, representing a first for impurity transport codes as these coefficients are generally extracted from
experimental data. Flan was upgraded to track impurities in arbitrary geometries provided by Gkeyll and
applied to a negative triangularity inner wall limited DIII-D discharge. Post-processing tools are in
development to correlate tungsten influx into the core with turbulent plasma characteristics.
Results
An article involving modeling the amorphization and siliconization of silicon carbide [1] was published in
Nuclear Fusion. This paper describes simulation of a previous DiMES experiment coupling a semi-analytic
surface model to the impurity transport code GITR, indicating that crystalline SiC will undergo amorphization
under deuterium plasma contact with implications of higher sputtering and fuel retention.
A paper characterizing the thermo-mechanical response of a DiMES ATJ graphite sample intentionally hit
by a high current (600 kA) runaway electron beam [2] was published in Nuclear Fusion. The modeling was
performed with a novel workflow featuring the RE orbit code KORC, Monte Carlo particle transport code
Geant4, and finite element multi-physics software COMSOL. Different runaway electron impact
characteristics were probed with the best match to damage observations obtained with 3 MeV initial RE
energy and 25° pitch angle.
A paper describing measurement of runaway electron (RE) energies and pitch angles using DiMES [3] was
published in Plasma Physics and Controlled Fusion. Post-disruption runaway electron kinetic energies and
pitch angles were determined from an experiment where a domed DiMES sample protruding approximately
1 cm above the divertor floor was subjected to a controlled RE impact.

 90

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

The results described above involving pre-lithiated samples exposed on DiMES [4] were published in
Nuclear Materials and Energy. This article presents the first direct comparison between Li-D co-deposits
and pre-deposited lithium films, examining fuel retention dependency on lithium deposition method under
H-mode plasmas in the far scrape-off-layer.
DiMES team members attended the 20th Plasma Facing Components and Materials (PFMC-20) Conference
held in Ljubljana, Slovenia, May 19-23, 2025. Six abstracts including DiMES results were accepted,
including the invited talk "Molecular Dynamics Modeling and Experimental Assessment of Helium Bubble
Growth, Surface Morphology Evolution, and Displacement Damage Effects in Multi-Component Alloys" by
M.A. Cusentino et al. and the oral presentation "Overview of Advanced Plasma-Facing Materials Testing
for Fusion Pilot Plants at DIII-D" by J. Coburn et al. The other four contributions consisted of poster
presentations on various DiMES-related topics.

electrons in DIII-D, Nuclear Fusion 65 (2025) 024002.

final loss instability in DIII-D, Plasma Physics and Controlled Fusion 67 (2025) 035020.
[4] M. Morbey et al., Deuterium retention in pre-lithiated samples and Li-D co-deposits in the DIII-D tokamak,
Nuclear Materials and Energy 43 (2025) 101915.

4.11 RESEARCH TRAINING PATHWAY FOR UNDERREPRESENTED MINORITY STUDENTS IN
ADVANCING MATERIALS AND MANUFACTURING FOR FUSION POWER — V. K. Vasudevan, S. G.
Srivilliputhur, T. A. Scharf, N. B. Dahotre, S. Mukherjee, B. Rout, M. Baskes (University of North Texas); N.
Argibay, P. Singh (Ames National Lab); X-Y. Yu, Y. Katoh (ORNL); S. Jana (PNNL); J. Deshpande, L.
Garrison, B. Stephens (Advisors; Commonwealth Fusion Systems)

The major goals of the project are: 1) Develop, promote, and catalyze interdisciplinary research in advanced
materials and manufacturing relevant to fusion science and for careers in materials and manufacturing
science relevant to fusion power. 3) Create protocols technology. 2) Prepare a significant set of Ph.D. (4)
and BS/MS (9) grad-track women and underrepresented minority graduates for inclusion, equity, and
outreach to recruit and train transformative and skilled graduate and undergraduate students in
interdisciplinary areas. The hands-on research and training will broadly address open science questions in
the discovery, development, and manufacturing of refractory metal multi-material systems for applications
under extreme conditions faced in fusion power systems. The research focuses mainly on discovery of new
tungsten-based refractory alloys with enhanced mechanical (strength, toughness) and thermomechanical
(creep, fatigue) properties and radiation damage resistance through computationally guided alloy design
combined with novel processing routes to produce the most desirable microstructure.

SUMMARY
Six PhD students, Maheshwari Meesa, Siva Shankar Alla, Blake Emad, Cristian Urias, Katie Estrada, and
Carson Hester and 3 BS/GradTrack students, Ella Viray, Matthew Schroeder, and Mariah Preston worked
on the project. They worked on different research projects. Maheshwari worked on design of tungsten-
based multi-principal-element alloys and alloying effects on short range and radiation resistance via first
principles calculations. Siva investigated thermomechanical processing via rolling of tungsten without
cracking and studied the effect of hot, cold, and low temperature rolling on the microstructure and
mechanical properties (strength, toughness, BDTT) of tungsten. Blake worked on alloying of tungsten with
titanium and the effects of processing and heat treatments on the microstructure and mechanical properties
of the W-Ti alloys. Cristian worked on spark plasma sintering of tungsten-titanium-chromium carbide,
tungsten-titanium carbide and Tungsten-YSZ dispersion strengthened alloys. Katie worked on laser
assisted additive manufacturing of tungsten and tungsten-rhenium alloy and the use of a scratch-based
technique to determine the fracture toughness of these alloys. Carson conducted studies of heavy ion
irradiation of pure tungsten and characterization of changes in mechanical properties via nanoindentation.
Ella conducted a project on spark plasma sintering and characterization of refractory NbTaMoTiZrAl
(Kustalloy) high entropy alloy. Matthew and Mariah worked on powder synthesis and spark plasma sintering
of oxide (YSZ, CeO2), carbide (TiC, ZrC) and MAX phase (Ti3AlC) dispersion strengthened tungsten alloys.
The results of the above projects are described in the following.

Short Range Order and Electronic Structure Control of Radiation Resistance in Tungsten-Based
Multi-Principal-Element Alloys-–M. Meesa, P. Sharma, N. Argibay, V. K. Vasudevan, P. Singh, and S.
G. Srivilliputhur
Tungsten based multi component alloys are the primary candidates for the plasma facing materials in fusion
applications while its limited ductility and radiation tolerance continue to constrain its use, underscoring the
need to understand how local chemical and structural order governs defect energetics and lattice response.
Here, we employ first-principles density-functional theory (DFT) to elucidate the role of short-range order
(SRO) as a design variable linking chemistry, bonding, and mechanical compliance in W-Ta-Cr-V multi-
principal-element alloys (MPEAs). Our calculations show that SRO strengthens local charge localization
and redistributes vacancy energetics, simultaneously elevating formation energies and selectively
modifying migration barriers through element-specific bonding asymmetry. Overall, these results reveal
SRO as an effective tool for tuning defect-mediated transportation, phonon response, and ductility in
refractory MPEAs, and offer critical guidance to identify next-generation tungsten alloys for fusion
applications.

 92

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Computational Methods
The CALPHAD calculations are done using the TCHEA6 databases specifically developed for refractory
MPEAs, available with the ThermoCalc software [1]. Our calculations were performed using the one-axis
model at 1 atmosphere and 300 K to 1600 K. The first-principles DFT, as implemented in the Vienna Ab-
initio Simulation Package (VASP), was utilized [2] to study the influence of vacancies on the structural and
electronic properties of W-Ta-Cr-V MPEAs. For the Brillouin zone integration of the BCC structures, a
gamma-centered Monkhorst-Pack k-mesh of 3×3×3 was employed during geometry optimization. A denser
5×5×5 mesh was used for self-consistent charge calculations [3]. A plane-wave energy cutoff of 520 eV
was applied consistently for both total energy (10-5 eV/cell) and force (-0.001 eV/Å) convergence
calculations. The Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) exchange-correlation functional, appropriate for solids,
was used [4,5]. We used the Super-Cell Random Approximates (SCRAPs) approach to determine vacancy
 𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝 𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝
formation energy (𝐸𝐸𝑓𝑓 ) that is independent of unit cell size in our alloys [6]. The 𝐸𝐸𝑓𝑓 values for the pure
elemental constituents (W, Ta, Cr, and V) were computed using 54-atom BCC supercells. The vacancy
formation energy was estimated using the relation
 𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝
 𝐸𝐸𝑓𝑓 = 𝐸𝐸𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝 − 𝐸𝐸0 ± 𝜇𝜇𝑖𝑖 Eq. (1).
where Epd and E0 are respectively the energies of the MPEA supercell with and without a point defect, 𝜇𝜇𝑖𝑖 is
the chemical potential of the species i associated with the point defect. The positive sign in Eq. (1).
corresponds to vacancy formation (atom removal) and the negative sign corresponds to interstitial formation
(atom addition) [7]. The Vacancy migration energy which is the minimum energy path between initial and
final atomic configurations of point defects, and the corresponding migration energy barriers, were
determined using the climbing image nudged elastic band (cNEB) method. In this study, five intermediate
images were employed to construct the migration trajectory. The total energies associated with each image
were computed to generate the energy profile along the defect migration path.
Results and Discussion:
Two types of systems one with the disordered and the other with the SRO structure for alloy compositions
W30 (W30Ta41Cr17V12), W40 (W38Ta36Cr15V11) and W50 (W50Ta29Cr12V9) were chosen to study the influence
of SRO on properties such as ductility and radiation resistance. The previous report discusses how these
alloys were considered mechanically stable based on Born’s stability criteria[8] and how the ductility of
these alloys decreases with increased W % in disordered systems while it increases and then decreases
in the systems with SRO with increased W % establishing that W40 with SRO has the optimum ductility
based on the ductility metrics such as the Pugh’s ratio and the Cauchy’s pressure[9].
The present report extends the discussion to the (1) thermal and dynamic stability of these alloys to
understand their phases stability and ductility based on phonon frequency calculations. (2) Effect of SRO
on radiation resistance is explained in detail by the Vacancy formation and the Vacancy migration energies
of the four elemental vacancies in these alloys.
Thermal, Mechanical and Dynamic stability:
The CALPHAD-predicted Gibbs free-energy landscapes of the MPEA alloys provide a good, quantitative
picture of the impact of composition and temperature[10] on phase equilibria and stable phases. Based on
the free energy calculations, the results of the predicted phases and their volume fractions with temperature
over the range of 500K (227°C) to 1500K (1227°C) are displayed in Fig. 1 for the three MPEAs W30
(W30Ta41Cr17V12), W40 (W38Ta36Cr15V11) and W50 (W50Ta29Cr12V9). As can be seen, in the alloy with lower
tungsten concentration (30 at.%W) a three-phase equilibrium of BCC, 𝜇𝜇 and C15 Laves phase are predicted
over the 500-1500K range. Using 1200 K (the black line in the Fig. 1) for comparison, the BCC solid-solution
is the major phase for W30Ta41Cr17V12 alloy and produces equilibrium volume fraction of 7.2 % Laves
and 15.8 % 𝜇𝜇-phases, which are in excellent agreement with our thermodynamic-kinetic predictions. The
C15 Laves phase forms principally from the Cr and Ta in the alloy. With an increase of W to 40 at.%, the
C15 Laves phase disappears. The BCC phase and the 𝜇𝜇-phase remain stable, the latter with volume
fraction of 23 %. Analytically, this crossover occurs when 𝜕𝜕2(∆𝐺𝐺𝐺𝐺𝐺𝐺𝐺𝐺)𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕2>0, where in the incremental
enthalpic stabilization provided by an additional W atom outweighs the entropic penalty driving BCC mixing
[11]. These CALPHAD results underscore the following critical points, i.e., (a) an increasing W content
deepens the BCC free-energy well, (b) elimination of the C15 Laves phase in the W40 [12] alloy, and (c)

 93

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

big reduction in the μ-phase formation in the W50 alloy. In particular, the W38Ta36Cr15V11 composition retains
a robust single-phase BCC field to high temperature while minimizing deleterious intermetallic phases,
which makes it a prime candidate for structural application in fusion-power systems. We also assessed the
formation enthalpy (Eform) of Wx(Ta58Cr24V18)1-x MPEAs relative to their most stable pure elemental phases
(i.e., W, Ta, Cr, V, all in BCC phase) using DFT for x=0, 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60 at.%. The results shown in
Figure 2 are consistent with the stability trends obtained in the CALPHAD analysis and establish the thermal
stability of the alloys considered in this study.

Figure 1. Thermodynamic phase stability of (a) W30 (W30Ta41Cr17V12), (b) W40 (W38Ta36Cr15V11), and (c)
W50 (W50Ta29Cr12V9) MPEAs evaluated via CALPHAD modeling, which shows volume fractions of
constituent phases between 300-1600 K. The overall trend suggests that increasing W content stabilizes

Figure 2. DFT calculated formation enthalpy of our Wx(Ta58Cr24V18)1-x MPEAs with increasing percent
tungsten.
Phonon analysis
The phonon dispersion plots in Fig. 3 indicate that all six W-rich disordered and SRO BCC alloys (W30,
W40, W50) are dynamically stable, as shown by the absence of imaginary frequencies [13]. The lattice
dynamics of these MPEAs systematically vary with both composition and chemical ordering. Specifically,
as W concentration decreases, the bands in both the disordered and SRO structures become flatter, which
suggests a softening of the alloys and increased ductility. A comparison at each composition further shows
that introducing SRO softens the bands, particularly in the W40 composition, which correlates with trends
seen in the Pugh’s ratio and Cauchy’s pressure. This effect indicates a softening of interatomic force
constants and a concurrent reduction in bonding covalency [14,15] The introduction of SRO further lowers
the transverse-acoustic mode (TA) frequencies by an additional ~40% at each composition, which
corresponds to a decrease in the effective force constant (𝛥𝛥𝛥𝛥/𝐾𝐾 ≃ 2𝛥𝛥𝛥𝛥/𝜔𝜔) [16].Analytically, the slope of
the acoustic branches near 𝛤𝛤 (𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕/𝜕𝜕𝜕𝜕) is proportional to the long-wavelength sound velocity 𝑣𝑣𝑠𝑠 , which is

 94

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 (𝐶𝐶 −𝐶𝐶12 )
calculated as � 11 . From the phonon dispersions, we extracted the longitudinal sound velocity for
 𝜌𝜌
several compositions. In the disordered alloys, vs decreases from 4723.03m/s in W50 to 4360.92 m/s in
W30 and drops further to 4675.30m/s in SRO W50 alloy, which translates to a shift in the Debye temperature
(qD). qD drops from 310.1K in disordered W50 to 301.7 K in SRO-W50 and further down to 289.9 K in SRO-
W30. According to the semi-empirical relation by Leibfried and Schlömann [17], a lower Debye temperature
correlates with reduced Peierls stress and enhanced plastic deformability. This trend is consistent with the
observed increase in the Pugh’s ratio and Cauchy pressure at lower W content.

Figure 3. Phonon dispersion curves of W-Ta-Cr-V MPEAs for disordered and short-range ordered (SRO)
configurations at varying compositions, i.e., W30 (a), W40 (b), and W50 (c). A progressive softening of
phonons is observed from W50 to W30, which is a reflected in the flattening of acoustic branches. This
phonon softening correlates with enhanced atomic mobility and reduced lattice stiffness (or, increased
ductility).

Vacancy formation energies (VFE)
Figure 4 shows the EfVac for Cr, Ta, V, and W in our disordered and SRO MPEAs (W30, W40, and W50)
[18]. Each data point represents an average over three configurations, and the error bars quantify the
variation in the energies due to local chemical environments. We considered three types of chemical
environments with configurations 1 & 2 having non-tungsten elements as nearest neighbors and
configuration 3 with tungsten atoms as predominant nearest neighbors.

Figure 4. Variation in average vacancy formation energy (𝐸𝐸𝑓𝑓𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉 ) for W, Ta, Cr, and V in W-Ta-Cr-V.
Disordered and short-range ordered (SRO) configurations are compared for W30 (a, d), W40 (b, e), and
W50 (c, f) alloys. The influence of local chemical environment and degree of SRO on 𝐸𝐸𝑓𝑓𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉 across atomic
sites are seen.
The composition of the alloy significantly influences vacancy formation energy (VFE), particularly for W [17].
Specifically, as W content increases, the VFE for W also increases, while the VFE of Cr, Ta, and V remains

 95

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

largely unchanged. This suggests that higher W content increases the energy costs for creating vacancies
within the tungsten lattice, a stabilizing effect that prevents lattice disruption. Our analysis provides the
following three key quantitative insights: (i) Cr vacancies are the easiest to form, and W vacancies the
hardest across all tested configurations, (ii) SRO systematically increases VFE and reduces the spread of
configurational variance [19,20], and (iii) the overall alloy composition, especially higher W content,
enhances the resistance to vacancy formation. These findings are critical for understanding and controlling
defect-related phenomena, such as diffusion, creep and radiation damage, in W-based alloys.
Vacancy Migration Energies
The vacancy migration behavior in the three W-rich MPEAs shown in Fig. 5 (W30Ta41Cr17V12 (a, d);
W38Ta36Cr15V11 (b, e); W50Ta29Cr12V9(c, f)) was quantified by comparing the peak migration barrier (Eₘmᵃˣ)
for each atomic species in both the fully disordered and the SRO configurations. The migration energy
increases for certain elements like W and decreases for certain other like Cr. These findings across all three
compositions demonstrate how SRO alters migration barriers (and pathways) by influencing local charge
distribution and atom clustering.

Figure 5. Energy barriers for vacancy migration in disordered (top) and short-range ordered (SRO) (bottom)
for three representative compositions in W30 (a, d), W40 (b, e), and W50 (c, f) alloys are shown. SRO
configurations exhibit notable modulation of migration pathways compared to their chemically disordered
counterparts, with local atomic environment effects becoming increasingly pronounced at higher solute
content.
Notably, in the disordered state, the ratio Emmax /EfVac ranges from 0.21 (Ta in W40) to 1.26 (Cr in W30),
whereas with SRO the ratio reduces to the range of 0.26-1.15 (Table S1), signifying that SRO both stiffens
some local bonds while simultaneously opening migration channels for other elements [20].
From the standpoint of phase stability and defect kinetics, the observed ±0.2 to ± 3.2 eV shifts in Eₘ in
SRO alloys will translate into 101 to 1016-fold changes in diffusion coefficients at 1000 K (D ∝ exp(-𝐸𝐸𝑚𝑚 /𝑘𝑘𝐵𝐵 T))
[21,22]. Coupled with the 10-12% increase in EfVac these electronic redistributions in SRO alloys can
dramatically slow some point defect processes (e.g., Ta migration in W30 and W40) while accelerating
others (e.g., W self-diffusion in W30). This provides us with a powerful principle to tune high-temperature
creep, radiation resistance, and overall phase robustness in W-based MPEAs.
Future Work
Among the 2000 compositions that were theoretically screened and the 15 compositions selected from the
pool, DFT calculations were performed to calculate the ductility metrics. The improvement in ductility in the
presence of the elements in the following order V > Zr > Ti was used as validation for consistency between

 96

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

the Theory and the DFT calculations as described in the previous report. In the future, the two compositions
with the highest and the lowest ductility are checked for computational consistency and experiments are
planned to make these two alloys to validate the trend observed in the DFT calculations.
Effects of K-Doping and Thermomechanical Processing on Fracture Toughness of Tungsten–S. S.
Alla, B. Emad, C. Hester, X-Y. Yu, L. Garrison, V. K. Vasudevan, and S. Mukherjee
Tungsten is a promising candidate for first-wall components in fusion power plants due to its exceptional
high-temperature properties and low tritium retention. However, its low fracture toughness and high strain
rate sensitivity (SRS) at low temperatures pose major limitations for structural reliability. To improve fracture
resistance and SRS in tungsten, the effects of hot rolling and potassium (K) doping were investigated by
comparing the scratch-based fracture toughness of as-received sintered unalloyed tungsten, hot-rolled
unalloyed tungsten, and K-doped hot-rolled tungsten. The hot-rolled unalloyed tungsten and K-doped
tungsten showed significant grain refinement and increased density of low angle grain boundaries. Scratch-
based fracture toughness measurements showed that hot-rolled pure tungsten had ~ 20% higher and K-
doped tungsten had ~ 68% higher toughness compared to the as-received condition. The scratch groove
profiles showed brittle fracture in the as received tungsten in contrast to quasi-ductile response in the K-
doped rolled tungsten. These findings highlight the role of thermomechanical processing and doping in
enhancing tungsten’s microstructural stability, strength, and toughness.
Materials and Methods
Scratch based fracture toughness tests were conducted using a RTEC Universal Tribometer equipped with
a Rockwell type C sphero-conical diamond stylus (200 μm tip radius, 60° cone angle). A constant normal
load of 100 N was applied, determined through preliminary depth calibration tests to produce stable fracture
features. The scratch tests were performed at a constant transverse speed of 0.01 mm/s over a 3 mm
scratch length. This setup ensured sufficient data resolution while minimizing thermal or rate-dependent
effects. Each test was repeated three times to ensure reproducibility and to get statistical variation. Post
scratch characterization was carried out using a confocal laser scanning microscope to obtain three
dimensional topographical profiles of the scratch tracks. Confocal laser scanning microscope were used to
capture representative cross-sections profile along the YZ plane, following procedure described in previous
studies [23-25]. These profiles were analyzed to extract the groove perimeter (P) and the projected contact
area (AC). The transverse force (FT) was measured along with the applied normal force (FN) using a biaxial
load cell. These values were used to estimate fracture toughness (KC) following the method developed by
Akono et al.[26].
Results and Discussion
Figure 6 summarizes the microstructural results for as-received tungsten, hot-rolled tungsten, and
potassium-doped hot-rolled tungsten. The as-received sample [Figure 6(a, d)] shows coarse, equiaxed
grains with random orientations, characteristic of a fully recrystallized state. In contrast, the hot-rolled
tungsten [Figure 6(b, e)] exhibits uniformly elongated, refined grains, indicating grain subdivision and partial
recrystallization. The K-doped hot-rolled tungsten [Figure 6(c, f)] reveals even finer, fibrous grains. The fine,
elongated grain structure suggests that recrystallization was suppressed [27,28]. The grain boundary (GB)
maps [Figure 6(g–i)] show differences in the distribution of high-angle grain boundaries (15-650) and low-
angle grain boundaries (<150) with rolling and doping. As-received tungsten [Figure 6(g)] is dominated by
high-angle grain boundaries (HAGBs), with only 4.4% low-angle boundaries (LAGBs). In contrast, hot-rolled
tungsten [Figure 6(h)] shows a substantial increase in LAGBs to 64.7%, indicating the formation of sub
grain features with deformation. The K-doped tungsten [Figure 6(i)] shows a further increase to 69.4%. This
increase in LAGBs suggests that potassium suppresses recrystallization by inhibiting grain boundary
migration through boundary pinning. Similar results have been reported in previous studies [27,29], where
doping elements were shown to suppress recrystallization by limiting grain boundary mobility. The local
average misorientation (LAM) maps [Figure 6(j–l)] reveal differences in stored strain across the samples.
The as-received tungsten [Figure 6(j)] shows minimal misorientation, confirming a strain-free condition. The
60% hot-rolled tungsten [Figure 6(k)] exhibits moderate misorientation, indicating partial strain
accumulation due to deformation. In contrast, the K-doped hot-rolled sample [Figure 6(l)] shows high values
of local misorientation, suggesting increased dislocation density and restricted grain boundary mobility.
These findings align with previous studies [28], which show that potassium doping inhibits dislocation
annihilation during annealing and helps retain internal strain.

 97

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 6. Microstructural characterization of as-received, hot-rolled, and K-doped hot-rolled tungsten
samples. (a–c) Backscattered electron (BSE) images; (d–f) inverse pole figure (IPF) orientation maps; (g–
i) grain boundary maps and (j–l) local average misorientation
Akono’s scratch-based fracture toughness model has been modified to include indenter geometry effects
and applied force [30-32]. These revised methods have been validated across a range of materials [31]
However, this study uses Akono’s original method to simplify the analysis and focus on bulk material
behavior. For tungsten and its alloys, fracture toughness (KC) data remain limited in the literature. The
inherently brittle nature of these materials makes conventional fracture testing difficult [8], emphasizing the
need for high-throughput approaches. In our calculations, pile-up was included to avoid underestimating
groove dimensions, which could otherwise lead to overestimating toughness. While not all pile-up
contributes to fracture resistance, including it provides a consistent basis for comparison. Figure 7(a-l)
shows the 3D profiles, SEM images of the scratch groove for the as-received, hot-rolled, and K-doped hot-
rolled tungsten samples. The as-received tungsten (Figure 7a) exhibits deep and irregular scratch groove
with asymmetric pile-up and localized regions of high material removal, indicating severe penetration and
poor resistance to fracture. The corresponding SEM images (Figures 7b-d) show deep and sharp cracks
as well as visible chipping along the edge of the scratch groove, indicating brittle fracture. This brittle
behavior is often observed in coarse-grained tungsten, where limited dislocation mobility at room
temperature promotes cleavage fracture [33,34]. In contrast, the hot-rolled tungsten (Figure 7e) shows a
smoother and more continuous scratch path with symmetric pile-up and reduced groove depth. SEM
images of the hot-rolled tungsten (Figures 7e-h) shows a uniform groove, with fewer cracks and less surface
damage. These features reflect improved fracture resistance, likely due to grain elongation and increased
dislocation density introduced during rolling. Similar morphology changes have been reported in rolled
tungsten, where elongated grains help deflect cracks and reduce fracture severity [35,36]. The K-doped
hot-rolled tungsten (Figure 7i) shows the most uniform and shallow groove among the three, with minimal
surface damage. The K-doped hot-rolled tungsten (Figures 7j–l) shows the most uniform scratch path, with
a shallow groove and isolated micro-cracks. This morphology reflects a quasi-ductile response, enabled by
the fine, fibrous grain structure stabilized through potassium-induced grain boundary pinning. The reduced
cracking suggests enhanced resistance to brittle fracture, in agreement with previous studies showing that
K-doped tungsten exhibits improved ductility and lower BDTT due to grain refinement and substructure
stabilization that suppress dislocation motion and grain boundary migration [37]. As shown in Figure 7(m),
both rolling, and K-doping improve fracture toughness (KC). Hot rolling increases KC by ~ 20%, while
potassium-doping combined with hot rolling results in a ~ 68% increase over as-received tungsten. In

 98

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

addition, Figure 7(m) shows the relationship between grain size and fracture toughness, where smaller
grains correspond to higher toughness. The fit show an R² value of 0.99, indicating a Hall–Petch type of
relationship between toughness and grain size. This trend suggests that grain refinement improves fracture
resistance by limiting cleavage fracture and promoting local plasticity near the crack tip.

Figure 7. 3D surface profiles of scratch groove, SEM images of scratch grooves in as-received (a–d), hot-
rolled (e–h), and K-doped hot-rolled (i–l) tungsten. (m) Scratch-based fracture toughness plotted against
the inverse square root of grain size, demonstrating Hall-Petch behavior.
The grain size dependence of fracture toughness observed in this study is consistent with previous reports.
For example, Reiser et al. [38] attributed this to both dislocation motion and crack propagation. Similarly,
Ahadi et al. [39] reported a comparable trend in NiTi alloys, where toughness improved with decreasing
grain size. Comparison of fracture toughness values from this study with literature reports is not
straightforward due to differences in testing methods. While this work utilizes a scratch-based approach
developed by Akono et al. [26] to estimate fracture toughness (KC), many previous studies report values in
terms of conditional stress intensity factors (KQ), maximum stress intensity values (Kmax), or J-integral-
based estimates. These values are strongly influenced by specimen geometry, loading conditions, crack
length, and the degree of plastic deformation, which makes direct comparison difficult. In this study, scratch-
based fracture toughness (KC) was determined for the as-received pure tungsten (~25 MPa√m), hot-rolled
tungsten (~30 MPa√m), and potassium-doped hot-rolled tungsten (~42 MPa√m). To validate this approach,
a 316 stainless steel sample was tested under identical conditions, yielding a KC of approximately
40 MPa√m. This value agrees with previous reports [40] for steels tested using the same scratch-based
method and is included in the supplementary section. Our results are in good agreement with previous
studies on processed tungsten. Lied et al. [41] reported KQ values ranging from 20 to 40 MPa√m for cold-
rolled and potassium-doped tungsten sheets. The improved fracture resistance was attributed to
microstructural refinement, including grain size reduction, increased grain boundary density, and high-angle
boundaries. These features promote crack deflection, reduce crack tip driving force, and dissipate energy
during fracture. In potassium-doped tungsten, potassium pinning further stabilizes the refined
microstructure by inhibiting grain growth during thermomechanical processing. Firneis et al. (2022) [42]
reported Kmax values as high as 60 MPa√m in ultrafine-grained tungsten foils by in-situ tensile tests. The
high apparent toughness was attributed to delamination toughening, which introduces energy-dissipating
interfaces, and R-curve behavior, where fracture resistance increases with crack extension due to

 99

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

progressive microstructural engagement. Although the absolute values vary due to methodology
differences, the trend is consistent that thermomechanical processing and dopant-assisted grain
stabilization play a critical role in enhancing the fracture toughness of tungsten. The scratch-based method
used in this work provides a reliable and efficient way to evaluate fracture resistance in small or brittle
specimens, making it suitable for screening and comparing tungsten-based materials.
In conclusion, EBSD analysis revealed progressive grain refinement and an increase in low-angle boundary
fraction and dislocation density in the hot-rolled and K-doped hot-rolled tungsten. Elevated local
misorientation, particularly in the K-doped condition, suggested high dislocation density and effective
suppression of recrystallization, contributing to microstructural stability. Scratch testing showed that hot
rolling improved fracture toughness by approximately 20%, while potassium doping led to a ~68% increase
compared to the as-received sample. These improvements are attributed to enhanced grain boundary
density and refined sub-grains and high density of mixed-character dislocation microstructure, which
promote dislocation glide, crack deflection, and suppress cleavage fracture. SEM and optical microscopy
of scratch grooves revealed a transition from brittle fracture in the as-received tungsten to quasi-ductile
behavior in the K-doped sample. Smoother grooves with fewer surface cracks were observed in the
thermomechanically processed samples, reflecting improved energy absorption and fracture resistance.
Microstructure and Properties of Tungsten-Titanium Alloys–B. Emad, S. S. Alla, C. Hester, X-Y. Yu,
V. K. Vasudevan, and S. Mukherjee
The development of plasma-facing materials (PFMs) is critical for future fusion reactors such as the ITER
and DEMO, which operate under extreme thermal, irradiation, and plasma conditions [43,44]. Tungsten (W)
is a leading candidate due to its high melting point, strength, and low sputtering, but its brittleness and
irradiation sensitivity limit its use [44-46]. Alloying with rhenium (Re) has been shown to improve ductility
but leads to irradiation-induced embrittlement causing structural instability [44,47,48]. Titanium (Ti) has
emerged as a promising alternative due to its low activation, cost effectiveness, and ability to enhance
sintering and mechanical behavior [45,49-51]. However, forming a homogenous W-Ti solid solution remains
challenging due to limited solubility, diffusion barriers, and phase separation during processing [51,52].
In this study, we investigated the microstructure and mechanical properties of two commercially available
W-Ti alloys, 95W-5Ti (5 wt% Ti, 95 wt% W) and 90W-10Ti (10 wt% Ti, 90 wt% W), in both as-received (AR)
and hot isostatic press heat-treated (HIP) conditions. Scanning electron microscopy (SEM), electron
backscatter diffraction (EBSD), energy-dispersive spectroscopy (EDS), and X-ray diffraction (XRD) were
used to characterize microstructural evolution, while mechanical behavior was evaluated through
compression, profilometry-based indentation plastometry (PIP), and scratch testing. Electrical resistivity
measurements were conducted to estimate thermal conductivity.
Previously, we reported that in the as-received (AR) condition, both alloys exhibited a heterogeneous two-
phase microstructure with distinct W-rich grains and Ti-rich regions, reflecting incomplete diffusion during
sintering. The 90W–10Ti alloy contained a much higher fraction of the Ti-rich phase (~0.50) compared to
95W–5Ti (~0.20), and both showed relatively small, uneven grain sizes typical of dual-phase systems. After
HIP heat treatment, the microstructure became more homogeneous, with grains coarsening and size
distributions becoming more uniform. The Ti-rich phase was largely eliminated, dropping to ~0.05 and
remaining only as nanoscale features, while Ti dissolved into the W matrix to form a BCC solid solution.
This transformation is confirmed by diffraction techniques and lead to solid solution strengthening through
lattice distortion and reduced dislocation mobility. As a result, HIP-treated samples showed significantly
improved high-temperature performance, retaining about 50% of their strength at 700 °C compared to only
~23% for AR samples.
In addition to prior mechanical testing, scratch testing was employed to calculate fracture toughness.
Fracture behavior is also seen to improve significantly after heat treatment. Scratch testing (Figure 8)
shows that AR samples exhibit deep grooves, cracking, and brittle fracture features due to microstructural
heterogeneity. HIP-treated samples, however, display smoother grooves and reduced damage, indicating
improved fracture resistance. Quantitatively, fracture toughness increases from ~34 to 39 MPa√m for 95W-
5Ti and from ~36 to 56 MPa√m for 90W-10Ti. The largest improvement (~56%) occurs in the higher Ti alloy
after HIP processing, highlighting the combined benefits of increased Ti content and solid solution
formation. The mechanical properties are summarized in Table 1.

 100

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 8. (a – m) Secondary electron (ETD) images of samples after scratch testing.

Table 1: Summary of mechanical properties from mini-compression testing, scratch testing, and Vickers
hardness testing.

Thermal conductivity was then calculated by 4-point point electrical resistivity testing. Electrical resistivity
measurements (Figure 9) reveal additional effects of microstructural evolution. In the AR condition,
resistivity values are relatively low (~10–11 × 10⁻⁸ Ω·m) because current primarily flows through continuous
tungsten pathways, with Ti-rich regions acting as minor scattering sites. After HIP treatment, resistivity
increases significantly (up to ~22.7 × 10⁻⁸ Ω·m for 90W-10Ti) due to enhanced electron scattering from Ti
atoms in the solid solution. This behavior follows Matthiessen’s rule, where solute atoms act as point defects
disrupting electron flow [53,54]. As a result, thermal conductivity decreases after heat treatment, dropping
from ~90 W/m·K in the AR condition to 67 W/m·K for 95W-5Ti HIP and 42 W/m·K for 90W-10Ti HIP. This
inverse relationship reflects the Wiedemann–Franz law, where increased electrical resistivity corresponds
to reduced thermal conductivity. The observed reduction exceeds predictions from simple rule-of-mixtures
models, emphasizing the strong scattering effects of titanium in the tungsten lattice [55-58].

 101

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 9. (a) shows electrical resistivity of W-Ti alloys as a function of at% titanium composition with pure
tungsten and pure titanium indicated by star symbols. (b) shows calculated thermal conductivity as a
function of at% titanium composition with pure tungsten and pure titanium indicated by star symbols.

In summary our experiments how that HIP heat treatment transforms W–Ti alloys from heterogeneous,
phase-separated systems into near-homogeneous solid solutions. This transition leads to increased yield
strength, improved high-temperature stability, enhanced fracture toughness, and reduced thermal
conductivity. Higher titanium content further amplifies these effects, demonstrating the critical role of solid
solution strengthening in optimizing the mechanical performance of tungsten-based alloys.
Spark Plasma Sintering of Oxide and Carbide Dispersion Strengthened Tungsten–C. Urias, M.
Schroeder, E. Viray, M. Preston, T. Ingale, H. Huang, N. Argibay, R. Banerjee, V. K. Vasudevan, and
T. A. Scharf
In this work, a Tungsten alloy was developed using a combination of solid solution and dispersion phase
strengthening, through the addition of Chromium (Cr), Yttria-Stabilized Zirconia (YSZ), and Titanium
Carbide (TiC). Traditionally, W-alloys are fabricated via powder metallurgy techniques like hot pressing
and/or hot isostatic pressing, which requires high temperatures and long dwell times. These methods often
result in undesirable grain coarsening and high impurity pickup. To mitigate these effects, Spark Plasma
Sintering (SPS) was utilized. By applying uniaxial pressure and a directional current, SPS enables rapid
consolidation at lower temperatures and shorter dwell times, promoting the production of near-net-shape-
plasma-facing components for Tokamak fusion reactors.
The mechanical properties of the W-Cr(ODS) alloy were determined via scratch-based techniques as
reported in [26,59-61]. Specifically, scratch testing was employed to determine fracture toughness (Kc) and
scratch hardness (Hs). While scratch testing is efficient, accurately measuring the scratch depth remains a
challenge due to material specific work hardening exponents and the resulting pile-up of load bearing
material. To further optimize performance, oxygen impurities were refined via annealing with Yttrium
shavings. Building on the work from Ames National Laboratory with RHEA’s, this process utilizes Yttrium’s
high oxygen affinity to act as a reducing agent to enhance ductility of these alloys.

The work hardening exponent was determined for each material via three indentations. The dwell condition
consisted of a normal force of 100N for 60s, followed by a displacement spacing of 1mm. Based on the
work of [62], the work hardening exponent relates indentation pile up height and depth via equation (2) from
Figure 10.
The work hardening exponent n was used to apply a correction factor to the measured depth. This
correction was applied to equation (2), where hc represents the corrected height. This was done to account

 102

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

for scattering events at the peak depths of the wear track from a laser based profilometer, Keyence VK-
x4000 3D, to accurately account for load bearing pile up.

Figure 10. Radial Indent profile for work-hardening exponent of WCr-AR.

 ℎ
                                                          ℎ𝑐𝑐 = (1 + )ℎ𝑚𝑚                                      (2)
 𝑑𝑑

Equation 2: Correcting height equations from measuring the pile up height (h) to its depth (d) to the nominal
surface and use to apply against the measure depth of the scratch (dm)
A speed of 0.01mm/s was chosen to represent the speed of an ASTM E399 chevron notch beam test. From
the Keyence the files were analyzed using gwyddion to process depth vs. position data (Figure 11). From
the MFT-5000 frictional force data, Figure 11 shows that the initial movement of the tip loads in a static
friction like condition, after the tip exceeds this, it undergoes dynamic friction. This is reflected in the depth
data, where no large changes is observed in the Fx graph and depth analysis. For the measurement of
fracture toughness and scratch hardness, an average was found after the transition from static to dynamic
friction, at which point the normal force and its average were recorded and its deviations. Subsequently,
the equations for circular segments with hc were used to find a range of data points, including perimeter,
area, width, and theta angle at each depth position.
The annealing for impurity removal was performed by encasing the material in a Tantalum canister, where
a bed of Yttrium (Y) shavings was laid beneath the sample and backfilled with Helium. The temperature for
these anneals was set as high as possible to provide the oxygen impurities with sufficient thermal energy
to drive the formation of Y2O3. The heating/cooling rate was approximately 3°C/min to a final dwell
temperature of 1400°C for 48 hours. Following this, the removal and separation of the shavings was
performed using 8M nitric acid, and samples were polished before the combustion-based analysis of LECO
ON. This step was necessary for any alloys containing Chromium, as a eutectic reaction caused the Yttrium
to melt and coat the sample as a liquid before resolidifying upon cooling. Figure 4

profile of scratch, red lines indicate where the depth vs postional data is used. (Right)
Results
The results in Figure 12 reveal that coupons made by high energy ball milling +SPS of pure W have a
higher Kc due to grain refinement, increase in hardness as well as higher density. The results from the
scratch-based techniques compare equally with reports in the literature [63]. With the introduction of Cr into
the system, compared to the pure W system at similar percent plastic strain a near doubling is achieved
but the AR and HEBM now flip. Likely from the phase separation of α(Cr,W) from the parent α(W,Cr) as the
AR, dominated by the α(W,Cr) solid solution strengthened phase. With the α(Cr,W) being a softer phase
allowing for easier dislocation formation. The introduction of YSZ as oxide dispersion strengthening
particles to WCr was effective in closing alligator like separation seen in the wake of the crack in WCr.

 104

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 12. Scratch fracture toughness of W/Cr/YSZ through a series of monoload scratch conditions.
Lastly the annealing of refractory-based alloys with yttrium chips has proven to be successful (Figure 13).
The effects of annealing of pure W-HEBM were not as large as that of WCrYSZ-HEBM. This difference is
thought to be likely from the initial starting condition of not having as large of a concentration of impurities
in the former or because of the partial melting caused by eutectic reaction between Cr and Y in the latter.

Figure 13. The before and after of the Y-annealing of pure W-HEBM (Left). The before and after of the Y-
annealing of W10Cr0.5TiC-HEBM (Right)
In summary, Tungsten alloys strengthened by chromium in solid solution and YSZ/TiC dispersion phases
were fabricated using spark plasma sintering (SPS), promoting rapid consolidation with reduced grain

 105

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

growth and impurity uptake compared to conventional powder metallurgy methods. Mechanical properties,
including fracture toughness and scratch hardness, were evaluated using scratch-based testing with
corrections applied for material-dependent pile-up through the work-hardening exponent. High energy ball
milling improved toughness and hardness in pre tungsten due to grain refinement, Chromium addition
altered phase behavior, influencing deformation mechanisms, while YSZ dispersion enhanced
microstructural stability through Zener pinning. Yttrium-assisted annealing demonstrated effective oxygen
reduction, particularly in chromium-containing alloys. These results support SPS processing and scratch-
based methods as viable approaches for optimizing tungsten alloys for plasma-facing fusion applications.
Ongoing work involves characterization of the changes in mechanical properties and microstructure (grain
size, dispersoid size, structure, composition, and distribution) as well as the production of mini tensile
specimens for ductility measurements as the current method is to use scratch-based compression analysis
which can lead to crack closing.
Ion Irradiation Response of Tungsten and the Effects of Thermomechanical Processing–C. Hester,
S. S. Alla, X-Y. Yu, B. Rout, V. K. Vasudevan, and S. Mukherjee
The main objective of this work is to develop a fundamental mechanistic understanding of the effects of
alloying, doping and thermomechanical processing on improving the mechanical properties and irradiation
tolerance of tungsten. Figure 14 shows the difference between the non-TMP and TMP tungsten samples
tested.
Ni ion irradiation of TMP and Non-TMP W was performed at 500 °C to simulate the operating environment
for PFCs as well as to promote defect diffusion and recombination. The effect of thermomechanical
processing and irradiation on the microstructure and mechanical behavior were characterized. Detailed
analysis of “pop-in” behavior and elastic to plastic transition measured by nanoindentation was used to
extract the activation volume for insights into how irradiation and thermomechanical processing alter the
mechanisms governing incipient plasticity in unalloyed tungsten. Upon Ni ion irradiation, the non-TMP
sample exhibited an irradiation hardening of 11%, whereas the TMP sample showed no significant change
in hardness. TMP-W showed a decrease in activation volume for plastic events from 4.3 Å³ for unirradiated
condition to 3.8 Å³ for irradiated sample, with no significant change in the stress required to initiate plasticity.
The non-TMP W sample on the other hand showed a reduction in both activation volume from 4.9 to 3.8 Å³
and average shear stress for plasticity by ~ 10% following irradiation.
Pure W was purchased from AEM Metal (Changsha, China) and sectioned into smaller pieces via wire
Electro-Discharge Machining. Samples were TMP’d at 1250°C by incrementally rolling in steps of 2% of
their initial thickness until a final reduction of 60% was achieved. Figure 14a shows a diagram
demonstrating the direction in which irradiation and indentation were performed relative to the rolling
orientation of the TMP-W. To simulate the effects of irradiation damage, heavy ion-irradiation was conducted
using Ni+ accelerated using 5 MeV at 500°C. A peak of 50 dpa was obtained from an ion fluence of 8.34E15
ions/cm2. Heavy ions were used to ensure a sufficiently high dpa was achieved during irradiation to simulate
the effects of extreme radiation damage. All irradiation for this study was conducted at the University of
North Texas’s Ion Beam Laboratory. Figure 14b shows the expected distribution of DPA and Ni throughout
the material calculated with Eqns. 3 and 4 using values from Stopping Range of Ions in Matter (SRIM). The
damage model employed was the full cascade model using the default values of lattice, binding, and surface
energies of W.

                                  𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷 =                                                               (3)
 𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐷

 𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴/𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐3
 𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴/𝑐𝑐𝑐𝑐2

Samples were polished using SiC pads up to 800 grit. They were then polished with 3 and 1 μm diamond
suspension and placed into a vibratory polisher filled with 0.02 μm colloidal silica for 24 hours resulting in
a surface similar to electropolishing, which is the standard employed in nanoindentation studies. All samples

 106

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

were prepped in this manner for irradiation and nano-indentation experiments, no additional polishing
followed the irradiation process before mechanical testing.
Nano-indentation tests were carried out using a TI-Premier Ⅱ with a three-plate capacitive 1D low load,
and high load transducer (Bruker, MN, USA), using a Berkovich tip. Incipient plasticity indents were
performed to a maximum load of 1000 µN at a loading rate of 25 µN-s. Indentation size effect studies were
performed using varied peak loads from 0.01 – 1.00 N. All indentation experiments followed the same
loading profile of 2 second load, 5 second dwell, and 2 second unload. Indentation studies had a minimum
indent population of 100 and were spaced by 10 and 50 μm for the incipient plasticity and indentation size
effect study respectively to avoid the plastic deformation region of previous indents. Reduced modulus of
the tested material was found using the Oliver-Pharr method. The unloading curves were fitted to determine
the reduced modulus inside the TI-Premier II’s software. Load-displacement curves were fitted with the
Hertzian elastic response to calculate when the indentation deviated from elastic response and determine
critical shear stress as well as the nature of the plastic event. In some cases, the load-displacement curves
showed abnormal artifacts consistent with poor contact or an unclean surface therefore these data points
were excluded from the results. It should be noted that these curves only made up a minority of the data
points and all data sets had at least 80 out of 100 good load-displacement curves. SEM imaging was
captured on the FEI Nova NanoSEM 230 fitted with a TSL Digiview III Electron Backscatter Diffraction
(EBSD) detector using OIMTM software (Hillsboro, Oregon).

Figure 14. a) Diagram of experiment conditions showing the orientation of the ion beam and indentation
with respect to the microstructure of the TMP-W, B) Damage curve of Ni+ in W generated by SRIM 2008.
Comparing the IPF images in Figure 15 of the a) non-TMP and b) TMP tungsten reveals a distinct change
in the grain morphology going from equiaxed to elongated pancake-like grains in the latter. The increase in
dislocation density can be seen in the ECCI images in Figures 15c non-TMP and 15d TMP tungsten, in
addition the distribution of dislocations differs between the samples as well, with non-TMP materials having
a majority of dislocations near the grain boundary while in the TMP sample the distribution is a lot more
even throughout the grain. Figures 15 e,f show the grain size and grain misorientation angle distribution
respectively for the TMP and non-TMP tungsten, The grain refinement is clearly seen between the two
graphs showing that upon TMP of the sample the average grain size reduces by ~30% and increase in
number of Low Angle Grain Boundaries (LAGB) by nearly five times. These microstructural changes are
important as in previous studies these features have shown to improve irradiation tolerance by offering
more sites for defect annihilation [64-68].

Fang et al [69] for the ratio of hardness after irradiation divided by the hardness before irradiation as a
function of depth throughout the material. This is a way to visualize the change in dislocation density after
irradiation, showing that the non-TMP tungsten has a larger increase in post-irradiation due to less grain
boundaries, LAGB, and a smaller dislocation density.

Figures 17 a,b show examples of how the plastic events were categorized, a) shows a distinct horizontal
section in the load displacement curve indictive of a dislocation nucleation events referred to as a pop-in,

and 11% for TMP tungsten, and the shear stress required for plasticity decrease upon irradiation for the
non-TMP tungsten but remains the same for the TMP material.

The major conclusions drawn from the current data is that upon TMP of tungsten the irradiation tolerance
increases significantly due to the improvement of the microstructure and dislocation density as seen in
Figure 15, resulting in more sites for defect annihilation resulting in a hardness increase of 1.6% after
irradiation as compared to 11%. In addition, the incipient plasticity study reveals that the activation volume
decrease is only half as much and no change is seen in the shear stress of the plastic events recorded after
irradiation for the TMP tungsten against the non-TMP condition.
The same studies will be performed on several different samples to compare the individual processing
routes including, warm-rolled tungsten, W-10wt%Ti, W-3Re, W-3Re-K, and W-K.
Microstructure and Mechanical Properties of Laser- and Electron Beam-Assisted Additively
Manufactured Tungsten and Tungsten-Rhenium Alloys—K. A. Estrada, K. K. Verma, S. S. Sharma,
X-Y. Yu, N. Argibay, V. K. Vasudevan, and N. B. Dahotre
Tungsten (W) and tungsten-rhenium (W-Re) alloys are promising candidates for extreme environment
applications due to their exceptional properties, such as resistance to extreme temperatures and neutron
irradiation damage. However, their intrinsically high melting and ductile-to-brittle transition temperatures
(DBTT) pose significant challenges to processing. This study investigates the fracture toughness of laser
powder bed fusion additively manufactured W-5 wt.% Re using a surface-based scratch technique, a
method that enables the use of small volumes and is tolerant to periodic print defects, like voids. W-Re
samples were additively fabricated with input laser fluences of 12.7 J/mm2 and 28.6 J/mm2, and their
microstructure, hardness, and fracture toughness were compared to commercial W-5Re sheet.
Microstructural analysis revealed that increasing the input laser fluence resulted in larger, irregular grains
and a higher density of cracks along high-angle grain boundaries. Scratch testing was performed using a
Rockwell C indenter tip, and fracture toughness (Kc) was calculated based on the lateral force, perimeter,
and load-bearing contact area of the scratch grooves. The commercial W-5Re sample exhibited the highest
Kc value, while the LPBF sample processed with the higher input laser fluence showed the lowest Kc.
Scanning electron microscopy of the scratch tracks revealed distinct periodic microcracks, with finer cracks
in the commercial sample and wider, more concentrated cracks in the additively fabricated samples. These

 110

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

findings highlight the utility of scratch-based methods as a rapid way to screen mechanical properties and
accelerate parameter tuning for process optimization. A paper based on these results was published in the
IJRMHM [23].
The present work builds upon prior efforts aimed at optimizing processing parameters to achieve dense,
crack-free tungsten-rhenium builds. In this study, the focus was on understanding texture evolution as a
function of process parameter variation. Previous studies have demonstrated that surface degradation in
tungsten, in both single-crystal and polycrystalline forms, exhibits strong orientation dependence [70-72].
Motivated by these findings, the relationship between microstructure and mechanical behavior is examined
to identify crystallographic orientations that offer improved damage resistance. Leveraging the inherent
capability of additive manufacturing to produce textured builds, this work explores the potential to tailor
microstructure during fabrication to enhance the performance of plasma-facing components [73].
A commercially pure tungsten plate produced via powder metallurgy was used to establish a baseline for
comparison with additively manufactured builds. For both L-PBF and EB-PBF, a parametric study was
conducted to identify the beam power that yielded the highest-quality builds, while all other processing
parameters were held constant to reduce variable effects.
The commercially produced tungsten plate exhibits equiaxed grains with mixed crystallographic
orientations, as shown in Figure 18a. The corresponding kernel average misorientation (KAM) and grain
boundary character maps (Figure 18b–d) indicate that the material is well stress-relieved and predominantly
composed of high-angle grain boundaries (HAGBs).

In contrast, the additively manufactured samples from both laser powder bed fusion (L-PBF) and electron
beam powder bed fusion (EB-PBF) display columnar grain structures, as shown in Figure 19. Despite this
columnar morphology, the AM samples still exhibit a distribution of crystallographic orientations. Figure 19A
presents inverse pole figure (IPF) and KAM maps for the L-PBF-fabricated samples, revealing a non-

 111

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

uniform grain morphology characteristic of the process. The corresponding KAM maps for these conditions
suggest a reduced dominance of HAGBs compared to the commercially produced plate.
A challenge arises when acquiring EBSD maps of EB-PBF-produced specimens. Due to the higher
processing temperatures relative to L-PBF, EB-PBF promotes the formation of pronounced subgrain
structures, which are not well resolved in conventional KAM maps. Figure 19B includes (a) a representative
low-magnification IPF map, (b) an IPF map acquired at a higher magnification and overlaid with image
quality (IQ), (c) a misorientation profile corresponding to the arrow annotated in (b), and (d) a KAM map
highlighting the limitations in capturing these substructures. Ongoing work is focused on improving data
acquisition and processing methods to obtain high-quality EBSD maps suitable for detailed interpretation.

Bulk properties were assessed to establish a foundation for comparing processing–structure relationships
across material conditions. The surface-based scratch method used to evaluate fracture toughness was
discussed in detail in a previous report. Figure 20 highlights the advantage of this approach, particularly in
cases where conventional V-notch specimens cannot be fabricated. Figure 20a shows a defect-free
surface, consistent with the commercially produced tungsten plate. In contrast, Figures 20b and 20c reveal
pronounced crack networks and voids in the L-PBF samples. A significant improvement in surface quality
is observed in Figures 20d and 20e, corresponding to the EB-PBF-fabricated samples, where no cracks
are evident and porosity is substantially reduced. The fracture toughness results obtained from scratch
testing for each sample are summarized in Figure 21.
Finally, to establish a clear process–property relationship between processing parameters and texture
evolution, it is essential to understand the underlying mechanisms and fundamental material behavior.
Accordingly, significant effort is dedicated to microscale testing and characterization of each sample.
Current work focuses on sample preparation and the acquisition of high-quality, high-magnification
micrographs, including backscattered electron (BSE), secondary electron (SE), electron channeling
contrast imaging (ECCI), and electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD). Nanoindentation will complement
these efforts, providing localized mechanical property measurements to support the microstructural
analysis. Results will be systematically collected and reported prior to implementing a new set of processing
parameters.

Results
In this project, investigations were conducted on important areas pertaining to the behavior of W-based
plasma materials as described above, including first principles modeling and simulations,
thermomechanical processing, K-doping, alloying effects, oxide and carbide dispersion strengthened
materials, irradiation behavior and additive manufacturing. Future work will expand on these efforts toward
the broader goal of achieving fundamental new insight for designing and developing new materials with
improved properties and methods to fabricate them.
