4.12 SUPPRESSION OF IRRADIATION HARDENING IN TUNGSTEN-COATED FERRITIC STEEL FOR
FUSION REACTOR BLANKET APPLICATIONS — Kotaro Seki, Kenta Yoshida, Hotaka Miyata, Akira
Hasegawa, Sosuke Kondo, Hao Yu, Yasuyuki Ogino, Ryuta Kasada (Tohoku University), Kazuyuki
Hokamoto, Shigeru Tanaka (Kumamoto University), Takaaki Koyanagi (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Abstract of a manuscript in Journal of Nuclear Materials (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jnucmat.2025.155657)

The manuscript was listed among the most-downloaded articles on the Journal of Nuclear Materials
website as of June 2, 2025.

W-coated reduced activation ferritic steels have been developed for use as plasma facing components in
fusion reactor blankets, offering excellent sputtering resistance and structural strength. Previous high-
temperature coating methods, such as diffusion bonding and brazing, caused interfacial deterioration due
to thermal stress from mismatched thermal expansion between W and reduced activation ferritic steel. To
address this, underwater explosive welding was introduced as a high-velocity cold process that joins
dissimilar materials while maintaining a strong, thin interface without the thermal issues associated with
traditional methods. In this study, the effects of neutron irradiation on the hardness and microstructure in
W-coated F82H reduced activation ferritic steel (W/F82H) joined by underwater explosive welding are
investigated. Following neutron irradiation at 290 °C, irradiation hardening is suppressed in W, F82H, and
their interface within the W/F82H material. Furthermore, microstructural observations indicate that the
recovery of work hardening and relaxation of elastic strain introduced during coating significantly contribute
to the suppression of irradiation hardening in W/F82H. In conclusion, W/F82H exhibits significantly
suppressed irradiation hardening compared with those in standalone materials. This suppression is
explained by residual stress from thermal expansion mismatch and the unique microstructure at the
interface. These results provide valuable insights for the development of more durable materials in nuclear
fusion applications.
This study was supported by the US–Japan FRONTIER collaboration.

 118

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

4.13 BENCHMARK THE OXIDATION KINETIC BEHAVIOR OF PRISTINE TUNGSTEN UNDER FUSION
OFF-NORMAL SCENARIOS — A. Jeyaveerasigamani, R. Sainju, Y. Zhu (University of Connecticut)

Tungsten (W) is currently the primary candidate and baseline material for use in fusion plasma-facing
components (PFCs). However, W is susceptible to oxidation during various fusion reactor off-normal
scenarios, including combined loss-of-coolant accident (LOCA) and loss-of-vacuum accident (LOVA)
events, as well as waste handling and maintenance conditions. Understanding the oxidation behavior of W
is important for fusion reactor safety and sustainability. The objective of this work is to establish a
benchmark reference dataset for the oxidation kinetic behavior of pristine W using W coupon preparation
procedures and a thermogravimetric analysis (TGA) oxidation workflow tailored to fusion plasma-facing
material (PFM) applications.

SUMMARY
TGA has been widely used to study the oxidation kinetics and mechanisms of bulk metals, including
refractory W. However, much of the historical TGA oxidation kinetic data for W reported in the literature was
published before the 1960s and often lacked clear information on coupon surface quality, experimental
procedures, or oxidation conditions relevant to fusion scenarios. In this work, an upgraded TGA system and
optimized experimental protocol were established for reproducible W oxidation kinetic testing relevant to
fusion PFM applications. Using this capability, the oxidation behavior of pristine W in dry synthetic air was
evaluated over temperatures from 400 °C to 900 °C and oxidation durations up to 18 h. These benchmark
oxidation kinetic data establish a baseline for future evaluation of radiation- and humidity-induced effects
on W oxidation, and for comparison with the oxidation resistance of advanced W-based materials.

Figure 1a shows the upgraded TGA system, based on a NETZSCH STA 449 F3 Jupiter Simultaneous
Thermal Analysis unit, used for this W oxidation study. In particular, as shown in Figure 1a, three major
instrumentation upgrades were implemented, including a high-speed platinum furnace, a hangdown carrier,
and a turbo pump assembly. The high-speed furnace provided a heating rate up to 1000 K/min. Compared

Figure 1. Upgraded TGA system and optimized experimental protocol for W oxidation kinetic testing. (a)
Overview of the TGA system with upgraded instrumentation, including a high-speed furnace, a hangdown
carrier, and a turbo pump assembly. (b) Temperature calibration and oxidation test procedures optimized
for this upgraded TGA setup to ensure reproducible kinetic measurements.
with conventional furnaces operating up to 50 K/min, this allowed the TGA kinetic test to reach the target
isothermal oxidation temperature much faster, thereby reducing oxidation during uncontrolled heating at
lower temperatures. The hangdown carrier was used to suspend a W coupon, allowing all exposed coupon
surfaces to interact more uniformly with the flowing gas during oxidation. The turbo pump assembly

 119

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

established a clean base vacuum on the order of 10-5 mbar, which helped mitigate the impact of residual
humidity in the furnace on W oxidation.
Besides these hardware upgrades, an optimized experimental protocol was established for this upgraded
TGA setup, as summarized in Figure 1b. This protocol included both temperature calibration and oxidation
test procedures designed to ensure reproducible kinetic measurements. The temperature calibration
utilized the Curie standard method, which relies on the magnetic transition of ferromagnetic materials rather
than conventional melting-point standards. In addition, the oxidation test protocol was optimized through
extensive experimentation, including testing various combinations of heating rate, furnace- and direct-
sample temperature control, flushing temperature and duration, and cooling procedure. To correct for
buoyancy effects, mass-gain measurements on reference samples (platinum and alumina) with the same
volume as the W coupons were performed before and after the oxidation tests as correction runs.
Furthermore, the W TGA coupon preparation procedure was standardized to further improve the reliability
and reproducibility of benchmark oxidation measurements. As shown in Figure 2a, conventional metal-
cutting methods such as slow-saw and water-jet cutting induced delamination in the W coupon, especially
near the surface, resulting in artificially high oxidation rates due to interlayer oxidation. Some of these
cutting methods are adopted by W suppliers. After screening several vendors, high-purity W sheet (99.97
wt%, PLANSEE) was selected as the pristine W material, and wire electrical discharge machining (EDM)
was employed to produce 10 mm × 10 mm × 1 mm coupons. As shown in Figure 2b, the EDM-cut coupons
subsequently underwent a mechanical polishing sequence using the MULTIPREP SYSTEM – 8” (Allied
High Tech Products Inc.), involving SiC paper (180 grit), Dia-Grid diamond disc (40 μm), and diamond
lapping films from 15 μm to 1 μm. A final polishing step using 0.05 μm colloidal silica was performed to
achieve an EBSD-quality W surface identical to that used for ion irradiation, thereby making the coupons
directly suitable for later radiation-impact studies.

Figure 2. Improved preparation of W TGA coupons for reproducible bulk W benchmark oxidation
measurements. (a) EDM cutting to produce uniform W surfaces for benchmark oxidation testing. (b)
Mechanical polishing to achieve an EBSD-quality W surface identical to that used for ion irradiation
experiments.
Figure 3 presents the oxidation kinetic data of pristine W measured using the above TGA setup for
temperatures from 400 °C to 900 °C in dry synthetic air, with oxidation durations ranging from 0.5 to 18 h.
Comparable mass-gain kinetic curves were observed for tests performed at the same temperature and
using W coupons with the same surface condition, confirming the reliability and reproducibility of the
established TGA workflow. The close-up inset in Figure 3 further showcases the effects of W surface
conditions on oxidation behavior at 625 °C. W surface conditions ranging from as-received to controlled
surface finish were tested, and the more divergent kinetic curves at 625 °C clearly demonstrated the need
to standardize the W coupon preparation procedure.

 120

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. Oxidation kinetic data of pristine W in dry synthetic air for temperatures from 400 °C to 900 °C.
The mass-gain kinetic curves in the close-up inset showcase the effects of W surface conditions on
oxidation behavior at 625 °C.

RESULTS
A TGA capability for benchmark W oxidation kinetic testing has been established, evaluated, and optimized
for pristine W under fusion-relevant off-normal scenarios. The resulting benchmark oxidation kinetic dataset
will be used in future mechanistic studies of orientation-dependent W oxidation and in studies aimed at
understanding how fusion-relevant radiation damage in tungsten affects oxidation kinetics.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

4.14 NANOINDENTATION AND MICRO TENSILE TESTS ON TUNGSTEN HEAVY ALLOYS FOR
FUSION APPLICATIONS — Peter Hosemann (University of California, Berkeley)

Tungsten is a leading candidate for a fusion first wall due to its high melting point, thermal conductivity,
strength, and low thermal expansion coefficient. However, pure W possesses a low ductile to brittle
transition temperature (DBTT) and low fracture toughness, both of which are worsened by neutron
irradiation [1]. In contrast, the samples tested here, liquid phase sintered (LPS) tungsten heavy alloys
(WHA), provide good thermal conductivity and mechanical properties while being easier to manufacture.
Though there are concerns with radioactivity from the Ni ductile phase, keeping nickel content below ~4%
meets the criteria for Class C waste according to the NRC [2]. For this study, we find the mechanical
properties of alloys with 90, 92.5, 95, and 97.5 W wt% content, with varying levels of Ni and Fe content.
These properties bring insight into the viability of these alloys for implementation in fusion reactors. Work
is still ongoing, but it has so far been found that elastic moduli remain around expected values of 900MPa,
with future work focused on quantifying the grain interface strength via micro tensile tests under SEM.

Four pieces with varying W contents were cut into small slices. One slice of each sample was dedicated to
nanoindentation after mounting in a polyfast puck and polishing down to 6um with diamond suspension.
These were then inserted into the Ti-990 Tribointenter, where a 13x13 hardness map was generated in a
region of interested consistent of both W grains and NiFe interface material. Figure 1 displays an example
hardness map of the 90W-7Ni-3Fe sample.

Figure 1. Hardness map on 90W-7Ni-3Fe sample. Hardness is in GPa, and 3μm–spaced indents
The sample identities are in Table 1.

 122

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Table 1. Sample Descriptions

In addition, SEM EDS scans of the samples along with micro tensile tests of laser-cut tensile bars of 80μm
widths were performed to quantify the grain-interface strength. Future work involved performing these tests
within the SEM, such that greater magnification and failure analysis is possible.
Results
Currently, data is available for all four sample nanoindentation grids, while EDS and tensile tests have been
performed on sample 1. Two grids were made on each end of the sample to account for any differences
throughout the material. Nanoindentation data for samples 2-4 can be seen here, while Figure 1 shows the
results for the first sample’s top grid. All grids were made with 3μm-spaced indents on the Ti-990
Triboindenter, and all hardness units are in GPa. For some of the measurements, outlier measurements
were removed and replaced with NaN.

Figure 3. Sample 2 top nanoindentation grid

From these grids, it is possible to observe a hardness gradient as the indents move farther away from the
binder and into the bulk grain. A more comprehensive analysis of hardness vs. distance from grain boundary
will be done in future work to understand the magnitude of this gradient. In addition to these hardness maps,
an EDS scan on sample 1’s top nanoindentation grid was performed.

Figure 9. EDS scan of the sample 1 top nanoindentation grid.

From this scan, we can observe a seemingly uniform distribution of binder elements within the tungsten
grains rather than a decline in concentration as one moves from the binder into the bulk of the grain.
Next, continuing with sample 1 analysis, microtensile tests were performed on femtosecond-laser-cut
tensile bars with a neck of approximately 80μm wide and 250-350μm thick. Images and data on 4 tensile
tests for sample 1 are displayed below. Since the original tester has a small mechanical issue with the strain
sensor, reporting a small plateau during the elastic region, then returning to normal, the strain is correct via

 125

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

the MATLAB program TensileProcess. [3]. Because of this, the elastic region has a slope of roughly 1/100
of the actual slope. In the future, DIC will be used to precisely measure strain throughout the experiment.

Figure 10. Bar 7 being pulled in the tensile test. Neck widths are 80μm

Figure 11. Tensile test data for sample 1. Calculated with an 80μm neck, data corrected via [3]
Despite the data correction in the elastic region, all tests showed similar UTS of between 950 and 1000MPa.
Conclusion
In this report, four samples were prepped and nanoindented to quantify hardness gradients between the
NiFe binder and W grains. Sample 1 displayed the highest hardness gradient, likely due to a greater depth
of binder than in higher W-content samples, which may have only a thin layer of binder on top of W grains.
Tensile tests demonstrated similar UTS values to those in the literature [4]

Much is left to be done on this project. For one, data analysis of hardness vs. distance from the grain
boundary, along with EDS line scans of these indents, would be useful for determining which material
compositions correspond to the hardness measurements. In addition, tensile tests under SEM, both at
heated and room temperature, would be useful for determining the interface strength between the binder
and grain while observing the failure mechanism. Work will continue throughout the semester on performing
tensile tests and EDS scans on all samples.

4.15 SIMULTANEOUS EXPOSURE TO NEUTRON RADIATION AND HYDROGEN ENVIRONMENT:
EFFECT ON HYDROGEN RETENTION — Weicheng Zhong, Takaaki Koyanagi, Adrian M. Schrell, Yutai
Katoh (ORNL), Yuji Hatano (Tohoku University).
Abstract: Full manuscript accepted in International Journal of Hydrogen Energy

SUMMARY
This study demonstrates a novel methodology that simultaneously exposes materials to both neutron
irradiation and a hydrogen environment, enabling study on in pile interaction between radiation-induced
defects and hydrogen in tungsten. The hydrogen environment was established by releasing hydrogen from
vanadium hydride within an irradiation capsule. The hydrogen pressure during irradiation was estimated as
14.2 Torr. Post-irradiation thermal desorption spectroscopy analysis showed increased hydrogen retention
in irradiated tungsten compared to unirradiated samples. Two dominant desorption peaks at ~470 ˚C and
~700 ˚C were observed in irradiated and unirradiated tungsten samples, suggesting similar trapping
mechanisms between the two samples. However, an additional desorption peak at ~800 ˚C in irradiated
tungsten from the hydride capsule suggests an interplay between irradiation induced defects and hydrogen
exposure, leading to the formation of additional hydrogen trapping sites. These findings provide critical
insights into hydrogen retention in tungsten under fusion-relevant conditions and stimulate future studies to
investigate the synergistic effects of neutron irradiation and hydrogen exposure.

Figure 1. The irradiation effect on short term (31.5 hours) hydrogen retention in tungsten at ~300-400 ˚C.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 5. VANADIUM ALLOYS AND DEVELOPMENT

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

5.1 USING APT TO UNDERSTAND DYNAMIC PRECIPITATION IN TiC NANOPRECIPITATE
STRENGTHENED V ALLOYS AT ELEVATED TEMPERATURE — Ying Yang, QQ Ren, Jack Carpenter,
Yan-Ru Lin, Xiang Chen, Yanli Wang and TS Byun (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Understand the mechanism responsible for the sustained work hardening of TiC nanoprecipitate-
strengthened V-4Cr-4Ti-0.1C (V44C) at elevated temperature, with emphasis on the role of dynamic
precipitation during deformation.

SUMMARY
V44C shows unusually strong work hardening at elevated temperature, which helps preserve both strength
and ductility. To understand this response, atom probe tomography (APT) was used to compare the
precipitation aged condition with the tab and gauge sections of high-temperature tensile specimens,
together with a higher-temperature solutionized condition. The deformed gauge section contains a much
higher number density of TiC nanoprecipitates than the undeformed tab section, showing that deformation
promotes additional precipitation rather than only coarsening pre-existing particles. Nearest-neighbor
analyses further suggest that carbon redistributes and clusters first, followed by Ti partitioning and
subsequent TiC growth. These results support a dynamic precipitation mechanism that continuously
introduces new nanoscale barriers to dislocation motion and extends work hardening in V44C.

Strong and sustained work hardening was observed in V-4Cr-4Ti-0.1C alloys at elevated temperature, a
behavior that contributes to the unusual combination of high strength and useful ductility in V44C. To identify
the governing mechanism, APT datasets were collected from the solutionized state, the tab section of the
700°C/30 min tensile specimen, the corresponding deformed gauge section, and the 800°C/60 min aged
condition.

Figure 1. Engineering stress-strain curves of V44C tensile tested at 700°C compared to that of V44 show
stronger and sustainable work hardening that simultaneously improve the strength and ductility of V44C at
elevated temperatures.
This comparison separates thermal exposure from the additional effects introduced by plastic strain. The
results consistently indicate that deformation accelerates nanoscale clustering and increases the density
of TiC-related features, providing direct microstructural evidence for dynamic precipitation in V44C.

 130

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

APT analyses were performed on four key states: the solutionized condition at 1350°C/15 min, the tab
section aged at 700°C/30 min, the deformed gauge section of the same tensile specimen, and the 800°C/60
min aged condition. The tab section serves as a thermally exposed but largely undeformed reference,
whereas the gauge section captures the combined effects of thermal exposure and plastic deformation.
Nearest-neighbor distance analyses were also conducted for the 1st through 4th nearest neighbors to
quantify short-range clustering and early precipitate evolution.
Results
The results are presented in two parts. Figure 2 compares the APT reconstructions of the solutionized,
aged, and deformed conditions, and Figure 3 quantifies clustering behavior through nearest-neighbor
statistics.

Figure 2. APT reconstructions of (a) the solutionized state at 1350°C/15 min, (b) the solution-plus-age
condition at 1350°C/15 min + 700°C/30 min, corresponding to the tab section of the tensile specimen, and
(c) the deformed state of the same solution-plus-age condition, corresponding to the gauge section of the
tensile specimen. The gauge section shows a markedly higher number density of nanoscale precipitate
features after deformation, consistent with deformation-assisted or dynamic precipitation during elevated-
temperature straining.
Figure 2 shows that the microstructure evolves significantly during deformation. In the solutionized state,
only limited clustering is apparent. After 700°C/30 min thermal exposure in the tab section, discrete Ti- and

 131

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

C-enriched features appear, indicating the onset of precipitation. The largest change occurs in the gauge
section, where the precipitate density is substantially higher than in the tab despite the same nominal
thermal history. This difference indicates that applied strain accelerates precipitation kinetics or stimulates
additional nucleation.
The increased population of nanoscale precipitates in the gauge section provides a direct microstructural
basis for the sustained work hardening of V44C during high-temperature deformation.

Figure 3. Nearest-neighbor distance distributions of C atoms (left column) and Ti atoms (right column)
measured by APT under different heat-treatment and deformation conditions. Dashed lines represent the
distributions expected for a random atomic arrangement, while solid lines represent the experimentally
measured distributions. Overlap between the dashed and solid curves indicates that the atoms remain
randomly distributed, with little evidence of clustering or redistribution. In contrast, separation between the
solid and dashed curves indicates atomic redistribution and clustering. The stronger and earlier deviation

 132

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

observed for C compared with Ti suggests that C is more mobile and clusters first, whereas Ti redistribution
occurs later and is associated with subsequent TiC precipitation and coarsening.
Figure 3 compares the nearest-neighbor distance distributions obtained from APT with the corresponding
distributions expected for a random solid solution. In this figure, the dashed curves represent the theoretical
random distribution, whereas the solid curves represent the experimentally measured distributions after
heat treatment and deformation. The left column shows the behavior of C atoms, and the right column
shows the behavior of Ti atoms under the different thermal and mechanical conditions. When the solid and
dashed curves overlap, the corresponding atoms remain close to a random distribution, indicating little or
no clustering or redistribution. In contrast, a clear deviation between the solid and dashed curves indicates
redistribution and clustering. The results show that C exhibits earlier and stronger deviation from the random
distribution than Ti, indicating that C atoms redistribute and cluster first. Ti also deviates from the random
distribution, but the effect is generally weaker at the early stage, suggesting that Ti redistribution lags that
of C. These observations support a dynamic precipitation mechanism in which rapid C migration initiates
local enrichment around Ti, followed by subsequent Ti partitioning and TiC precipitation/coarsening during
elevated-temperature exposure and deformation.

Future work will examine the TEM image and perform MD simulation to understand the interaction between
dislocation and particles. The APT analysis will be applied to other TiC precipitate strengthened V alloys. In
situ TEM under mechanical testing will be used to connect precipitate evolution with work-hardening rate
and ductility.

 133

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

5.2 COMPUTATIONAL THERMODYNAMICS AIDED DESIGN ON ADVANCED V ALLOYS — Ying Yang,
QQ Ren, Jack Carpenter, Yan-Ru Lin, Xiang Chen and Yanli Wang (Oak Ridge National Laboratory), and
Steve Zinkle (University of Tennessee-Knoxville)

Develop advanced vanadium alloys with improved high-temperature strength and creep resistance while
maintaining acceptable tensile ductility and processability. The present effort uses computational
thermodynamics to guide alloy chemistry and phase stability, with emphasis on identifying compositions
that can form beneficial nanoprecipitates or optimized solid-solution strengthening states for service at
elevated temperature.

SUMMARY
Computational thermodynamics was used to guide the design of advanced V alloys by linking composition
selection with phase stability, anticipated precipitate evolution, and practical processing windows. A
CALPHAD-based database for the V-Cr-Ti-Y-O-C-N system was established and applied to compare
candidate compositions against the reference V44 alloy. The calculations indicate that carbon-containing
compositions including V44C and V6Cx can promote thermodynamically stable TiC nanoprecipitates over
a broad temperature range, whereas the reference alloy V44 and the newly designed V4T alloy remain
primarily solid-solution strengthened. Based on these predictions, new alloys were manufactured and
evaluated through tensile testing and step-load creep testing was also applied to V44C. The current results
show that the newly designed alloys, particularly V44C and related V6C variants, provide a clear
improvement in high-temperature strength over V44. The creep results further suggest a meaningful benefit
at 700°C, while performance at 800°C trends back toward the baseline alloy behavior. Together, these
results demonstrate that computational thermodynamics provides an effective framework to accelerate the
design and screening of advanced V alloys for fusion structural applications.

Vanadium alloys remain attractive candidate structural materials for fusion systems because of their high-
temperature capability, irradiation tolerance, reduced activation potential and chemical compatibility with
pure Lithium liquid metal. However, the conventional V-4Cr-4Ti (V44) alloy still has limited strength and
creep resistance at the temperatures of interest for more demanding service conditions. In this work,
computational thermodynamics was used to support the design of advanced V alloys that can either
introduce stable nanoprecipitate strengthening or optimize the balance between solute content, precipitate
chemistry, and manufacturability. The progress during this reporting period focused on three linked tasks:
(1) development of a thermodynamic database for the multicomponent V-Cr-Ti-Y-O-C-N system, (2) down-
selection of candidate alloy chemistries based on calculated phase stability, and (3) initial mechanical
evaluation of newly designed alloys relative to the reference V44 alloy.

A thermodynamic database of V, Cr, Ti, Y, O, C and N was developed using the CALPHAD (CALculation of
PHAse Diagram) approach. The database was used to calculate equilibrium phase fractions as a function
of temperature for both reference and newly proposed alloys, with particular attention to the stability window
of TiC and oxide phases that may influence strengthening and processing response. These calculations
were then used to select candidate compositions representing two design strategies: nanoprecipitate-
strengthened alloys, where controlled carbide formation is intentionally promoted, and solid-solution-
strengthened alloys, which provide a alternative for alloy design comparison. Selected alloys were
manufactured following the route shown in Figure 2, and their tensile properties were measured at room
temperature and at 700°C. Step-load creep testing was also performed to benchmark the high-temperature
deformation response against available data for existing V alloys.

 134

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Calculated phase fractions as a function of temperature in (a) V44C, (b) V6Cx (only TiC fraction
is shown), (c) V44 reference alloy, and (d) V4T. The top row represents nanoprecipitate-strengthened alloy
concepts, while the bottom row represents solid-solution-strengthened alloys.
Figure 1 summarizes the thermodynamic basis for the present alloy design. The calculations distinguish
the newly designed carbide-containing alloys from the solid-solution-strengthened alloys including the
reference V44 alloy and the new V4T alloy. In V44C and the V6Cx series, TiC is predicted to remain stable
across a broad intermediate-temperature range, supporting the use of strengthening precipitates during
processing and service. In contrast, the reference V44 alloy does not show the same carbide-stability
window, and V4T remains largely solid-solution strengthened. These calculated differences provided the
rationale for selecting carbon-modified compositions for experimental evaluation.
The calculations also help define practical processing windows, because a useful alloy chemistry must form
the desired strengthening phase while avoiding excessive formation of unwanted phases over the thermal
range relevant to melting, thermomechanical processing, and annealing. This is particularly important for
advanced V alloys, where relatively small chemistry changes can significantly alter carbide, oxide, or
interstitial-related phase equilibria.
The manufacturing route used to translate the computational design into experimental materials includes
arc melting/casting, encapsulation, hot forging, cold rolling, and final vacuum annealing. This route was
selected to produce chemically homogeneous material while enabling sufficient thermomechanical working
to break down the as-cast structure and establish a controlled final microstructure. The figure also highlights
that the thermal schedule differs from the conventional V44 practice, reflecting the need to tailor processing
to the new alloy chemistries rather than directly inheriting legacy heat treatments.
An important outcome of this work is that alloy design and process design must be considered together.
For nanoprecipitate-strengthened V alloys, the same composition that enables beneficial TiC formation can
also change the sensitivity of the alloy to rolling temperature, annealing time, and cooling history. Therefore,
the computational thermodynamics calculations are being used not only to identify promising compositions,
but also to guide the selection of manufacturing steps that preserve the intended precipitate state before
mechanical testing.
Results
The initial mechanical property results are summarized in Table 1 and demonstrate that the computationally
designed alloys provide a meaningful increase in strength relative to the baseline V44 alloy. The data
include room-temperature and 700°C tensile properties for the reference alloy, the carbide-strengthened

 135

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

V44C alloy, the V4T alloy, and three V6C variants. Together, these results provide an early screening
dataset to examine how the different strengthening concepts affect yield strength, ultimate tensile strength,
and total elongation.
At room temperature, several newly designed alloys show substantially higher strength than V44. The most
notable improvement is observed for V44C and V6C2, both of which exhibit large increases in yield strength
and ultimate tensile strength compared with the reference alloy. These gains are accompanied by a
reduction in total elongation relative to V44, which is expected when strengthening is increased, but the
ductility remains measurable and sufficient for continued optimization. The V4T composition also shows
improved ultimate tensile strength while maintaining comparatively high elongation, suggesting that it may
offer a useful intermediate balance between strength and ductility.
The 700°C tensile results are especially encouraging because they directly address the primary limitation
of the current V44 alloy. V44C exhibits the largest increase in both yield strength and ultimate tensile
strength at 700°C, while also retaining the highest elongation among the alloys listed in the table. The V6C
alloys also outperform the reference alloy at elevated temperature, although the magnitude of improvement
varies with composition. This spread in behavior is valuable because it demonstrates that the computational
framework can generate a family of candidate alloys whose performance can then be refined
experimentally, rather than relying on a single trial composition.

Table 1. Tensile properties of newly designed V alloys compared with the reference V44 alloy.

 136

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Step-load creep test results of V44C, showing that performance at 700°C is superior to existing
alloy benchmarks, while the 800°C response approaches the baseline V44 behavior.
Figure 2 extends the tensile-property comparison by probing the time-dependent deformation behavior of
V44C under step-load creep conditions. The 700°C results indicate that the newly designed alloy performs
above the existing alloy benchmarks across the stress range shown, supporting the conclusion that the
strengthened microstructure remains effective under creep loading and not only under monotonic tensile
testing. In contrast, the 800°C data trend back toward the baseline behavior of V44, which suggests that
the strengthening contribution becomes less distinct at this higher temperature, likely because the faster
coarsening of TiC at 800°C which makes dominant deformation mechanisms begin to reduce the advantage
provided by the designed precipitate state.
These creep results help define where the new alloy concept is most beneficial. The present data suggest
a strong benefit around 700°C, while additional refinement will be needed to extend the same advantage
toward 800°C and beyond. This identifies where future changes in chemistry, precipitate stability, and
processing should be targeted.

Future work will focus on correlating the measured mechanical properties with detailed microstructural
characterization so that the strengthening contribution from TiC-containing precipitates can be quantified
more directly. Additional creep rupture testing will be performed to establish longer-term behavior and to
better define the transition in performance between 700 and 800°C. The computational thermodynamics
framework will also be expanded to support the next round of composition optimization, including refinement
of interstitial control and precipitate stability. Ultimately, these efforts will be used to converge on advanced
V alloy chemistries that provide a more robust combination of manufacturability, tensile strength, creep
resistance, and microstructural stability for fusion structural applications.

 137

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

5.3 INVESTIGATIONS INTO HELIUM-ION INDUCED NANOBLISTERING IN SINGLE-CRYSTAL
VANADIUM: FINITE-ELEMENT AND EXPERIMENTAL ASSESSMENT OF NANOINDENTATION-
INDUCED DEFORMATION AND FRACTURE — Peter Hosemann (University of California, Berkeley)

Helium irradiation in candidate fusion materials can generate near-surface nanobubbles, swelling, and
nanoblistering that degrade mechanical integrity and may contribute to material release into the plasma [1].
In single-crystal vanadium, high-dose helium implantation produces nanobubbles that coalesce into crack-
like cavities at the stopping range and eventually form surface blisters [2]. This report investigates whether
nanoindentation of such pressurized blisters drives crack growth at the blister perimeter or whether it
primarily changes blister-cap geometry. The work combines finite-element (FE) modeling in Abaqus with
AFM, SEM, FIB, helium-ion implantation, and nanoindentation experiments. Across the studied loading
range, Berkovich indentation does not measurably increase blister diameter and therefore provides no
evidence of perimeter crack growth. Instead, the dominant effects are cap reshaping, plastic deformation,
and, under flat-punch loading, blister-cap fracture.

Single-crystal vanadium specimens (4 x 6 x 2 mm) were mechanically polished and verified by EBSD/EDS
before irradiation. Helium implantation was performed in a Zeiss ORION NanoFab helium-ion microscope
at 25 keV with doses up to 8 x 10^17 ions/cm^2 over 10 x 10 um patches, producing surface blisters that
were then examined in SEM. AFM (Veeco Nanoscope III) was used to measure blister geometry before
and after indentation. SEM/FIB (ThermoFisher Scios 2) was used to inspect blister morphology, cut cross-
sections, and mill vent holes for depressurized tests. Nanoindentation was performed with a Bruker Hysitron
TI 990 using both Berkovich and flat-punch tips; Berkovich indentation depth was set to roughly 10%
beyond the initial blister height measured by AFM.

Figure 1. FIB-Cut surface blister with visible interior cavity
The FE model (see fig. 2) idealized a blister as an axisymmetric solid with a sharp internal seam
representing the cavity perimeter crack. Representative geometry was an inner void radius of 1 um, shell
thickness of 200 nm, and relaxed cap height of 200 nm. A depth-graded elastic-plastic constitutive law was
derived from micropillar compression data at eight irradiation dose levels and mapped through a USDFLD
field variable with peak damage near z ~ 200 nm. Internal gas pressure was applied to the cavity surfaces,
followed by indentation with a 70.3 deg equivalent conical indenter for Berkovich loading [4] or with a flat
punch. Outputs of interest were the J-integral, T-stress, local stress components, von Mises stress,
equivalent plastic strain (PEEQ), blister-cap displacement, and force-displacement response [3].

 138

 Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 Figure 2. FEA Blister Model
 Table 1: Key experimental and modeling conditions.

Parameter Value / Approach

Outputs evaluated
 displacement, and force-displacement response.

 RESULTS
 Pressure loading expanded the blister and concentrated stress and plastic strain around the cavity tip and,
 to a lesser extent, the blister cap. T-stress analysis indicated that the J-integral can be treated as a single
 crack-driving parameter only up to moderate cap displacements (positive T-stress up to approximately U =
 0.179 um); beyond that, crack assessment should also consider local stresses and plasticity. In Berkovich
 FE simulations with constant or rising interior pressure, J increased during pressure loading, decreased
 during indentation, and rebounded to only slightly higher values during indentation release. Plastic strain
 accumulated mainly during initial pressure loading and during unloading from indentation, not during the
 indentation step itself. For depressurized blisters, the force-displacement curve became concave downward
 and J could become negative during indentation, consistent with crack closure rather than growth.

 139

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. Experimental and FE force-displacement curves for Berkovich indentation into blister
Experimental Berkovich indentation supported these FE trends. AFM measurements on four blisters
showed diameter changes between -4% and +5%, with no systematic lateral expansion of the blister
perimeter. Blister heights mostly decreased after Berkovich indentation (-8% to -15%), consistent with cap
reshaping and permanent plastic deformation. Force-displacement curves (see fig. 3) showed the same
qualitative behavior as the simulations: near-linear loading for pressurized blisters and a concave-
downward response for depressurized blisters, with a kink corresponding to contact of the internal cavity
surfaces. Matching experimental blister geometry in the FE model required internal pressures above 300
MPa and around 390 MPa for one representative blister; for that case, the simulated reaction force
remained about 60% higher than the experiment, likely because swelling strains, exact shell-thickness
variation, and constitutive uncertainty were not included.

Figure 4. Surface blister post flatpunch indentation
Flat-punch indentation (fig. 4) produced a distinctly different response. Experiments showed kinks in the
force-displacement curves together with SEM evidence of blister-cap cracking. AFM after flat-punch loading
showed increased cap height in three of four blisters (9%, 18%, and 38%) and volume increases of 35-40%
in the blisters that were quantified. These observations indicate fracture through the blister cap and gas
release rather than crack growth along the cavity perimeter. FE simulations captured the higher reaction
force under flat-punch loading but, because cap fracture was not modeled explicitly, could not reproduce
the post-fracture experimental response.

 140

 Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 Figure 5. Force-displacement curve for flatpunch indentation on blister. Orange circle marking where a
 possible blister cap fracture occurred, followed by a decrease in reaction slope.

Flat-punch + pressurized blister cracking, post-indent height increases of fracture and gas release
 9-38%, and volume increases of 35-40%. instead of perimeter growth.

 This study shows that, for helium-ion-induced nanoblisters in single-crystal vanadium, Berkovich
 indentation to the investigated depths primarily redistributes stresses and plastically reshapes the blister
 cap without measurably driving perimeter crack growth. The governing feature of the global mechanical
 response is the presence or absence of internal gas pressure, which controls the overall shape of the force-
 displacement curve. In contrast, flat-punch loading promotes a different failure mode: fracture through the
 blister cap. The combined FE/experimental framework also demonstrates that a functionally graded, depth-
 dependent constitutive model is essential for reproducing the observed mechanical behavior of irradiated
 blistered material.

 Future work should include swelling and residual stresses in the FE model, a physically coupled pressure-
 volume formulation rather than prescribed pressure evolution, cohesive-zone or other fracture-capable
 elements to model blister-cap cracking, and improved constitutive data from microtensile or related tests
 closer to the actual void depth. Additional in situ and lower-temperature experiments are especially
 promising, since a more brittle response may allow perimeter crack growth to be activated and observed
 directly.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 6. FUSION CORROSION AND COMPATIBILITY SCIENCE

 143

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

6.1 COMPATIBILITY OF REDUCED ACTIVATION FERRITIC MARTENSITIC STEEL IN STATIC
EUTECTIC PbLi — M. Romedenne, B.A. Pint (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

The objective of this study is to evaluate the impact of non-metallic impurities (Nitrogen, Carbon and
Oxygen) on compatibility of U.S. RAFM steel in static and flowing lithium at 600 °C. The impact of alloy
microstructure will also be investigated by exposed welded steels in static and flowing lithium. This work is
funded under the INFUSE program.

SUMMARY
In the present report, initial results related to the static exposure of CNA-rod in lithium containing various
amounts of nitrogen were reported. Characterization using SEM-EDS were used to evaluate the extent of
nitridation, Cr dissolution and susceptibility to cracking.

Introduction
The emergence of private fusion enterprises is renewing focus on materials challenges posed by liquid
breeder blanket concepts. Due to its favorable heat transfer properties, liquid lithium has been proposed as
potential plasma-facing material for divertor structures [1-3], D-T recycling medium [4] and coolant [5].
However, liquid lithium has been shown to present significant compatibility challenges related to liquid metal
embrittlement [6,7], reduction of ceramic coatings [8], dissolution and nitridation of structural steels and Nb-
based alloys and liquid metal environment embrittlement of steels and vanadium-based alloys [4,9-13].
Following the later development of Reduced Activation Ferritic Martensitic (RAFM) steels [ref], further
compatibility studies were performed [14-18]. Exposures of RAFM steel JFL-1 and F82H were conducted
in static commercial and purified lithium. Various mass loss kinetics were observed as a function of nitrogen
content in lithium as well as a function of the size and nature of crucible [14-18].
The purpose of the present study is to investigate the impact of nitrogen content in lithium on the extent of
lithium-induced nitridation, dissolution, room-temperature tensile properties and susceptibility to cracking of
U.S. RAFM steel. For that purpose, a newly developed U.S. RAFM steel, CNA was exposed to static lithium
with varying amounts of nitrogen impurities for 500 h at 600 °C.
Experimental Procedures
Materials
RAFM CNA (HT7 heat) was provided by ORNL [19] in the form of a 2.4 cm diameter rod (henceforth named
CNAR) in the normalized (10 min at 1,180 °C) and tempered state (60 min at 760 °C). CNAR analyzed
composition is reported in Table 1. Lithium rods were used and purchased from Sigma Aldrich (composition
in Table 2)

Liquid metal exposures

 144

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Liquid metal exposures were performed in Mo capsules (76.2 mm tall of 25.4 mm outer diameter). SS3 type
specimens were EDM machined from the provided rod material (Table 1) and milled to remove all EDM
surface prior to exposure. Two specimens were inserted per individual capsule and attached to Mo end
caps using Mo wires (Figure 1a). Prior to assembly, the capsules and caps were pre-cleaned with
hydrofluoric acid. The end caps were then electron beam welded to the Mo capsule body in vacuum and
Gas Tungsten Arc Welded in an Argon-filled glove box (impurity levels ≤1 ppm O2 and H2O) to prevent Mo
from oxidizing during welding. Exposures were performed for 500 h at 600 °C in a box furnace (Figure 1b).
Various lithium environments were evaluated, hot-gettered lithium (4.5 g) using Zr and Ti foils (Henceforth
named Li+TiZr) (Figure 1c), commercial lithium (4.5 g) (henceforth named Li) and lithium with 10.3 mg of
Li3N powder (henceforth named Li+Li3N) to vary the dissolved nitrogen in lithium.

Figure 1. (a) 2 SS3 type specimens attached to Mo cap using Mo wires, (b) welded capsules inside the
box furnace and (c) Ti and Zr foils before insertion inside the Mo capsule.
A summary of the lithium capsules is reported in Table 3. A total of 6 specimens were exposed in commercial
Li, 8 specimens in Li+TiZr and 4 specimens in Li+Li3N (Table 3). As a safety measure, each capsule was
sealed in an evacuated type 316 stainless steel outer capsule. Exposures were performed in a box furnace
at 600 °C for 500 h (Table 3). Before cooling, the capsules were inverted to allow the liquid metals to drain
away from the specimens and were opened on the wire end in the glove box. For environmental, health
and safety concerns, the samples were sequentially cleaned of residual Li using ammonia.

Characterization and thermodynamic modeling
Specimens were weighed before and after exposure and ammonia cleaning using a Mettler Toledo XP205
balance with an accuracy of ±0.04 mg. After cleaning, specimens were sectioned and mounted as cross
sections and polished to 1 µm finish for microstructural characterization. The as received and exposed
specimens were observed using secondary electron (SE) and backscattered electron (BSE) imaging using
a scanning electron microscope (ZEISS Gemini 460 SEM) equipped with energy dispersive X-Ray
spectroscopy (EDS) that was used for line scans and elemental maps. Surface X-ray diffraction
characterization (XRD) of selected specimens was conducted on the Panalytical MRD diffractometer from
nominally 20 to 120° 2θ using CoKα radiation (λ=1.788965 Å) in Bragg-Brentano geometry. All scans used
¼° fixed slits, ½° anti-scatter slit, 0.04 soller slits coupled with a 10 mm mask (beam length). For the phase
identification procedure, a search match was conducted using the Jade software [33] and the ICDD 2021
database [34]. Thermodynamic calculations were performed using ThermoCalc 2023b software
and TCFE11 database at 600 °C using alloy composition reported in Table 1.
Results
Figure 2 shows mass change of the CNAR specimens after lithium exposures for 500 h at 600 °C. Mass
gains were measured after exposure in lithium with added Zr and Ti foils. Large mass losses were measured
in commercial lithium. In lithium with added Li3N powder both mass gains and mass losses were measured
that averaged to lower mass losses compared to commercial lithium.

BSE cross section images of the specimens after various Li environments exposures are reported in Figure
3 for the grip and fracture section of the specimens. No signs of intergranular attack or cracking was
observed in Li with added Ti and Zr foils (Figure 3a and b). Significant intergranular attack (25 ± 5 µm) was
observed in Li+Li3N (Figure 3c and f) that resulted in cracking near the fracture region of the specimen
after tensile testing (Figure 3f). In commercial Li, thinner intergranular attack was observed (Figure 3b and
d). The depth of attack varied significantly along the surface with regions with no visible intergranular attacks
and intergranular regions about 6 ± 4 µm deep (Figure 3c and d). The extent of cracking was less
pronounced in commercial lithium (Figure 3d) than in Li+Li3N (Figure 3f) and also varied along the surface
of the specimen while cracking was observed along the whole specimen surface in the case of Li+Li3N
environment (Figure 3f).

Figure 3. BSE images of CNAR after 500 h in (a,c) LiTiZr, (b,e) commercial Li and (c,d) Li+Li3N at 600 °C,
Mo capsule after room temperature tensile testing in the (a, b, c) grip and (d, e, f) fracture regions.
EDS Cr elemental depletion profiles revealed Cr dissolution after Li exposure in various environments
(Figure 4). The extent of Cr depletion increased with increasing nitrogen content in lithium (Figure 4). The
Cr concentration at the Li-alloy interface decreased with increasing nitrogen content in lithium (Figure 4).

Future work will investigate impact of dissolution and nitridation on room temperature tensile properties as
well as the impact of Cr depletion on phase transformation using thermodynamic calculations and surface
XRD measurements. TEM characterization will be performed to identify the nature of precipitates within the
Cr-depleted layer in the case of Li+Li3N environment. The impact of carbon and oxygen impurities on room
temperature tensile properties will be also studied in static lithium as well as impact of temperature (700
°C) and microstructure (evaluation of weldments).

6.2 DEVELOPMENT AND DE-RISKING OF LI ELECTROLYSIS AND COREXT PROCESS BY FLOW-
LOOP INTEGRATION — M. Romedenne, A. Willoughby (Oak Ridge National Laboratory)

Two objectives were planned for FY25, compatibility testing of various doped oxides in static lithium and
establishment of a lithium purification furnace in a glove box. The funding was received in two batches, and
it was decided to order the glovebox and related hardware first to optimize delivery timelines. This resulted
in the delay of the start of the static lithium capsules until the second batch of funding was received (June
2025).

SUMMARY
A specialized glovebox was engineered for high-temperature lithium purification, featuring a thermowell
rated for 600-650°C, a nitrogen gas purification and cooling system, and a custom-machined lid and
condenser to manage lithium vapors. In the experimental phase, specimen electrolyte discs were wrapped
in titanium foil, sealed within molybdenum capsules, and exposed to liquid lithium for 100 hours at 350 °C.
The post-exposure analysis revealed that both the NASICON and LAGP specimens, along with portions of
the titanium foil, partially or completely dissolved in lithium, while testing for LZZO remains pending due to
supply chain delays.
Progress and Status
Introduction:
The development of sustainable tritium breeding and recovery technologies is a critical for the realization
of commercial fusion energy. Among the candidate breeder materials, liquid lithium (Li) is highly regarded
for its high tritium breeding ratio, excellent heat transfer properties, and high tritium solubility. However, the
management of the tritium inventory within the molten metal remains a significant engineering challenge.
The CoRExt process has been proposed as an integrated solution to continuously recover tritium from liquid
lithium blankets [1]. A central component of this electrochemical extraction technology is the use of lithium-
ion conducting solid electrolytes capable of maintaining structural and chemical stability at elevated
operational temperatures, typically ranging from 350 °C to 600 °C. The deployment of solid electrolytes in
molten lithium necessitates exceptional resistance to chemical reduction and dissolution. While oxide-
based ceramics such as NASICON (Na3Zr2Si2PO12) and LAGP (Li1.5Al0.5Ge1.5P3O12) are recognized for
their high ionic conductivities in ambient-temperature battery applications [2], their thermodynamic stability
in direct contact with metallic lithium at high temperatures is poorly documented. Literature on alkali metal
compatibility suggests that many multicomponent oxides undergo rapid interfacial degradation or complete
dissolution when exposed to the aggressive reducing environment of liquid lithium [3].
Recent efforts to de-risk these materials involve both static immersion tests and dynamic flow-loop
integration to simulate the kinetic conditions of a fusion blanket. Understanding the corrosion kinetics and
dissolution mechanisms of these electrolytes is vital for ensuring the purity of the recycled tritium stream
and the longevity of the extraction system. Failure of these ceramic interfaces not only compromises the
electrochemical efficiency of the CoRExt process but also risks introducing dissolved impurities into the
primary lithium loop, potentially fouling downstream components. Consequently, rigorous experimental
validation of electrolyte stability, such as the static 100-hour exposures conducted at 350 °C, is essential to
identify viable material candidates for high-temperature tritium recycling applications
Glovebox design:
The glovebox was designed to accommodate a thermowell that can accommodate temperatures up to 600
°C and liquid lithium (Figure 1a). A nitrogen gas purification system and cooler system were ordered. A lid
and condenser were designed to condense lithium vapors during high temperature purification of lithium
(Figure 1b) and were designed and machined. High temperature and corrosion resistant crucibles were
ordered.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. (a) Glovebox design and (b) Computed Aided Design of lid and condenser that will be installed
in the glovebox to alloy ex-situ purification of melted lithium.

Experimental procedure static lithium:
LZZO (Li7La3Zr2O12 99.9 % metal basis excl. Hf 10*20*1 mm), NASICON (Na3Zr2Si2PO12 19.4 diam *
1 mm) and LAGP (Li1.5Al0.5Ge1.5P3O12 19 mm diam * 1 mm thick) specimens were purchased from
American Elements, and MTI corporation respectively. To this day, LZZO from American Elements have not
been received due to supply chain and manufacturing issues according to the vendor. NASICON and LAGP
were received and weighed and Keyence prior to static commercial lithium exposure for 100 h at 350 °C
using Mo capsules. Due to the brittleness of the 1 mm discs, the specimens were enveloped in Ti foils to
be inserted within the Mo capsules (Figure 1a). The capsules and caps were pre-cleaned with hydrofluoric
acid and were inserted into the glove box. Specimens were placed within individual capsules and attached
to Mo end caps using Mo wires (Figure 1a and b). The end cap was electron beam welded to the Mo
capsule body in vacuum and Gas Tungsten Arc Welded in an argon-filled glove box (impurity levels ≤1 ppm
O2 and H2O) to prevent the Mo from oxidizing during welding. The capsules were filled with 4.5 g of
commercial lithium purchased from Sigma Aldrich (99.99 metal purity). As a safety measure, each capsule
was sealed in an evacuated type 316 stainless steel outer capsule and exposed in box furnace for 100 h
at 350 °C.

7.1 DEVELOPMENT OF COLD SPRAY ADDITIVE MANUFACTURING FOR TUNGSTEN-BASED
PLASMA — G. Crawford, N. Madden, F. Thompson, B. Jasthi, J. Petersen, L. Raman (South Dakota School
of Mines and Technology), E. Lang (University of New Mexico)

The overall goal of the proposed project is to explore and develop cold spray additive manufacturing for W-
based materials used in plasma-facing components. The project aims to establish the relationships among
cold spray process conditions, microstructural features, and irradiation-induced degradation of W-based
materials fabricated using cold spray technology. Ultimately, this project will demonstrate the effectiveness
of cold spray-produced W-based composites as plasma-facing materials.

SUMMARY
Cold spray is a solid-state additive manufacturing process that requires detailed characterization of the
microstructure of powders. The cold spray of tungsten powder is limited, and the overarching goal of this
project is to expand the current knowledge of cold sprayability of tungsten, to enable its use across a wider
range of cold spray systems. A preprocessing step of ball milling tungsten powder was utilized to enhance
its cold sprayability. X-ray diffraction residual stress measurements combined with backscatter electron
microscopy were used to understand the effect of ball milling on residual stress and microstructural changes
in the tungsten powder.

The quality of the powder used in cold spray processes is crucial because of the technique's nature as a
solid-state additive manufacturing method. During cold spray deposition, the initial microstructure and
phase of the powder are preserved. Consequently, two distinct types of tungsten powder were acquired,
differing primarily in particle shape, internal microstructure, tungsten purity, and overall cost. This acquisition
facilitates a comparative analysis to elucidate how differences in powder characteristics influence the cold
sprayability of tungsten powders. Figure 1 presents representative scanning electron microscopy (SEM)
images of the high and lower quantities tungsten powders.

Figure 1. Scanning electron microscopy images of (a) high quality tungsten powder and (b) low quality
tungsten powder.
To improve the cold sprayability of tungsten across a wider range of cold spray systems, beyond the current
high-end systems capable of reaching high temperatures, our initial approach involved preprocessing
tungsten powders. Preprocessing cold spray powders is a common technique widely used in the deposition
of lower-temperature materials to boost efficiency and quality [1,2]. Cold working of tungsten has been
shown to improve ductility and strength [3]. The preprocessing for the cold spraying of tungsten powder

 155

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

utilized cold working through ball milling. Figure 2 shows representative X-ray diffraction (XRD) residual
stress measurements using the omega method, where d-spacing is plotted against sin²(ψ). Figure 2a
displays the stress state of the as-received tungsten powder, whereas Figure 2b shows the residual stress
after ball milling for 10 min with a high-energy process. The psi splitting seen in Figure 2b indicates the
presence of both normal and shear stresses in the milled tungsten.

Figure 2. X-ray diffraction measurement of the residual stress around the 211 peak in (a) the as-received
sample and (b) the ball-milled sample for 10 min.
To ensure the flowability of tungsten powder during cold spray, a direct comparison was made between the
ball-milled and as-received powders. The measured particle sizes are shown in Figure 3, which indicates
an average size change of 1.4 μm. The microstructures of both samples were analyzed using electron
backscattered diffraction (EBSD), and the results are displayed in Figure 4. Figures 4a and 4b present the
Euler color maps for the as-received and ball-milled powders, respectively, and Figures 4c and 4d show
the grain orientation spread maps, confirming the residual stress observed via XRD. Ball milling of tungsten
powders is likely to lower the ductile-to-brittle transition temperature, as decreasing the flow stress
ultimately makes the powder more amenable to cold spraying and expands the processing window.

Figure 3. Particle size distribution for (a) as-received tungsten powder, and (b) 10-minute ball-milled
tungsten powder.

 156

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 4. Electron backscattered diffraction maps of tungsten powder, where (a) and (b) are Euler maps of
the as-received and 10-minute milled sample, and (c) and (d) are grain orientation spread maps of the as-
received and 10-minute milled sample, respectively.

These initial experiments will involve single-splat tests, examine deformation behavior, compare the
preprocessed and as-received powders, and analyze different tungsten qualities. The first phase will begin
with a parametric study of the cold spray temperatures, residual stress from ball milling, and tungsten
powder sources, using single-splat cold spray depositions. The deformation behavior of tungsten will be
studied through FIB cross-sections of single-splat particles. This analysis will help determine the optimal
cold spray parameters, the necessary preprocessing of tungsten powder, and the best powder sources for
subsequent work.

 8. MECHANISMS AND ANALYSIS

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

8.1 ARRHENIUS-MECHANICS FRAMEWORK FOR FATIGUE SUSCEPTIBILITY IN FUSION
MATERIALS — Jakub Veverka, Shen J. Dillon (University of California, Irvine)

Develop a physically grounded framework for predicting fatigue susceptibility in fusion-relevant structural
materials under thermal and mechanical cycling by connecting atomistically accessible activation enthalpy,
activation volume, and activation entropy to S-N behavior, crack-growth thresholds, and microstructural
design variables.

SUMMARY
Fatigue is a critical concern in fusion systems because pulsed operation, startup/shutdown, thermal
gradients, and constrained expansion produce repeated stress and temperature excursions even in
nominally high-strength components. In this work, fatigue is formulated as a rare-event Arrhenius hazard
process in which the cycle-integrated probability of a damaging activation event determines life. The central
result is that susceptibility is controlled not only by the barrier magnitude but by the shape of the effective
free-energy barrier along the cyclic stress path. A negative, stress-dependent activation entropy can flatten
the peak barrier over a finite stress range, producing an endurance-limit-like plateau, while a monotonic
barrier produces no plateau. Because the governing quantities can be extracted from atomistic calculations,
the framework offers a route for screening fusion alloys and microstructures for fatigue resistance before
exhaustive long-duration testing.

The problem it addresses is directly relevant to fusion reactor materials development. Fusion components
experience repeated thermal and stress cycling during startup, shutdown, pulsed operation, plasma
transients, thermal gradients, and mechanical constraint. Those cyclic excursions can initiate damage or
drive subcritical crack extension even when monotonic strength is nominally adequate. A predictive
framework for fatigue susceptibility is therefore important for ranking candidate alloys, interpreting
irradiation-induced changes in microstructure, and prioritizing expensive long-duration test campaigns [1].
The present work formulates fatigue as a cycle-integrated rare-event process. For a cyclic stress history

transparent: fatigue susceptibility is governed by the full stress-dependent free-energy landscape sampled
over the cycle, not simply by a single barrier value at one nominal stress.
A central outcome is that the shape of the peak barrier controls whether an endurance-limit-like plateau can
emerge. In the rare-event limit, the cycle hazard is dominated by the neighborhood of the peak stress 𝜎𝜎pk =
𝜎𝜎𝑚𝑚 + 𝜎𝜎𝑎𝑎 , so the existence of a plateau requires the peak barrier to become locally stationary with respect

to amplitude, ≈ 0 . If 𝛥𝛥𝐺𝐺pk decreases monotonically as the cycle amplitude rises, the hazard
 𝑑𝑑(𝜎𝜎𝑎𝑎 )
increases continuously and the S-N curve has no horizontal segment. By contrast, if the entropy term is
negative at low stress and evolves with stress so that it offsets mechanical barrier lowering over a finite
range, the barrier develops a knee or flattened segment and an endurance-limit-like plateau appears. This
means that barrier shape, not only barrier height, is the mechanistic signature of fatigue resistance.
Figure 1 shows this result directly for surface-nucleation, Taylor, and Hall-Petch-type barriers. The top row,
which includes the stress-dependent activation entropy, exhibits endurance-limit-like plateaus and the
expected temperature and frequency shifts. The bottom row sets S* = 0 and removes the plateau,
demonstrating that entropy is not a secondary correction but a governing control parameter for fatigue
susceptibility.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Calculated S-N curves for surface, Taylor, and Hall-Petch-type Arrhenius fatigue models. Top row:
stress-dependent activation entropy included. Bottom row: S* = 0. The entropy-modified barrier produces
endurance-limit-like plateaus, frequency dependence, and temperature suppression; removing entropy
eliminates the plateau.
The same entropy-modified barrier also provides a physically meaningful route to threshold-like crack-
growth behavior. Figure 2 shows a fit of tungsten fatigue crack-growth data using the same Arrhenius
framework. When the entropy contribution is retained, the model produces a sharp low-ΔK suppression and
an effective threshold; setting S* = 0 removes this behavior. This is important for fusion applications because
cyclic service conditions can involve both initiation and early crack growth, so a single barrier-based
description of both regimes is more useful than disconnected empirical fits [2].

Figure 2. Tungsten fatigue crack-growth data fit with the Arrhenius crack-growth model. The entropy-
modified barrier (solid line) produces strong low-ΔK suppression and a threshold-like response, whereas
the S* = 0 model (dashed line) does not.
The microstructural sensitivity follows naturally from the same hazard structure. If Ξ denotes the number of
 cyc ~ 𝛯𝛯 𝑛𝑛cyc , so both the local barrier
statistically available damage sites, the total cycle hazard scales as 𝛬𝛬tot
shape and the number of available initiation sites control susceptibility. In the associated calculations, finer
grain size, higher dislocation density, lower surface stress concentration, and reduced population of weak
surface sites all increase fatigue resistance by either raising ΔG_eff locally or reducing the effective
sampling of low-barrier events. This point is particularly relevant to fusion environments, where irradiation,

 160

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

transmutation, and thermal exposure continuously reshape defect populations and local stress
concentrations.
Beyond the specific tungsten parameterization used here, the framework identifies actionable descriptors
for fusion materials design: high baseline enthalpic barriers, favorable activation volumes, negative low-
stress activation entropy, and microstructures that reduce local stress concentration or increase effective
hardening length scales. Because these descriptors can, in principle, be estimated from DFT, molecular
dynamics, nudged-elastic-band calculations, or atomistically informed mesoscale models, the present work
provides a practical path for computationally screening fusion alloys and irradiation-evolved microstructures
for fatigue susceptibility before exhaustive testing.

The activation entropy associated with a plasticity will be measured experimentally and related with fatigue
response. We have demonstrated several methods to calculate activation entropy from experimental data.

8.2 ARRHENIUS-HAZARD FRAMEWORK FOR FRACTURE TOUGHNESS EVOLUTION IN FUSION
MATERIALS — Jakub Veverka, Shen J. Dillon (University of California, Irvine)

This activity seeks to develop a rate-dependent mechanics framework for predicting fracture response in
fusion-relevant materials from microstructure, temperature, and loading history by treating crack advance,
plasticity, and related crack-tip processes as competing activated hazards rather than as independent
stress-only criteria.

SUMMARY
Fracture is a central life-limiting process for fusion materials because neutron damage, transmutation
gases, thermal gradients, and cyclic thermo-mechanical loading continuously evolve the microstructure that
controls crack resistance. In this work, fracture toughness is treated as an emergent composite property
rather than a fixed material constant. Bond rupture, bulk plasticity, crack-tip plasticity, dislocation emission,
twinning, microcracking, and shielding processes are formulated as competing Arrhenius-rate channels
whose cumulative hazards determine when unstable crack growth occurs. This places brittle fracture and
plastic relaxation within a common thermodynamic framework and provides a route for predicting toughness
evolution a priori as the underlying microstructure changes. Phase-field simulation shows that the approach
can recover a variety of fracture microstructures and responses.

Fracture remains one of the most consequential integrity challenges in fusion systems. Plasma-facing and
structural materials are exposed to extreme thermal gradients, thermomechanical cycling, irradiation
hardening and embrittlement, and defect/gas accumulation that continuously alter crack-tip shielding, local
plasticity, and bond-rupture barriers. As a result, fracture toughness cannot generally be treated as a fixed
property. Instead, it evolves with microstructure and service history. This is particularly important for fusion
because safe component lifetimes will depend on anticipating how crack resistance changes before
catastrophic failure occurs.
Model Development
Conventional fracture mechanics provides the correct macroscopic language through energy release,
stress intensity, and cohesive resistance, but it becomes difficult to predict toughness a priori once multiple
dissipative channels act simultaneously [1-3]. The central issue is that bond rupture, crack-tip plasticity,
bulk plasticity, twinning, dislocation emission, phase transformation, frictional shielding, and microcracking
are fundamentally rate-dependent processes. Treating them only as stress-dependent corrections obscures
the competition that actually selects whether a crack blunts, arrests, tears stably, or becomes unstable. To
address this, fracture was formulated within an Arrhenius mechanics framework in which the crack tip is
treated as an activation site with a stress-biased barrier and activation volume [4]. Fracture initiation occurs
when the integrated hazard for crack advance reaches order unity, while competing plastic and relaxation
channels reduce or redistribute the local driving force. Within this structure, apparent fracture toughness is
the loading condition at which the crack-opening hazard overtakes the competing relaxation hazards. The
key to implementing this approach is defining plasticity mechanisms and fracture as thermodynamically
consistent rate dependent processes.
The formulation retains the standard fracture variables in a natural way. Crack length, constraint, mode
mixity, and tip radius enter through the amplified local crack-tip stress, while microstructure enters through
the local barrier landscape and site density. This provides a direct path from atomistic or mesoscale
descriptions of bond rupture and plastic flow to continuum-scale toughness, crack-velocity relations, and
ductile-to-brittle transitions. Most importantly for fusion applications, the same framework can ultimately
accept irradiation-driven inputs such as defect density, gas content, hardening state, and process-zone
shielding as evolving internal variables rather than as ad hoc correction factors.
Fracture and competing deformation mechanisms are treated as activated rate channels. For a given
 Δ𝐺𝐺 ∗ −𝜎𝜎 𝑣𝑣 ∗
process 𝑖𝑖, the rate is written as 𝜂𝜂̇ 𝑖𝑖 = 𝜂𝜂̇ 0,𝑖𝑖 exp �− 𝑖𝑖 tip 𝑖𝑖 �, where 𝜂𝜂̇ 0,𝑖𝑖 is a prefactor, Δ𝐺𝐺𝑖𝑖∗ is the activation
 𝑘𝑘𝑘𝑘
barrier, 𝑣𝑣𝑖𝑖∗ is the activation volume, and 𝜎𝜎tip is the local crack-tip stress. The corresponding cumulative

 162

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 𝑡𝑡
hazard is 𝐻𝐻𝑖𝑖 (𝑡𝑡) = ∫0 𝜂𝜂̇ 𝑖𝑖 (𝑡𝑡 ′ ) 𝑑𝑑𝑡𝑡 ′ , and the observed fracture response follows from competition among
channels. In this picture, unstable crack advance occurs when the hazard for crack opening overtakes the
competing hazards associated with crack-tip relaxation, plastic flow, twinning, and related shielding
processes. Thus, apparent fracture toughness emerges from rate competition and evolves naturally with
microstructure, temperature, and loading history.
Results
Figure 1 shows a representative coupled phase-field result. At early loading, the initial notch is present but
new damage is largely arrested while plastic activity begins to localize ahead of the tip. With increasing
opening, the plastic zone broadens and the fracture field activates within that same zone rather than along
an independent brittle path. The load-opening response exhibits initial hardening, a peak associated with
rapid damage advance, and subsequent continued tearing. This is the key qualitative result of the present
framework: in a plastically deforming material, fracture is selected by competition between hazard channels
rather than by a single fixed toughness parameter. In a fusion context, this distinction is essential because
irradiation and transmutation alter both the fracture and plasticity channels simultaneously. Figure 2 shows
the analogous simulation with the plasticity barrier increased such that the response is purely brittle fracture.

Figure 1. Representative coupled Arrhenius plasticity-Arrhenius fracture phase-field simulation for a
notched plate. Top row: crack damage field at increasing load steps. Bottom row: evolving log10(dislocation
density). Right: reaction force-opening response. Damage remains pinned while plasticity localizes and
then advances through the evolved process zone once the fracture hazard overtakes the competing
relaxation hazard.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Phase-field simulation of KI loading of a pre-notch showing the evolution of the crack damage
and dislocation density at each step, along with the reaction force – opening curve. This example uses the
same fracture response as shown in Figure 1, but with plasticity effectively suppressed by inputting an
arbitrarily large 𝐻𝐻 ∗ for plasticity.

Future work will focus on calibrating the coupled hazard formulation against fusion-relevant materials and
microstructures and integrating evolving descriptors of microstructural damage which can be naturally
integrated within our hazard framework.
