8.3 DISLOCATION-MEDIATED HELIUM TRANSPORT FOR HIGH TEMPERATURE HELIUM
EMBRITTLEMENT IN AUSTENITIC 316L AND FERRITIC FE-9%CR ALLOYS — Z. Qi, Y. Li, M.L.
Crespillo (University of Tennessee), S.J. Zinkle (University of Tennessee and Oak Ridge National
Laboratory)
Extended Abstract: Full manuscript to be published

SUMMARY
High temperature helium embrittlement (HTHE) is widely considered a potential lifetime-limiting degradation
mechanism for structural materials in fusion and advanced generation IV fission reactor environments.
While austenitic stainless steels have historically shown susceptibility under certain creep conditions,
ferritic/martensitic steels are often assumed to be intrinsically resistant. This study systematically evaluates
the roles of implantation rate, elastic stress, and dislocation line density on helium-induced cavity evolution
in austenitic 316L stainless steel (SS) and ferritic binary Fe-9%Cr alloy under conditions relevant to
elevated-temperature reactor service.
Helium implantations (4.5 MeV) were conducted at 750 °C with implantation rates ranging from 28 to 280
appm/h for 316L SS and Fe-9%Cr, with varying pre-deformation conditions and with or without an initially
applied elastic stress of ~100 MPa during implantation. At these elevated temperatures and relatively low
implantation rates, cavity nucleation occurred predominantly at defect–matrix interfaces due to favorable
helium binding energies that reduce the thermodynamic barrier for He precipitation. As a result, cavity
density was governed primarily by microstructure rather than implantation rate.
For both alloys, no discernible elastic stress effect was observed on matrix or grain boundary (GB) cavity
behavior under the applied elastic loading. In contrast, strong dislocation line effects were identified. In 316L
SS, increasing dislocation line density enhanced GB cavity density and GB helium concentration. In
specimens with low precipitate density, dislocation lines effectively trapped helium in the matrix, increasing
local cavity nucleation and transporting helium toward GBs. In microstructures containing moderate
precipitate densities, competition among precipitates, dislocation lines, and GBs as defect sinks altered
matrix cavity density but still demonstrated dislocation-assisted helium transport.
In Fe-9%Cr, similar dislocation-mediated behavior was observed. In pre-deformed conditions with low
precipitate density, increasing dislocation density increased both matrix and GB cavity densities, including
the formation of very large GB cavities. In recrystallized and cold-worked conditions containing moderate
precipitate densities, matrix cavity coarsening accompanied by reduced cavity density was observed with
increasing dislocation density, while GB cavity behavior remained comparatively stable. Detailed
experimental observations support pipe diffusion along dislocation cores as a dominant helium transport
mechanism under elastic stress conditions. Stronger capability of nucleating matrix cavities due to rapid
dislocation recovery and the presence of moderate precipitate density in recrystallized and cold-worked Fe-
9%Cr during high-temperature implantation likely moderates long-range helium transport to GBs.
Re-evaluation of prior creep studies reporting pronounced HTHE in austenitic alloys indicates that those
experiments involved plastic deformation, where high dislocation densities were either pre-existing or
continuously generated during plastic flow. Under such conditions, dislocation sweeping combined with
pipe diffusion likely accelerates helium transport to GBs, amplifying embrittlement severity. In contrast,
under service-relevant elastic stress conditions, dislocation sweeping is not expected to dominate.
These results demonstrate that HTHE susceptibility in both austenitic and ferritic alloys is strongly linked to
plasticity-associated dislocation mechanisms rather than to elastic stress-enhanced helium diffusion alone.
Ferritic Fe-9%Cr cannot be assumed intrinsically immune to HTHE; susceptibility depends sensitively on
microstructure, particularly precipitate density and dislocation content. Because reactor structural materials
are designed to operate within allowable stresses that limit plastic deformation, prior studies conducted
under accelerated plastic conditions may overestimate HTHE severity. Long-term evaluations under
elastically strained, service-representative conditions remain necessary to determine the true embrittlement
resistance of advanced fusion structural materials.

 165

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 9. MODELING PROCESSES IN FUSION SYSTEM MATERIALS

 166

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9.1 DEVELOPMENT OF A MACHINE LEARNED INTERATOMIC POTENTIAL FOR STUDYING
RADIATION DAMAGE IN VANADIUM — Mary Alice Cusentino, Anne Davis (Sandia National Laboratories)

Vanadium is being considered as a candidate structural material for the breeder blanket component of
future fusion reactors due to its good thermal conductivity, low activation, and chemical compatibility with
FLiBe [1]. However, vanadium has a high hydrogen solubility resulting in hydrogen diffusion and trapping
in the vanadium structural alloy which will be further impacted by microstructural changes from neutron
irradiation. Understanding hydrogen permeation into vanadium and how radiation indued defects impact
hydrogen diffusion and trapping will be critical for accurately predicting the tritium breeding ratio. In this
project, we are developing models and performing multi-scale simulations with coupled, targeted
experiments to better understand these processes.

SUMMARY
We have developed an atomic cluster expansion (ACE) machine learned interatomic potential (ML-IAP) [2]
for studying radiation effects in vanadium. The ACE ML-IAP was trained to a large dataset of structures
relaxed using density functional theory (DFT) and accurately reproduces key defect properties like defect
formation energies, vacancy binding energies, and defect migration barriers. The ML-IAP was used to
perform primary knock-on atom (PKA) simulations at a variety of PKA energies. We have quantified the
number of defects produced per PKA event and have observed the formation of ½<111> vacancy loops at
higher PKA energies of 10 KeV and above.

Atomic Cluster Expansion Model Development
To perform molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of radiation damage in vanadium, we first developed a
machine learned interatomic potential (ML-IAP) for describing the force interactions between atoms. We
use the Atomic Cluster Expansion (ACE) descriptors as outlined in [2] and have used the same general
workflow for fitting the V ACE potential as we have for previous ML-IAPs developed for plasma-material
interactions [3,4] using FitSNAP [5]. We performed density functional theory (DFT) simulations using VASP
[6,7,8] for generating the training data for fitting the ML-IAP. The training set included elastically compressed
and expanded unit cells of BCC, FCC, and HCP V as well as sheared and strained unit cells of BCC V. We
also performed high temperature DFT-MD of V at 300 K, 1000 K, and 5000 K. Lastly, we included defect
structures included both vacancies and interstitials. The training set included a total of 1,179 configurations.
The potential was optimized using the genetic algorithm capability within Dakota [9]. The properties targeted
as objective functions during the optimization included the energy and force errors, the cohesive energies,
elastic constants, defect formation energies, vacancy binding energies, and migration barriers. The
performance of the potential on a subset of these properties compared to the DFT is shown in Figure 1.
The energy and force training errors with respect to the DFT training set is 0.072 eV/atom and 0.12 eV/Å-
atom. The V ACE ML-IAP well reproduces the basic properties of BCC V including both the cohesive
energies and elastic constants as shown in the upper left of Fig. 1. The ML-IAP correctly predicts the
vacancy and <111> dumbbell formation and migration barriers (Fig. 1, bottom left). While the divacancy first
and second nearest neighbor binding energies are well reproduced as seen in the plot of Fig. 1, the three
and four vacancy structures have somewhat higher binding energies with ACE (orange) than DFT (green)
suggests. Overall, the defect properties are consistent with DFT values making this potential suitable for
performing radiation damage studies in V. We further tested the potential by performing a 10 KeV PKA
simulation at 300 K and the potential was stable and generated typical point defect structures. We are
currently working on extending this potential for hydrogen to study diffusion and trapping at radiation
induced defects.

 167

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Results for the optimized V ACE ML-IAP including elastic constants and cohesive energies (top
left), vacancy and <111> dumbbell formation and migration barriers (bottom left), vacancy binding energies
(right), and energy and force errors on the training data (bottom right). The DFT values, which the ML-IAP
was optimized to, is also reported for all material properties.
Molecular Dynamics Simulation Methods
Molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of primary knock-on atom (PKA) events in vanadium were performed
using LAMMPS [10] with an atomic cluster expansion (ACE) [2] machine-learned interatomic potential. We
simulated PKA energies of 10 and 25 keV, which is representative of the range of neutron energies found
in the tritium breeding blanket. A total of 40 simulations were run at each energy at 300 K, and the results
were averaged. A ZBL potential is included to better describe short-range interactions, and periodic
boundary conditions are applied in all directions. A simulation box of appropriate size is selected at each
energy to ensure that atoms do not cross over the boundaries of the box. For the 10 keV simulations, this
resulted in a box with an edge length of 360 Å containing 3.5 million atoms. For 25 keV, we used a box with
an edge length of 390 Å containing 4.4 million atoms. The system is initially thermalized using the NPT
ensemble for 10 ps. Then, an atom is selected at random, shifted to the center of the simulation box, and
imparted with a velocity required to achieve the desired neutron energy in a randomly selected direction.
We run an NVE calculation for a total time of 50 ps, using a variable timestep that updates periodically so
no atom moves further than 0.05 angstroms in a single timestep, with a maximum timestep of 1 fs. Energy
is dissipated through the use of a langevin thermostat, applied to the last 4 Å at the edges of the simulations
box. The output was analyzed and images were created using Ovito [11]. We used the Wigner-Seitz defect
analysis to identify interstitials and vacancies, and we used the dislocation extraction analysis (DXA) to
identify dislocation loops.
Results

 168

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Damage resulting from single cascade PKA simulations. a) The number of Frenkel pairs as a
function of time. The line is an average over 40 simulations with the shaded region representing the
standard deviation. Peak damage is reached within 0.3 ps before Frenkel pairs recombine to reach a
steady-state defect structure. b-d) Images of defects that form at b) 01. ps, c) 0.3 ps (peak damage), and
d) 50 ps (steady-state structure), with blue dots representing vacancies and grey dots representing
interstitials for a 10 keV PKA event. e) Image of a ½ <111> dislocation loop. White dots are interstitial atoms
contained within the dislocation loop.
Single cascade PKA simulations reveal typical defects and damage structures that form in vanadium. PKA
simulations were conducted at energies of 10 and 25 keV at 300 K. PKA events result in the formation of
Frenkel pairs, clusters of vacancies and interstitials, and dislocation loops (Figure 2). Larger energies result
in increased formation of Frenkel pairs, with an average of 469 and 1165 pairs forming at peak damage for
10 and 25 keV events, respectively (Figure 2a). Following peak damage (Figure 2c), Frenkel pairs
recombine as the system relaxes to a steady-state structure, with the surviving defects forming long-lived
persistent defects in the microstructure. After 50 ps (Figure 2d), the average number of Frenkel pairs is 107
for 10 keV events and 252 for 25 keV events. The recombination rate is similar for both energies (~77%).
In addition to the larger number of defects, the size of the damaged region increases with neutron energy,
with an average peak damage volume of 180,000 Å3 at 10 keV and 430,000 Å3 at 25 keV. The time to reach
peak damage is similar for both energies (0.25-0.28 ps), with slightly longer times at 25 keV due to the
formation of larger damage structures. The time to relax to a steady-state configuration, however, takes
longer for the higher energy simulations (3.43 versus 2.26 ps), again due to the formation of larger damage
structures.
Vacancies and interstitial atoms form clusters that grow in size following the initial relaxation from peak
damage. Vacancy clusters tend to be more abundant and larger in size at 25 keV, with an average of 135
clusters ranging in size from 2-48 atoms at 50 ps, compared to 10 keV, with an average of 60 clusters
ranging in size from 2-28 atoms at the same time. Interstitial clusters follow a similar pattern, with an
average of 227 clusters ranging in size from 2-27 atoms at 25 keV and 50 ps and only 98 clusters with sizes
from 2-19 atoms at 10 keV and 50 ps. In general, there are more interstitial clusters than vacancy clusters.
This is explained by the smaller size on average of the interstitial clusters, which typically form dumbbells
that diffuse quickly through the structure. In some of the simulations, either a ½ <111> dislocation loop or a
<100> dislocation loop forms following the initial relaxation from peak damage. The ½ <111> dislocation
loop has been previously reported in PKA simulations in vanadium using an embedded atom model

 169

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

potential [12], and a representative image of the dislocation loop is included in Figure 2e. The loops are
approximately the same size at both simulated energies, with loop lengths on average of 44 and 41
containing 132 and 128 atoms at 10 and 25 keV, respectively. The dislocation loops are more likely to form
at the higher PKA energy, forming ~33% of the time at 25 keV compared to 10% of the time at 10 keV. The
<100> loop forms occasionally in the 25 keV simulations (5% of the time). Dislocation loop sizes correlate
with vacancy cluster sizes.
The results of these PKA simulations are crucially important to understand microstructural damage in
structural alloys used in tritium breeding blankets. The types of defects that form, their frequency of
formation under different conditions, their relative size, and how they cluster within the microstructure are
important parameters to consider when estimating tritium diffusion and trapping. The generated defects,
particularly vacancy clusters and dislocation loops, act as trap sites for tritium, which may limit the potential
tritium breeding ratio.

The V ACE ML-IAP will be extended to first include H and then eventually Ti and Cr. We plan to perform
further analysis of the PKA simulations for additional temperatures and energies as well as simulations with
multiple PKA events. These damaged microstructures will then be used to model hydrogen permeation and
trapping once the ML-IAPs are developed. MD simulations of hydrogen permeation on these damaged
structures will illustrate hydrogen trapping at these defect sites, allowing us to calculate more accurate
diffusion coefficients. Additionally, the generated damage statistics from these simulations will be used to
parameterize mesoscale models, to better estimate tritium diffusion and trapping at larger length and time
scales.

34 (2023) 101394. http://doi.org/10.1016/j.nme.2023.101394

 171

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9.2 STRESS AND DISLOCATION EFFECT OF HenVm DEFECT-COMPLEX DIFFUSION IN BCC Fe —
Quanyou Zhang (The University of Tennessee), Tao Liang (The University of Tennessee), Zehui Qi (The
University of Tennessee), Siwei Chen (The University of Tennessee), Steve John Zinkle (The University of
Tennessee and Oak Ridge National Laboratory), Haixuan Xu (The University of Tennessee)

The objective of this work is to investigate the behavior of small helium–vacancy clusters (HenVm) iin
structural materials under applied tensile stress at elevated temperatures: (1) characterizing cluster
properties and migration mechanisms at the atomic scale via computational modeling and in situ TEM, (2)
quantifying the effects of applied stress, irradiation dose, and pre-existing dislocations on helium transport,
and (3) developing and validating machine-learning interatomic potentials to accurately capture vacancy-
helium interactions.

SUMMARY
In this study, the diffusion mechanisms and migration energy barriers of He₁V₁ defect complexes near
dislocations in bcc Fe were investigated using SEAKMC [1–3] simulations. The results reveal a two-step
pipe diffusion mechanism in which helium first dissociates from the vacancy and then migrates rapidly along
the dislocation line as an interstitial with an extremely low barrier (~0.03 eV). The presence of dislocations
significantly reduces the dissociation barrier compared with bulk diffusion, with edge dislocations lowering
the barrier more strongly than screw dislocations. Spatial mapping of migration barriers shows that the
dislocation stress field strongly influences He₁V₁ diffusion, particularly near the dislocation core and within
tensile stress regions. These findings indicate that dislocations can act as efficient transport pathways for
helium in irradiated materials, potentially accelerating helium redistribution and cavity evolution.
Understanding these mechanisms is important for predicting and mitigating high-temperature helium
embrittlement (HTHE) in structural materials used in fusion and Generation IV nuclear systems. We have
developed a machine learning interatomic potential (MLIP) for the Fe–He–Cr system using the Moment
Tensor Potential framework [4].

Simulation Methods
He₁V₁ diffusion near dislocations in bcc Fe was investigated using the self-evolving atomistic kinetic Monte
Carlo (SEAKMC) [1–3] method. Edge and screw dislocations were constructed in a 23 × 23 × 28 nm
simulation cell; the screw dislocation was generated using elasticity theory as Hao et al. [5], while the edge
dislocation followed the model developed by Osetsky et al [6]. All structures were relaxed in LAMMPS [7]
using the Fe–He interatomic potential developed by Gao et al [8]. A He₁V₁ cluster was inserted at lattice
sites adjacent to the dislocation line to represent a defect complex interacting with the dislocation core.
Migration energy barriers (MEBs) and diffusion mechanisms were identified through saddle-point searches
using the dimer method in SEAKMC at 800 K. A guided saddle-point search strategy was employed, in
which the key dissociation mechanism of He from the vacancy was first identified in bulk bcc Fe and then
used to guide saddle-point searches near dislocations. To investigate the influence of the dislocation stress
field on He₁V₁ migration, the defect complex was systematically inserted at Fe lattice sites surrounding the
dislocation line within a radius of 45 Å to fully cover the dislocation core region. Migration energy barriers
were calculated using SEAKMC through saddle-point searches with the dimer method at 800 K. At each
lattice site, multiple saddle points corresponding to He dissociation from the vacancy were identified, and
the MEB was obtained by averaging the energy barriers of these events. The saddle-point configurations
were extracted from the SEAKMC simulations and visualized in OVITO [9] to analyze the detailed diffusion
mechanisms and the variation of MEB around the dislocation line.
Results

 172

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. Dislocation pipe diffusion mechanism for He1V1 cluster along 1/2 <111> screw dislocation (a-c)
and along ½<111> edge dislocation (d-f).
Figure 1 illustrates the pipe diffusion mechanism of He₁V₁ along screw and edge dislocations. The diffusion
mechanism along the screw dislocation is shown in Fig. 1a–c. The process begins with the dissociation of
the He atom from the vacancy to a nearby tetrahedral interstitial site with a migration energy barrier (MEB)
of 2.05 eV (Fig. 1b). The dissociation direction is nearly parallel to the dislocation line. After dissociation,
the interstitial He atom preferentially diffuses along the screw dislocation line following a helical path with a
very small barrier of ~0.03 eV, enabling rapid transport along the dislocation core. The diffusion mechanism
along the edge dislocation is similar. The He atom first dissociates from the vacancy and migrates from the
compressive stress field toward the tensile region of the edge dislocation with an MEB of 1.45 eV. The
resulting interstitial He then diffuses along the edge dislocation with a nearly straight path and a barrier of
~0.03 eV. These results suggest that the edge dislocation reduces the dissociation barrier of He₁V₁ more
significantly than the screw dislocation.

 173

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Dislocation stress field effect on MEB by 1/2 <111> screw dislocation (left) and ½<111> edge
dislocation (right)
Figure 2 shows the effect of the dislocation stress field on the migration energy barrier (MEB) of He₁V₁
around the dislocation line. The color scale represents the change in MEB relative to the bulk value due to
the local stress field. For the screw dislocation (left panel), the MEB variation follows a threefold symmetry
pattern around the dislocation core. The MEB of He₁V₁ at sites closest to the dislocation line decreases to
~2.1 eV and gradually recovers to the bulk value at a distance of ~16 Å. For the edge dislocation (right
panel), the spatial distribution of the MEB exhibits mirror symmetry with respect to the dislocation line. The
sites directly beneath the dislocation core show a strongly reduced MEB of ~1.4 eV, while regions farther
from the dislocation line exhibit barriers of ~2.2–2.3 eV. These values approach the bulk limit and are
consistent with our calculated bulk He₁V₁ dissociation barrier of 2.383 eV, which is in good agreement with
previously reported values of ~2.3 eV [10–12]. Notably, the bulk MEB is comparable to the binding energy
between He and a vacancy (~2.3 eV) [13–21], indicating that He–vacancy unbinding is the key aspect
governing He₁V₁ diffusion. Compared with this bulk value, the reduction in MEB is more pronounced near
the dislocation core. In general, the tensile region above the edge dislocation exhibits lower MEB values
than the compressive region below the dislocation. Notably, the MEB of ~1.45 eV for He₁V₁ dissociation
along the edge dislocation is in close agreement with the ~1.4 eV dissociation energy reported in He
desorption experiments by Vessen et al. [22]. This suggests that the experimentally observed lower
dissociation energy, relative to the bulk value, can be explained by the influence of dislocation stress fields.

Future work will focus on extending the current framework to evaluate 1) HenVm diffusion in Fe–Cr alloys
(BCC) using the Fe–He–Cr MTP potential developed in this project, and investigate the effect of Cr on
diffusion mechanisms and barriers; in bulk, under applied stress, and in the presence of dislocations, to
assess the effect of crystal structure. 2) Cr segregation behavior around <001> self-interstitial loop with
different Fe-Cr Machine learning interatomic potential. These studies will establish a mechanistic
understanding of He–vacancy cluster diffusion and Cr segregation behavior at defects, advancing our
understanding of high-temperature helium embrittlement (HTHE) in structural materials.

9.3 INTEGRATED MODELING OF THE CRITICAL PROCESSES GOVERNING D/T RETENTION IN PFM
— T. Sizyuk (Argonne National Laboratory)

The objective of this work is to elucidate (1) dynamics of D/T retention in W-based materials and (2) defects
evolution based on self-consistent integrated modeling of major processes occurring on the material
surfaces exposed to D/T plasma. This work includes models’ development and benchmarking against
various experiments on D retention and type, size, and density of evolving defects in tungsten-based
materials.

SUMMARY
Detailed models of bubbles/blisters growth from nanocavities, vacancy formation on the surface induced
by D supersaturation, and variation in D diffusivity depending on changes in material microstructure were
developed and integrated with models of collisional interactions on the surface, diffusion, desorption, and
trapping/detrapping implemented in the ITMC-DYN package [1, 2]. Simulations, using this advanced
integration of models and processes, provide accurate explanations of various experimental results. Self-
consistent simulations show synergistic effects of blistering and D supersaturated surface layer (DSSL)
formation on dynamics of D retention and transport to the bulk of W irradiated at different D fluences. These
models can accurately predict T retention and material degradation under various operating scenarios of
the fusion device.

Recently, we upgraded ITMC-DYN package that integrates many important processes relevant to plasma-
material interactions in fusion reactors [1,2]. The package includes detailed Monte Carlo models of ion/atom
collisions using various interatomic potential functions, particles thermal diffusion and segregation in near-
surface layers, chemical and physical erosion, hydrogen isotope trapping/detrapping, molecular surface
recombination and desorption, and calculation of rate coefficients in multi-component materials based on
the dynamic material composition. The collisional processes responsible for target atoms sputtering, mixing,
and particles reflection are therefore integrated with detailed models for temperature-dependent processes
leading to temporal and spatial material evolution. All models are integrated in a self-consistent manner that
allows accurate simulation of realistic and specific experimental conditions and parameters, such as
multiple and simultaneous ions composition, energies, and fluxes; material characteristics and temperature;
and the experimental setup.
During the reported performance period, several new models were implemented in the package to simulate
hydrogen isotopes interactions with metallic PFMs. These models include detailed mechanisms of
bubbles/blisters growth from nanocavities, conditions for blisters burst, vacancy formation induced by D
supersaturation, and changes in D diffusivity depending on changes in material microstructure.
Modeling of blisters growth is based on the derived equations for pressurized cavity development [3]. To
calculate the flow of D to the cavity, we used the equation for equilibrium concentration of D in the cavity
and D in the solution [4].
We developed an algorithm to simulate blister growth based on the above equations as well as using
equations of state of deuterium [5]. In the developed algorithm, initial distribution of void-type defects as
starting points for blisters is used. We calculate the defects distribution in depth and consider areal density
of defects. Defect growth at each location in depth is simulated individually, however, the growth process
depends on evolution of defects in other locations and their effects on D diffusion and trapping on its way
to each defect. Contribution of each defect to total traps and to D concentration with depth is summarized.
We also developed algorithms to simulate DSSL formation. This method is based on the mechanism of
hydrogen-induced superabundant vacancy formation described by Ohsawa, et al [6]. It was shown, using
DFT and thermodynamic calculations, that the formation of second vacancy in W is energetically more
favorable than trapping of 12 D atoms in monovacancy [6]. From this, we can explain enhanced D retention
on the surface by increased probability of vacancy formation at high concentration of mobile D, exceeding
the solubility limit.

 177

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

We consider two major types of intrinsic defects in our simulations of D diffusion and retention. Small
defects, e.g., monovacancy, small vacancy clusters, or dislocations are treated in simulations using the rate
theory of trapping/detrapping. Up to 12 D atoms can be trapped at each point in these defects. Our previous
analysis of experimental results predicted concentration of small intrinsic defects in hot-rolled W pre-
annealed at 1300 K. Another type of intrinsic defects includes large vacancy clusters or nanovoids which
are considered in simulations as starting points for bubbles/blisters growth.
Our modeling of bubbles/blisters growth and related D retention mechanisms includes, among others,
algorithms for the distribution of intrinsic defects in depth and uses areal density of these defects as
illustrated in Figure 1.

Figure 1. Schematics of intrinsic defects distribution for simulations of bubbles/blisters growth.
Detailed description of recently developed models implemented in the upgraded package, ITMC-DYN+,
and various simulation results will be given in the paper [7].
Results
Major recent results of this research include (1) development and integration of described above models
and (2) benchmarking of developed models at D fluxes and material temperatures relevant to the conditions
at the walls in fusion device. We studied W surface response to D irradiation at flux of 1020 D m−2s−1, 38 eV
energy, and at 370 K material temperature. Defects evolution and D retention were studied at various
fluences; results were compared with experimental data [8] regarding the spatial distribution of trapped D
and size and density of formed blisters. These simulations show bubbles/blisters growth dynamics and their
effects on trapping sites accumulation. ITMC-DYN+ simulation results and experimental data [8] of spatial
distribution of trapped D at three fluences are shown in Figures 2. Simulations are in good agreement with
experimental results obtained using NRA [8]. Figures 2 show also simulation results of blister sizes with
depth at different fluences. These results were compared with average blister sizes at similar fluences
observed in experiments [8]. Average blister sizes in these experiments are (a) hundreds of nanometers at
3x1022 D/m2 fluence, (b) ~3 µm at 6x1022 D/m2 fluence, and (c) ~6 µm at 3x1023 D/m2 fluence. Simulations
show maximum diameters of blisters as ~1 µm (Fig. 2a), ~3 µm (Fig. 2b), and ~7.5 µm (Fig. 2c) at similar
fluences correspondently.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. ITMC-DYN+ simulated depth distribution of trapped D and related blister sizes at three different
fluences (a) 3x1022 D/m2, (b) 6x1022 D/m2, (c) 3x1023 D/m2. Experimental data for benchmarking are from
[8]. Average blister sizes in experiments are on the range of hundreds of nanometers at 3x1022 D/m2
fluence, ~3 µm at 6x1022 D/m2 fluence, and ~6 µm at 3x1023 D/m2 fluence.
We studied the integrated effects of blisters growth and formation of dislocation networks around the blisters
on D retention. Dense clouds of dislocation networks surrounding blisters in W irradiated by D were found
in experiments [9]. Our simulations show that significant amount of D can be trapped in dislocation networks
formed around the blisters, at studied material temperature of 370K. Figure 3a shows ITMC-DYN+
simulation results of distribution of trapped D (at 3x1023 D/m2 fluence) between different types of defects
including (1) retention in vacancies and/or dislocations (on the surface of material and on the inner surfaces
of blisters), (2) retention inside the blister (in the form of both atoms and molecules), (3) retention in
dislocation networks surrounding the blisters. These simulations include conditions for blisters bursting with
the release of D accumulated inside the cavity. This explains low D concentrations in the sub-surface layers,
around 100-500 nm from the surface. Figure 3b shows distribution and sizes of blisters at the same fluence.
Burst blisters are indicated by blue lines. For better visualization of blisters sizes in this figure, we increased
the number of blisters along surface direction in ~6 times in comparison with real number of blisters in
simulations.

 179

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. ITMC-DYN+ simulated (a) distribution of trapped D in different types of defects and (b) related
blisters distribution and sizes at 3x1023 D/m2 fluence. Experimental data for benchmarking are from [8]. The
number of blisters along surface direction is ~6 times larger than real number of blisters in simulations.
The above results highlight the importance of self-consistent simulations including modeling of all processes
governing D/T transport and trapping in materials. These simulations help predict dynamics of T retention
at different scenarios of fusion device operation.

The above models and simulations will be extended to study properties of DSSL and its effects on D
diffusivity and retention in the bulk of material. We will benchmark these models at different D fluxes and
material temperatures as well as at various dpa in PFM.

9.4 MODELING OF BULK AND RADIATION INDUCED DEFECTS PROPERTIES OF TiB2 — Y. Osetskiy
(ORNL), G. Samolyuk (ORNL)

The objective of this task is to obtain an understanding of the bulk and radiation induced properties of TiB2
and mechanisms operating at atomic scale in TiB2 under neutron irradiation. This includes defects
dynamics, interactions, growth and gas (H, He) transport and accumulation mechanisms.

SUMMARY
The following defects and effects are under consideration: a) point defects and small clusters, their
interactions and mobility, b) extended defects such as dislocations and dislocation loops, stacking faults
and grain boundaries, and c) microstructure evolution mechanisms under irradiation and its effect to the
material mechanical and physical properties. Main techniques applied are density functional theory (DFT)
for modeling defect properties, classical and DFT molecular dynamics (MD) for modeling defect dynamics,
machine learning approach for developing classical interatomic force fields.

We utilize a first principle-based approach to study basic properties of TiB2, including bulk physical
properties, radiation induced defects, and their interactions. The calculations have been executed within
the density-functional theory [1] using the generalized gradient approximation (GGA) and the Perdew-
Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) [2] parametrization of the electronic exchange-correlation functional. The electronic
structure was calculated using the plane-wave basis projector augmented-wave approach [3] as
implemented in the Vienna Ab-initio Simulation Package (VASP) [8]. The high accuracy of modeling results
was provided by the high inner energy cut-off, 520 eV, and low ionic energy convergency criterion, 10-6 eV.
Supercells of different sizes, up to 648 lattice sites, were used to investigate cell size effects on defects’
energy and structure.
Results
First, the calculations have confirmed that basic properties of point defects, vacancy, and interstitial atoms
in both sublattices, are well converged in supercell with less than ~400 atoms. This can be seen in Figure
1, where size dependences of vacancy and interstitials atoms formation energy in Ti and Be sublattices are
presented.
Configurations of the most stable Ti and B interstitial atoms are shown in Figs.2 and 3. Total energy of
supercells and formation energies of defects modelled in the largest system, 648 lattice sites, are presented
in the Table 1.

For understanding dislocation loops evolution and degradation of mechanical stability under irradiation we
plan to:
 - Model loop growing in different planes for understanding their evolution.
 - Model mobility and diffusion of B- and Ti- interstitial atoms. We expect that anisotropy in their
 diffusion can explain anisotropic radiation growth of TiB2 under neutron irradiation.
For predicting H (D) and He retention and desorption needs, we plan to:
 - Model vacancy clusters and their interactions with D(H) and He atoms accumulating binding energy
 for Vn-Dm (-Hem) vacancies-gas atoms combinations.
 - Apply ab initio MD for modeling D(H) and He diffusion in the TiB2 bulk.
 - Develop a kinetic Monte Carlo model describing vacancy-gas clusters evolution during both
 growing and desorption processes. The former will model microstructure evolution, the later wil be
 used for modeling desorption experiments.

9.5 FIRST PRINCIPLES STUDY OF DEUTERIUM MOBILITY in TiB2 — Y.Osetskiy, G. Samolyuk, L.
Nuckols, M. Eisenbach, R. Messerly, J. Rapp (ORNL)

The objective of the LDRD SEED project “Computational Framework for Modeling Titanium Diboride
Chemical Erosion by Plasma” is to obtain an understanding of the chemical erosion during D-plasma
interaction with diboride-based Ultra High Temperature Ceramics (UHTC).

SUMMARY
The developed framework is validated against experimentally derived surface chemistry evolution from
plasma exposure experiments. This is be the first, comprehensive computational machine learning-based
framework for developing multiscale models capable for experimental validation. The new interatomic force
fields are used to model physical erosion directly by molecular dynamics. For chemical erosion, the possible
chemical reactions and their rates for realistic plasma-material interaction conditions will be modelled by
combining kinetic Monte Carlo approach, chemical rate theory with parameters defined by ab initio and
reactive molecular dynamics calculations.

We utilize a first principle-based approach to study basic properties of TiB2, including bulk physical
properties, radiation induced defects, and their interactions. Electronic structures and energies have been
calculated within the density-functional theory [1] using the generalized gradient approximation (GGA) and
the Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) [2] parametrization of the electronic exchange-correlation functional.
Total energies and electronic structure were calculated using the plane-wave basis projector augmented-
wave approach [3] as implemented in the Vienna Ab-initio Simulation Package (VASP) [4].
We have worked on constructing classical MM force fields (FFs) from first-principles molecular dynamics
data. Constructing robust force fields that reproduce the behavior observed in first-principles calculations
is a multi-step process that starts with generating first-principles training data. For this purpose, first-
principles MD trajectories were generated using the VASP code for relaxation of the following TiB2 systems,
which were subsequently used for fitting the force fields: ideal TiB2, multiple configurations with two, three,
and four interstitial boron atoms, and trajectories for melting simulations.
Accumulating a computational database of TiB2 properties for constructing interatomic FFs involves
modeling complex processes such as surface reactions, defect dynamics, and interactions. Among them,
we emphasize the importance of phenomena occurring on the plasma-facing surface. Our initial modeling
of chemical reactions involving D, Ti and B on TiB2 shows that chemical erosion on the crystalline surface
is virtually impossible due to the very high stability of crystalline TiB2. However, under realistic plasma
bombardment conditions, alterations in surface binding behavior and ~few nm deep amorphization
formation layer is likely due to ballistic interactions with the plasma particles, unavoidable trace plasma
impurities, and with possible redeposition from sputtered materials [5]. . The presence of the amorphous
layer may influence the overall evolution of PMI and, in particular, the chemical erosion. The following
mechanisms may be important here: defect dynamics in amorphous layers and near the interface with bulk
crystalline materials, deuterium transfer from the bulk of the crystal to the amorphous surface and chemical
reactions on the amorphous surface involving plasma ions and atoms present on the surface, such as Ti,
B, D.
Results
In a first step, we simulate D transport in crystalline and amorphous TiB2 using ab initio molecular dynamics
(AI-MD). This requires extensive simulations over a wide temperature range and may only be possible using
the supercomputing capabilities of the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF) and the National
Energy Research Center (NERSC). The initial results are presented in Fig. 1 as Arrhenius plots for the
temperature-dependent deuterium diffusion coefficients in crystalline and amorphous TiB2. The results
obtained for the D mobility in these materials have not yet been published in the literature. It is shown that
the diffusion coefficient of D is significantly lower in amorphous ceramics, for example, the ratio of the
diffusion coefficients in the amorphous to crystalline state extrapolated to 1000 K is ~0.02. This is reflected
in the significant difference in the diffusion activation energies shown in the figures: 0.92 eV in the

 185

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

amorphous vs 0.42 eV in the crystalline states. These unique results assume an important role of the
crystal/amorphous interface in the whole PMI phenomenon..

Figure 1. Temperature dependence of D diffusion coefficient in crystalline and amorphous TiB2 (symbols).
The activation energies indicate in the inset are obtained by an Arrhenius treatment (lines).

Future work considers the following steps:
 - Surface amorphization should occur due to the transfer of implanted D ions to the surface.
 - Reactions on the amorphous surface have a wide range of energies (=> rates). It is assumed that
 low energy reactions are the mechanisms of chemical erosion and they will be studied extensively.
 - The best accuracy was demonstrated by the HIP-NN force fields. A proposal for their extensive
 testing on FRONTIER within the DD program is submitted.
 - An extended large-scale MD simulation program for the direct modeling temperature dependent
 chemical reactions on the TiB2 amorphous surface using the developed force fields and rate theory
 model.
 - The influence of oxygen and other plasma impurities.

9.6 COMPUTATIONAL MODELING OF COMPOSITION-DEPENDENT DISLOCATION MOBILITY IN Fe-
Cr ALLOYS — Kieran Edmonds, Manikandan Muruganandam, Anter El-Azab (Purdue University),
Hyunsoo Lee , Enrique Martinez (Clemson University), Tomohisa Kumagai (Central Research Institute of
Electric Power Industry)

Ferritic steels, based on the binary Fe-Cr system, are leading candidates in the search for structural
materials for use in nuclear fusion reactors due to their low activation and good mechanical strength at
higher temperatures. However, they are still susceptible to irradiation hardening and embrittlement, due to
the creation of defects and composition fluctuations [1].
For this reason, we aim to develop a composition-aware multiscale model for strength and yielding in this
system. This model will be grounded in discrete dislocation dynamics (DDD), which itself must be grounded
in atomistics via its mobility law. The latter law relates the Peach-Koehler force on a dislocation to its velocity.
In order to be accurate, the mobility law must take into account the mechanical and chemical features of
the material of interest, subject to the relevant environmental conditions.
There have been numerous attempts to extract mobility laws for use in DDD, see, for example, [2-4].
However, the focus has mainly been on pure metals and limited alloys for screw and edge dislocations,
with most attention on FCC systems. What is needed is a systematic understanding of dislocation mobility
in BCC solid solution alloys, and across a wide range of dislocation characters. We propose to bridge this
gap starting with the Fe-Cr system. Building on the investigation of the FeCrAl system by Kumagai et al.
[5], our foremost task is to obtain a thorough understanding of mobility in the homogeneous Fe-Cr system
with novel Cr concentrations.

SUMMARY
Molecular dynamics simulations were performed to characterize dislocation mobility in Fe–Cr alloys across
a range of compositions, temperatures, stresses, and character angles. Results show that the mobility
follows linear viscous behavior at low velocities and becomes nonlinear at higher stresses. We make use
of a piecewise empirical mobility law to reflect this behavior. Increasing Cr concentration raises friction
stress and reduces mobility. Mobility also decreases as dislocations transition from edge to screw character.
Temperature effects show lower friction stress but higher phonon drag at elevated temperatures. These
trends provide a foundation for developing composition-dependent mobility laws for use in discrete
dislocation dynamics simulations.

Computational procedure
For the [111](11� 0) slip system and desired angle 𝜃𝜃 between the Burgers vector b and dislocation line
direction ξ, the supercell is oriented such that 𝑧𝑧 ∥ [11� 0], 𝑦𝑦 ∥ 𝜉𝜉̂, and 𝑥𝑥 is parallel to the glide direction.
Molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are run with LAMMPS [6] and an EAM Finnis–Sinclair (FS) type
interatomic potential for the FeCrAl system [7]. The cell size is 50 x 18 x 20 nm, with custom shifted periodic
boundary conditions (SPBCs) along 𝑥𝑥, PBCs along 𝑦𝑦, and free surfaces along 𝑧𝑧 with four fixed atomic
planes at the top and bottom. These slabs are then sheared along the direction of b for 300 ps, with the
middle atoms integrated and thermostatted at the desired temperature with NVT. The system is given 100
ps to reach steady state, and then dislocation position, velocity, and shape are measured over time with a
common neighbor analysis.

Results

 187

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Preliminary findings from these bulk MD runs for dislocation mobility can be interpreted with respect to alloy
composition, character angle, and temperature effects.
At lower dislocation velocities, mobility can be treated as a linear relationship mediated by viscous drag, of
the form 𝑣𝑣(𝜏𝜏) = 𝑏𝑏�𝐵𝐵 𝜏𝜏 = 𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚, where 𝑣𝑣 is the dislocation velocity, 𝑏𝑏 is the Burgers vector, 𝐵𝐵 is the viscous
drag coefficient (with inverse 𝑚𝑚 the mobility coefficient), and 𝜏𝜏 is the resolved shear stress on the
dislocation. However, at higher velocities, the relationship between 𝑣𝑣 and 𝜏𝜏 becomes increasingly nonlinear.
One cause of this is relativistic effects as the dislocation velocity approaches the shear wave speed of the
crystal from below. To account for this, nonlinear forms for the mobility law in which the relation reduces to
linear at low velocities and saturates to an asymptotic value of 𝑣𝑣 have been proposed in the past [8, 9].
In a similar fashion, we consider nonlinear algebraic saturating functions as part of our piecewise mobility
framework. The analysis presented here is done using the following empirical form:
 0 𝜏𝜏 < 𝜏𝜏𝑓𝑓
 𝑣𝑣(𝜏𝜏) = � 𝛽𝛽𝜏𝜏eff
 𝛼𝛼 𝜏𝜏𝑓𝑓 ≤ 𝜏𝜏
                                                               �1 + (𝛽𝛽𝜏𝜏eff )2
where 𝜏𝜏eff = 𝜏𝜏 − 𝜏𝜏𝑓𝑓 , 𝛼𝛼(𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶, 𝜃𝜃, 𝑇𝑇), and 𝛽𝛽(𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶, 𝜃𝜃, 𝑇𝑇) are empirical parameters that are fit via a multi-start nonlinear
least-squares procedure (see figure 1).

Figure 1. Example of our nonlinear empirical form fitted to glide velocity vs shear stress data as a function
of alloy composition for a 54.74° character angle mixed dislocation at 300 K.
We analyze our data by considering 𝜏𝜏𝑓𝑓 (𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶, 𝜃𝜃, 𝑇𝑇) and the linear mobility 𝑚𝑚(𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶, 𝜃𝜃, 𝑇𝑇) in the low-velocity viscous
drag regime. This is done by evaluating the slope of the tangent line to the nonlinear fit at 𝜏𝜏 = 𝜏𝜏𝑓𝑓 .
Much work, both classical and modern, has been done on the effects of composition on strengthening [10,
11]. Though theoretical formulations differ, all agree that solute strengthening occurs because solutes pin
dislocations at locally favorable sites, which must be overcome for glide to begin. The novelty of this work
lies in a focus on solute effects on dislocation mobility once glide has begun, across a comprehensive range
of solute concentrations (see figure 2). In addition to an observed increase in the friction stress with
increasing Cr concentration, there is an observed decrease in mobility with increasing Cr concentration, as
we hypothesized. This is evidence that solutes play a statistically significant role on dislocation behavior
above yielding as well, via pinning effects on drag.

 188

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Mobility 𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 in the linear low-velocity limit as a function of Cr concentration, varied across
dislocation character angle at 300 K. The angles in parentheses are the absolute differences between the
character angle and 90°, which serves as an effective distance-from-pure-edge measure.
BCC is known for a discrepancy between the glide thresholds and mobilities of screw and edge dislocations,
with those of the screw dislocation being significantly higher than those of the edge dislocation [12]. As
shown in figure 3, we observe that mixed dislocations become increasingly less mobile as their character
angle moves away from 90° (pure edge) and toward 0° (pure screw). Trigonometric interpolations of
character angle dependence in BCC have been popular [5]. Such an approach would generally fit our data
well, although there exist deviations from a would-be smooth interpolation at certain character angles and
Cr concentrations, as well as some general asymmetry across mirror character angles.

Figure 3. Mobility 𝑚𝑚𝑚𝑚 in the linear low-velocity limit as a function of dislocation character angle, varied
across Cr concentration at 300 K.
In general, across all dislocation characters and alloy compositions, we see that the friction stress is higher
at 300 K than at 750 K (see figure 4). This is in agreement with theory, which understands yield strength to
have a temperature dependence, since thermal activation can lower the energy barrier to glide from lattice
resistance, solute pinning effects, and kink nucleation barriers [10]. Likewise, once in the glide regime, we
see that mobility tends to be higher at 300 K than at 750 K, due to the effect of increasing phonon drag with
increasing temperature. Our mobility law reflects these patterns well.

 189

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 4. Glide velocity vs shear stress data as a function of temperature for a 38.94° character angle mixed
dislocation in a Fe-15wt%Cr random alloy.

Bulk MD runs are finalizing for the upper extremes of Cr concentration. The immediate product of this
research will be an empirical mobility law for use in DDD across a wide range of chemical compositions
that may arise locally in Fe-Cr-based ferritic steels under irradiation. Additionally, we will consider observed
physical trends in dislocation behavior within the context of our empirical form and existing theory, with the
goal of moving toward a more phenomenological mobility framework.
Crucial to this end will be a thorough analysis of the unique behavior of screw dislocations in this BCC
system, as well as an examination of the explicit effects of nonrandom solute configurations, such as
irradiation-enhanced Cr clustering and 𝛼𝛼′ precipitation.

9.7 MODELING PLASTICITY-MEDIATED FLOW IN METALS WITH PRESSURIZED CAVITIES — Karl
Garbrecht, Laurent Capolungo (LANL)

The objective is to better understand and predict the bulk-scale mechanical creep behavior of porous
polycrystalline metals that have overpressurized cavities (e.g., irradiated metals with helium bubbles) by
quantifying the complex coupling among cavity aspects (e.g., size distribution, inhomogeneous
overpressure values, spatial arrangement) and matrix properties (e.g., rate-dependency, crystallographic
lattice) in metallic single crystals.

SUMMARY
The stress stress-strain response of polycrystals is dependent on the stress-strain response of its single
crystals. The response of single crystal is naturally dependent on the local mechanical fields, which are
governed by considerations like cavity aspects and matrix properties. The effects of these considerations
on the homogenized mechanical response of single crystals are not well understood. Therefore, this work
identifies which considerations can introduce the most variation in the constitutive response and uncover
the underlying mechanisms driving the variation. This was accomplished using the full-field crystal plasticity
code (LApx) that has been modified to simulate the mechanical response of porous single crystals with
overpressurized cavities. A coupling between cavity arrangement and lattice orientation was uncovered that
introduced up to 7-fold variation in the stress-strain response among the microstructures simulated. This
understanding enables development of synthetic microstructures that introduce less bias and will improve
accuracy of creep life predictions.

The principal tasks completed include an expanded set of simulations motivated by previous findings and
analysis of the effective hydrostatic response of microstructures and local mechanical fields. Hydrostatic
stress loading conditions are used because the effective triaxiality (T) in the case of overpressurized cavities
can be much greater than that computed solely from the external loading. Specifically, the effective triaxiality
is given by the sum of the volumetric average cavity overpressure in addition to the external loading. Thus,
microstructures with overpressurized cavities under deviatoric external loading can still experience
relatively high T.
Analyzing local fields from different synthetic microstructures is required to quantify the potential variation
cavity aspects and matrix properties can introduce into the homogenized stress-strain response and to
identify the underlying drivers. To this end, we compare four cavity spatial arrangements that are
characterized as semi-random (SR), ordered grid (OG), body-centered cubic atomic arrangement motif
(BCM), and face-centered cubic atomic arrangement motif (FCM) shown in Figure 1. The semi-random
arrangement has two cavity size distributions (one shown in this report), polydispersed and monodispersed,
which is denoted as SR-Poly and SR-Mono, respectively. The other spatial arrangements are
monodispersed, which is denoted as BCM-Mono and FCM-Mono. Overpressure distributions in the SR-
Poly, for example, are simulated with both a cavity size dependent overpressure, a homogeneous
overpressure, and voids that denoted as SR-Poly-I, SR-Poly-H, and SR-Poly-V, respectively. The
monodispersed microstructures all have a homogeneous overpressure (e.g., BCM-Mono-H).

 192

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1: Cavity size distribution and spatial arrangements of cavities with color-coded overpressure values
for microstructures with f=0.0188. Note that the body centered motif (BCM) and face centered motif (FCM)
microstructures have had the cavities shifted compared to their typical representation. This is so that
cavities are not placed at the corners of the microstructure, which is a requirement for using the conformal
mapping process.

Results
Results are summarized using normalized strain rates where the strain rates from reference microstructures
that have the same effective hydrostatic driving force, effective porosity value, and strain rate sensitivity are
used for normalization. As the hydrostatic driving force is the same, the normalized strain rates are
equivalent to normalized compliance. For example, the strain rate from an OG-Mono-H microstructure with
f=0.0188, n=7, Σm =250 MPa and P=53.3 MPa can be used to normalize the strain rates from an SR-Mono-
H, SR-Poly-H, and SR-Poly-I microstructures with the same f, n, and Σm + P to evaluate different cavity
spatial arrangements.
Results indicate that creep life predictions of porous polycrystalline materials can be significantly biased by
synthetic microstructures. Specifically, Figure 2 summarizes that up to seven-fold variation in the
instantaneous effective stress-strain response was observed among the microstructures compared, which
all have the same effective porosity and strain rate sensitivity. The underlying mechanism driving the
variation shown in Figure 2 is a coupling between cavity spatial arrangement and lattice orientation for
which the magnitude of the effects on compliance increase with porosity and the strain rate sensitivity
exponent value. The practical significance of these results is that when porosity is below approximately 2%,
an OG-Mono microstructure with a matrix in the ⟨100⟩ can reasonably approximate a more realistic SR-
Poly microstructure in any lattice orientation and strain rate sensitivity. However, for higher porosity values,
use of simpler microstructures to approximate more realistic ones is expected to introduce bias. Thus, more
realistic microstructures (i.e., SR-Poly) should be used or bias from idealized microstructures (i.e. OG-
Mono) needs to be accounted for when calibrating constitutive models.
Results also indicate that it is possible to approximate the effective stress-strain behavior of different cubic
single crystals based on numerical simulations of other cubic lattice structures. Specifically, when
comparing an OG-Mono-V (i.e., ordered grid with voids) embedded in different cubic lattice structures,
Figure 3 shows that the effective compliance is the same for FCC and BCC1 (i.e., BCC {110}⟨111⟩ ) and that
the maximum variation is less than 1.2x when accounting for total number of slip systems. Note that the
total number of slip systems, the BCC3 and BCCall have 24 and 48 slip systems, respectively, as opposed
to 12 in the rest. So, the absolute strain rate in the BCC3 and BCCall is first divided by two and four,
respectively, before normalization.
Furthermore, it was found that the highest von Mises equivalent stress concentrations occur at the cavity
matrix interface in 12 locations. This is shown by Figure 4, which includes a threshold plot of von Mises
equivalent stress concentrations for two lattice orientations. Since plot a in Figure 4 is for a matrix in the
⟨100⟩ orientation, these 12 stress concentrations occur where the cavity surface normals are in the ⟨110⟩
directions. Plot b in Figure 4 shows the same set of plots, for a matrix in the ⟨110⟩ orientation. Comparison
of Plots a and b indicates that the lattice orientation governs the locations of these stress concentrations
and that they rotate with the lattice.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 4: Coupling between cavity arrangement and lattice orientation in ordered grid monodispersed (OG-
Mono) and semi-randomly arranged polydispersed (SR-Poly) microstructures for two lattice orientations.
The key point is that the coupling, as evidenced by the von Mises equivalent stress fields, is observed in
the OG-Mono and the SR-Poly microstructures. Moreover, the locations of stress concentrations, which are
governed in part by the lattice orientation, can be found in the same locations and orientations relative to
each cavity in the OG-Mono and SR-Poly microstructures. All plots are for an effective porosity of 0.018, a
strain rate sensitivity exponent of 7, and a BCC1 crystal structure. Plot a) shows cavities embedded in a
⟨100⟩ lattice orientation, where the top left depicts a threshold plot of 12 von Mises equivalent stress
concentrations around a cavity in an ordered grid monodispersed (OG-Mono) microstructure, the bottom
left depicts a slice plot of the von Mises equivalent stress field through the center of the same microstructure,
and the right depicts a slice plot through a Sr-Poly microstructure. Plot b) shows cavities embedded in a
⟨110⟩ lattice orientation, where the top left depicts a threshold plot of 12 von Mises equivalent stress

left depicts a slice plot of the von Mises equivalent stress field through the center of the same microstructure,
and the right depicts a slice plot through a Sr-Poly microstructure.

Future work consists of integrating the uncovered coupling between cavity arrangement and lattice
orientation into creep life predictions for porous metals with overpressurized cavities. Current polycrystalline
simulations are insensitive to this coupling; however, they indicate that the local stress-strain response of
single crystals govern creep life predictions (Figure 5 and 6). Figure 5 and 6 indicates that heterogenous
porosity growth in specific single crystal voxels (i.e., cubic elements) compounds over time, ultimately
leading to failure. Therefore, improving the representativeness or better accounting for bias in synthetic
single crystal microstructures used for model development and calibration will enable more accurate
microstructure-informed polycrystalline simulations for predicting creep life.

9.8 A SPATIALLY-RESOLVED MODEL OF NEUTRON-IRRADIATED TUNGSTEN COUPLING
STOCHASTIC CLUSTER DYNAMICS AND FINITE DEFORMATION PLASTICITY— Giacomo Po,
Sabyasachi Chatterjee (University of Miami), Qianran Yu, Jaime Marian (University of California Los
Angeles), Yang Li (Shanghai University), Kenneth Roche (Pacific Northwest National Laboratory).

ABSTRACT
Structural materials used in nuclear reactors face severe degradation in mechanical properties, such as
hardening and embrittlement. At the microscopic scale, this occurs due to the creation and accumulation of
irradiation-induced defects and their interaction with system dislocations. Although techniques exist which
can model evolution of irradiation defects, for instance kinetic transport theory-based models, their
interaction with mechanical deformation of the bulk material has not been investigated extensively. In this
work, we demonstrate a novel spatially-resolved multiscale coupling between microscopic irradiation defect
evolution, modeled using Stochastic Cluster Dynamics (SCD) and macroscopic mechanical deformation
modeled using a finite-deformation plasticity model. SCD is used to determine the statistically averaged
defect cluster spacing, dependent on operating conditions such as irradiation dose and temperature. This
acts as an initial condition that governs the critical resolved shear stress of dislocation glide in the
macroscopic plasticity model. This framework is used to predict mechanical behavior in post-mortem test
of irradiated Tungsten samples, which has found its importance as structural material used in nuclear
reactors. The results obtained using the coupled approach are in good agreement with experimental data
of uniaxial tension tests. The model is able to capture the eﬀect of temperature and irradiation dose on the
material hardening. Two methods are proposed to estimate hardness – using Tabor’s Law relating uniaxial
yield stress to hardness and from flat-punch simulations. The results are in reasonable agreement with
hardness data from micro-indentation experiments of irradiated Tungsten samples. The model is also able
to reveal microstructural details such as spatial variation in defect density and local stress.

[1] Chatterjee, Sabyasachi, Qianran Yu, Yang Li, Kenneth Roche, Jaime Marian, and Giacomo Po. "A
spatially-resolved model of neutron-irradiated tungsten coupling stochastic cluster dynamics and finite
deformation plasticity." Journal of nuclear materials 605 (2025): 155526.

 197

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9.9 COUPLED CLUSTER AND DISLOCATION DYNAMICS MODELING OF MICROSTRUCTURE
EVOLUTION IN IRRADIATED MATERIALS — Giacomo Po, Matthew Maron, Inam Lalani (University of
Miami), Kristopher Baker, Benjamin Ramirez Flores (Knolls Atomic Power Laboratory), Thomas Black,
James Hollenbeck (Bettis Atomic Power Laboratory), Yang Li (Shanghai University), Nasr Ghoniem
(University of California Los Angeles)

ABSTRACT
We develop here a coupled cluster and dislocation dynamics framework to study the microstructure
evolution of irradiated materials. The framework not only accounts for the three-dimensional diffusion of
radiation-generated clusters, but also their interaction with dislocation networks and the resultant climb
motion of discrete dislocations within finite crystals. The framework is solved with a superposition solution
scheme, and is applied to investigate the evolution of the irradiation-induced dislocation loops in zirconium
(Zr), considering the effects of various bias factors including the diffusion anisotropy difference (DAD) of
interstitials and interstitial clusters, the dislocation bias of defects to discrete dislocation segments, and the
production bias of defects from the radiation cascade. We find that the DAD is the most critical factor
influencing the kinetics of the loop evolution in Zr, while the recombination/interaction of mobile defects can
induce a strong spatial dependence of the loop evolution together with the DAD. The method is also adopted
to study the evolution of interstitial ⟨𝑎𝑎⟩ and vacancy ⟨𝑐𝑐⟩ dislocation loop ensembles consistent with the
microstructure observed during irradiation-induced growth of Zr. Our findings not only reveal the spatial
dependence of the size and ellipticity of the dislocation loops, but also suggest a limit on the anisotropy
factor of interstitials to reproduce the co-growth of ⟨𝑎𝑎⟩ and ⟨𝑐𝑐⟩ loops in zirconium, in good agreement with
experimental observations and other simulation results.

[1] Li, Yang, Matthew Maron, Kristopher Baker, Benjamin Ramirez Flores, Thomas Black, James
Hollenbeck, Inam Lalani, Nasr Ghoniem, and Giacomo Po. "Coupled cluster and dislocation dynamics
modeling of microstructure evolution in irradiated materials." Journal of the Mechanics and Physics of
Solids(2025): 106366.

 198

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9.10 SPATIALLY-RESOLVED CLUSTER DYNAMICS MODELING OF IRRADIATION GROWTH —
Giacomo Po, Matthew Maron, Inam Lalani (University of Miami), Kristopher Baker, Benjamin Ramirez
Flores (Knolls Atomic Power Laboratory), Thomas Black, James Hollenbeck (Bettis Atomic Power
Laboratory), Yang Li (Shanghai University), Nasr Ghoniem (University of California Los Angeles)

ABSTRACT
We develop here a spatially resolved, three-dimensional continuum model coupling cluster dynamics (SR-
CD) and crystal plasticity to investigate irradiation growth in zirconium. The model uses scale separation to
divide the population of the irradiation cluster into mobile and immobile families. Small interstitial and
vacancy clusters are modeled using anisotropic reaction–diffusion equations. Among the immobile clusters,
an atomistically-informed vacancy cluster to vacancy loop transition is taken into account. The coupling
between the evolution equation of CD and the plastic deformation of the material is two-fold, with stress-
informed bias factors and local inelastic strains computed from the evolution of the evolving cluster
population. The numerical implementation of the model utilizes the finite element method to analyze both
single-crystal and polycrystalline samples. The growth strains that are computed align well with the
experimental data provided by Carpenter for single-crystal Zr. Furthermore, the transformation of a vacancy
cluster into a complete vacancy loop, occurring at a size of 14 nm, is in agreement with experimental
observations and atomistic simulations. The density, size, and growth rate of the dislocation loops, denoted
as ⟨𝑐𝑐⟩ and ⟨𝑎𝑎⟩, also exhibit good agreement with transmission electron microscopy (TEM) analysis of
irradiated Zr and its alloys. Our findings demonstrate that there is a spatial correlation between the growth
of these dislocation loops and growth strains, significantly influenced by the crystal size. To explain the
expansion of the ⟨𝑎𝑎⟩ axis and the contraction of the ⟨𝑐𝑐⟩ axis in irradiated Zr, it is necessary to consider the
diffusion anisotropy difference (DAD) of mobile interstitial species. We show that the PWR Kearns
parameters, specifically 𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓 = 0.63, 𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓 = 0.32, 𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓 = 0.05, confer enhanced irradiation resistance to Zr along
the principal directions when compared to single crystals. Additionally, reducing the grain size to nanograins
further enhances the resistance to irradiation-induced growth, particularly along the direction with the
highest volume fraction of basal poles [0001].

[1] Maron, Matthew, Yang Li, Inam Lalani, Kristopher Baker, Benjamin Ramirez Flores, Thomas Black,
James Hollenbeck, Nasr Ghoniem, and Giacomo Po. "Spatially-resolved cluster dynamics modeling of
irradiation growth." International journal of plasticity 177 (2024): 103989.

 199

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

9.11 UNRAVELING TRANSMUTATION EFFECTS IN TUNGSTEN-BASED PLASMA-FACING
MATERIALS — D. Cereceda (Villanova University)

This Early Career project aims to unravel the transmutation effects in tungsten-based plasma-facing
materials (PFMs) by integrating radiation-induced transmutation, first-principles DFT electronic structure
calculations, and machine learning into a coherent materials design framework. The PI's proposed strategy
to pursue this goal starts by integrating inventory codes and first-principles electronic structure calculations
to predict the chemical composition, the atomistic arrangement, and the thermomechanical properties of
candidate PFMs as their composition changes during the course of irradiation due to transmutation.

SUMMARY
In previous years of this project, we have already: (i) predicted a detailed sequence of nuclide concentration
maps showing the time evolution of the composition of five tungsten-based PFMs (W, two W-based high-
entropy alloys, and two W-based smart alloys) from the beginning of their operational life until the end of
ten continuous full-power years under the expected conditions of the DEMO prototype fusion reactor [1, 2];
(ii) identified the most energetically stable atomistic configurations, including their total energies, short-
range order, and local segregation information, for the five W-based PFMs at the beginning of their
operational life [3]; and investigating the thermo-mechanical response of these energetically favorable
configurations as they evolve due to transmutation, using first-principles density functional theory (DFT)
calculations [4]. During the current reporting period, we focused on accelerating the discovery of minimum
energy configurations (MECs) [5], a critical preliminary step in investigating the local chemical environment
and energetics of transmuting tungsten-based PFMs.

Earlier computational works have successfully coupled density functional theory (DFT) calculations with
Monte-Carlo (MC) sampling in an approach (denoted as MC-DFT hereinafter) that allows for the discovery
of minimum energy configurations (MECs). While the MC-DFT approach is suitable for identifying MECs, it
requires large cells, a substantial number of initial independent samples, and numerous MC swaps to
converge to a realistic atomistic configuration. To overcome the above bottlenecks, we proposed a novel
computational approach that integrates MC sampling, first-principles DFT calculations, and machine
learning techniques to accelerate the discovery of MECs. It is grounded in the principles of the Cluster
Expansion (CE) method and incorporates a Local Outlier Factor (LOF) model, which facilitates the rapid
and accurate prediction of the energy of the swapped configurations, enhancing the computational
efficiency of the traditional MC-DFT framework.
Figure 1 shows the details of the proposed accelerated MC-DFT algorithm (referred to as a-MCDFT
hereinafter) and its framework for evaluating the energy of a given atomistic structure. The main difference
between the traditional MC-DFT and the a-MCDFT methods lies in how they calculate the energy of the
swapped configurations. In the MC-DFT method, these energy evaluations are always performed using
DFT calculations in every MC step. For their part, in the a-MCDFT approach, a LOF module first compares
the newly swapped configuration with those in the training set and then, depending on their similarity,
calculates the energy of the system using a previously trained surrogate model or DFT calculations.

 200

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 1. (a) Detailed description of the MC-DFT algorithm. Highlighted is the step where the accelerated
MC-DFT (aMC-DFT) is applied; (b) detailed flowchart of the aMC-DFT for calculating the energy of the
swapped configuration, a loop that is repeated every MC step.

Once the model has been trained and the optimized set of hyperparameters has been chosen, we applied
the a-MCDFT method to find MECs of a tungsten-based WCrTiTa high-entropy alloy, our target alloy
system. The results are shown in Figure 2, which provides a comparative analysis of the MCDFT and a-
MCDFT methods, with a particular focus on their energy convergence, computational cost, and acceptance
rate.

Figure 2. Comparison of minimum energy convergence, computational cost, and acceptance rate among
different methods during Monte Carlo (MC) simulations.

 201

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

We aim to complete a comprehensive set of DFT calculations to predict the local chemical ordering,
energetics, and mechanical properties of all relevant chemical compositions and energetically stable
atomistic configurations of five W-based PFMs as they evolve through transmutation.

9.12 BENCHMARKING INTERATOMIC POTENTIALS TO ENHANCE ACCURACY IN MODELLING
FUSION MATERIALS — Mianzhen Mo, Umang Agarwal, Erika Hunting, Siegfried Glenzer (SLAC National
Accelerator Laboratory, Menlo Park, CA)

This project aims to validate interatomic potentials used in atomistic models of materials for nuclear fusion
power plant applications. The team is developing and applying a new class of pump-probe experiments,
using ultrafast electron diffraction (UED) and time-resolved X-ray scattering at SLAC, to measure the
physical and structural properties of fusion materials with atomic-scale spatial and temporal resolutions.
These measurements provide critical benchmarks and validation of large-scale molecular dynamics (MD)
simulations and improve their predictive accuracy for describing radiation damage and long-term materials
evolution and melting behavior in fusion plant environments.

SUMMARY
During the CY25 reporting period (Jan. – June), our efforts centered on preparing and executing the
Ultrafast Electron Diffuse Scattering (UEDS) measurements of W-Re alloys. First, we successfully
fabricated nanofilm samples from bulk single-crystal W–Re alloys using focused ion beam lift-out processes
at Stanford nanofabrication facilities, followed by helium implantation at LANL to approach neutron-
irradiation material conditions. Second, we completed a successful UEDS experiment on W–Re alloys at
SLAC’s MeV-UED facility, obtaining high-quality thermal diffuse scattering data and Deby-Waller factor
results that unveil the structural dynamics of W-Re alloys. Third, we submitted a manuscript on the ultrafast
melting processes of Cu, an elemental constituent material relevant for Cu-based heat-sink alloys in divertor
applications. In this work, we studied the structural dynamics of ultrafast-laser induced melting in Cu using
UED combined with MD simulations. We validated the EAM potential model developed by Sheng et al. that
shows excellent agreement with our experimental results.

The following summarizes our project progress made between January 2025 and June 2025.
• We successfully developed a method to extract single-crystal nanofilms from bulk W-Re alloys using
 the focused ion beam (FIB) instruments at Stanford Nano Shared Facilities (SNSF), c.f. Figure 1. This
 represents a critical step toward the proposed electron diffuse scattering measurements, which require
 (1) nanofilms to accommodate the limited penetration depth of electrons, and (2) single-crystal
 specimens to produce well-defined Brillouin zone boundaries on the diffraction patterns for resolving
 the diffuse scattering dynamics in momentum space. Using the FIB method, we made five lamellas in
 total, three of which are (100)-oriented single-crystal W-5Re alloys and the other two are W-10Re alloys
 of the same crystal orientation.
• We also prepared polycrystalline nanofilms of W-Re alloys for Debye-Waller factor (DWF)
 measurements. These alloy samples were 30-nm thick and made through co-sputter deposition at the
 CINT facility of LANL under user proposal #2024BU0030 (PI: Mo). Using this approach, we fabricated
 a series of polycrystalline W - xRe samples with x = 0, 5, 10 and 100 at %. These thin films, deposited
 on NaCl substrates, were subsequently transferred to TEM grids by a lift-off process in purified water.
 This transfer step was carried out at SLAC prior to the UED experiment.
• In addition, we performed helium implantation to some of the fabricated samples with the goal of
 studying helium effects on the lattice dynamics of W-Re alloys. This was conducted at the IBML facility
 of LANL under the user proposal #2024BU0030 (PI: Mo). The employed irradiation parameters are 25
 keV He+ ions at a fluence of 1.5e16 ions/cm2. The helium and vacancy concentration are calculated to
 be ~0.6% and ~1.5% respectively based on the SRIM simulation.
• We successfully conducted the UED experiment at SLAC’s MeV-UED facility during the awarded beam
 time between May 6 and May 10. We obtained high-quality diffuse scattering data on W-5Re alloy, c.f.
 Figure 2, and DWF measurements on all the polycrystalline samples, c.f. Figure 3 for the representative
 results on pristine W-5Re alloy.
• We submitted a manuscript on ultrafast laser-induced melting in Cu studied using UED combined with
 MD simulations [1]. Because Cu is a key constituent of Cu-based heat-sink alloys for divertor
 applications, understanding its melting behavior provides insight into the thermal response of these

 203

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 materials under extreme loading conditions relevant to fusion energy systems. In addition, this work
 establishes the most suitable embedded-atom method (EAM) potential for accurately describing the
 thermal heating and melting dynamics of Cu.

Figure 1. Single-crystal thin-film sample preparation for the UEDS measurement. (a) Workflow diagram
illustrating the step-by-step procedure to extract nanometer thin film samples from the bulk single-crystal
W-Re disc using both Xe plasma FIB and Ga FIB. (b) SEM image of the final W-10Re lamella sample, 30

Figure 2. Time-resolved diffuse scattering signal measured for the single-crystal W-5Re sample (as shown
