delays after the laser excitation. Blue color indicates negative change, while yellow color indicates positive
change of the scattering signal, i.e. the diffuse scattering signal. The blue spots are single-crystal diffraction
peaks that are subjected to intensity attenuation due to Debye-Waller effect. (b) Time-resolved diffuse
scattering intensity change along a given high-symmetry line.
Results
In Figure 2, we show the results of the time-resolved diffuse scattering signal for the single-crystal W-5Re
sample. In this measurement, we pumped the sample with 50 fs, 400 nm laser pulses at fluences of ~10
mJ/cm2 and probed the induced structural dynamics with 3.2 MeV electrons with pulse durations of 200 fs.
From the snapshots of the scattering difference patterns (Fig. 2a), we observed clear attenuation of the
diffraction peak intensities due to the Debye-Waller effect, indicated by the blue spots in the difference

 204

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

patterns. The diffuse scattering signal is distributed over the whole pattern with the strongest changes
appearing in regions between diffraction peaks. Because diffuse scattering is sensitive to lattice vibrations,
the time-dependent evolution of the diffuse intensity (Fig. 2b) reflects the creation and annihilation of
phonon populations following photoexcitation [2]. These measurement results will be directly compared to
MD simulations and to validate the interatomic potential models for W-Re alloys.

Figure 3. Debye-Waller factor measurement of the polycrystalline W-5Re sample at a laser fluence of 5
mJ/cm2. (a) Example of a static electron diffraction pattern showing the presence of multiple Debye–
Scherrer rings. (b) Scattering intensity lineout obtained by azimuthally averaging the diffraction pattern; all
the intensity peaks are identified and labeled by corresponding reflection planes of BCC W. (c) Time-
resolved difference scattering intensity of the W-5Re sample after femtosecond laser excitation. The Debye-
Waller effect on scattering intensities is clearly visible at the indicated diffraction peak positions. (d) The
time evolution of the inferred lattice temperature change is shown (red dots). The black solid line is an
exponential fit to the data indicating a time constant of ~2.8 ps. The thermal equilibrium is reached for time
scales > 10 ps resulting in a maximum measured temperature jump of 230 K.
Complementary to the diffuse scattering measurement, we also performed DWF measurements on the
polycrystalline samples to study the ultrafast thermal response in W-Re alloy systems. Debye-Waller factor
is used to describe the attenuation of the diffraction peak intensity due to thermal heating and is given by
[3]:
                                 DWF = 𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒 (−1/3 ∆〈𝑢𝑢 2 〉 ∙ 𝑄𝑄2 ),                                         (1)
with ∆〈𝑢𝑢 the relative change of the mean square displacement (MSD) of the nuclei that is dependent on
 2〉

lattice temperature, and 𝑄𝑄 the scattering vector given by 𝑄𝑄 = 4𝜋𝜋 sin 𝜃𝜃/𝜆𝜆 with 𝜃𝜃 the scattering angle and 𝜆𝜆
the electron wavelength.
In Figure 3, we show the results of temporal evolution of the diffraction measurements on the W-5Re sample
excited at a laser fluence of ~5 mJ/cm2. From the scattering pattern and corresponding intensity lineout
(Fig.3a and Fig. 3b), we can identify the reflection peaks and confirm that the sample is in the BCC W lattice
structure. After the laser excitation, we observed a strong Debye-Waller intensity decay at diffraction peak
positions as shown in Fig.3c. Based on Eqn.1, we extract the MSD from the Q square dependence of the
observed diffraction peaks [3] and obtain the lattice temperature evolution as shown in Fig.3d. This result
will further allow us to extract the electron-phonon coupling (EPC) strength using two-temperature model
(TTM) simulations [3]. By comparing measurements across different alloy compositions and helium-
implanted samples, we will determine how the Re concentration and irradiation-induced defects influence

 205

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

the EPC strength in W–Re alloys. These results will validate the potentials used in MD simulations of fusion
materials under heat load and He accumulated material conditions. These data will also illuminate our
understanding of nonequilibrium processes relevant to collisional cascades [4].

Figure 4. Ultrafast-laser induced melting in Cu. (a) Snapshots of MD simulated atomic configuration at
different times over the melting process. The atoms in the simulations are colored according to the
surrounding atomic structure: green are for atoms in a fcc lattice, grey are for disordered (liquid) atoms. At
6 ps, the slab transitions to a full liquid state, consistent with the UED measurement. (b) Evolution of the
fraction of fcc atoms (dash-dot line) and the average ion temperature (red solid line) revealed from the MD
simulations that have been validated against UED measurement. Figure taken from Ref. [1].

In Figure 4, we show the key result of our study on ultrafast laser-induced melting of Cu [1]. In this work,
we employed intense fs laser pulses to heat and melt the Cu thin film samples and probed the resulting
solid-to-liquid phase transitions using UED. These experimental results enabled direct validation of TTM-
MD simulations of ultrafast melting in Cu. We found that the EAM potential developed by Sheng et al. [5]
shows excellent agreement with the experimental result, demonstrating that this potential accurately
captures the lattice constant and might be used to predict other key bulk thermophysical properties of Cu.

9.13 SOLID SOLUTION STRENGTHENING EFFECT INVESTIGATION ON DISCRETE DISLOCATION
DYNAMICS SIMULATIONS — Hyunsoo Lee, Enrique Martinez (Clemson University), Kieran Edmonds,
Anter A El-Azab (Purdue University), Giacomo Po (University of Miami), Nikhil Admal (University of Illinois
Urbana-Champaign).

As part of enhancing our understanding on microstructure evolution under irradiation, we are currently
developing a model for solid solution strengthening. The most direct way to study strengthening effects is
through molecular dynamics (MD) simulations. However, MD remains computationally expensive for
investigating mesoscale phenomena. Therefore, the main objective of this work is to implement a robust
multiscale modeling pipeline for studying strengthening mechanisms. Specifically, our current approach
within the discrete dislocation dynamics (DDD) model involves analyzing the depinning transition of
dislocation lines. It has been shown that the pinning transition stress under stochastic quenched noise fields
can be related to the critical resolved shear stress (CRSS) in metallic crystals [1]. In our current
development, we are using the Mechanics of Defect Evolution library (MoDELib) as the main framework for
investigating strengthening under such noise fields. This represents a significant advancement in modeling
strengthening effects more accurately, as MoDELib incorporates realistic line tension effects arising from
core energy and segment interactions. Thus, studying depinning transitions under stochastic solid solution
noise in MoDELib dislocation simulations will provide a pathway to investigate strengthening in a more
physically grounded manner. We aim to extend the model to anisotropic materials using this multiscale
framework. This framework will then be applied to study strengthening in BCC FeCr alloys, which are strong
candidates for structural applications in fusion devices.

SUMMARY
We are developing a mesoscale model for solute strengthening based on stochastic pinning theories. A key
objective is to understand the behavior of dislocations in concentrated solid solutions to predict how
chemistry alters dislocation glide and plastic deformation.

Figure 1. Solid solution pinning noise sampled with correlation information from MD. The shear stress xz
(left) and yz (right) components are presented.

 207

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. Solid solution correlation reproduced from the sampled correlated noise. The computed
correlation reproduces the original input correlation.

Initially, the spatial correlation information from MD is extracted only in a 2D format (< 𝜎𝜎(𝑥𝑥, 𝑦𝑦)𝜎𝜎(𝑥𝑥 + 𝑑𝑑𝑥𝑥 , 𝑦𝑦 +
𝑑𝑑𝑦𝑦 ) >). The noise is then computed using this correlation information via the random grating method [2].
We verified the correctness of the implementation in DDD by reproducing the original input autocorrelation
with the ensemble-averaged sampled noise autocorrelation, as shown in Figure 2. However, we found that
sampling the spatial correlation in 2D is not viable for properly simulating noise effects within the non-
singular dislocation model [3]. The current DDD scheme uses this non-singular model to regularize the
dislocation core energy. Consequently, the quenched noise field must be convolved with the non-singular
spreading function [1, 3]. The fact that the spreading function is 3D, while our sampled correlation is 2D
poses challenges in the implementation.
We developed a code base that computes the autocorrelation of each Virial stress component directly from
MD simulations on crystal lattices (e.g., FCC and BCC), as shown in Figures 3 and 4. Using the full 3D
correlation in crystal lattice format resolves the earlier convolution issue with the spreading function, as
both are now 3D functions operating in the same dimensional space. Furthermore, representing the
correlation in lattice format enables analysis of magnitude variations along actual crystallographic
directions, moving beyond the simplified concepts of transverse and longitudinal trends. Currently, the DDD
package lacks a framework to utilize correlation information in this lattice format. Therefore, we are
modifying our previous MD noise implementation to integrate the new correlation into our mesoscale DDD
simulations.

Figure 3. Spatial correlation computed on lattice points. Full 3D spatial correlation information is visualized
in Ovito (left). The auto-correlation of the shear stress xz (middle) and yz (right) components are visualized
on a FCC (001).

Figure 4. Shear component (xz, yz) auto correlations along [100] and [010].

9.14 DEPENDENCE OF RADIATION INDUCED SEGREGATION (RIS) OF CR ON SINK
DIMENSIONALITY AND MORPHOLOGY IN FE-CR ALLOYS — Mohammadhossein Nahavandian,
Enrique Martinez (Clemson University), Anter El-Azab (Purdue University)

The objective of this study was to systematically investigate how sink dimensionality and morphology
control Cr segregation in an Fe-Cr model alloy. We move beyond 1D sink models to understand the effect
of more realistic, multi-dimensional sink geometries [1].

SUMMARY
In this study, relying on the authors’ previous work [2], we investigate how RIS affects chromium (Cr)
distribution in an Fe–3%Cr alloy, focusing on the role of sink geometry in governing segregation behavior.
Using kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) simulations, along with analytical and finite-difference (FD) methods, we
analyze 1D, 2D, and 3D planar sinks, as well as 3D spherical sinks, across a range of temperatures. We
find that sink geometry strongly influences segregation profiles: planar sinks show a linear dependence on
interface spacing, while spherical sinks exhibit a stronger, non-linear dependence. Overall, our
computational and analytical results are in good agreement, providing insight into how microstructural
features control radiation-driven chemical redistribution.

On the analytical side, we derived steady-state solutions for vacancy concentration under irradiation,
including closed-form expressions for spherical geometries, and coupled these to solute transport using
the Wiedersich [3] formalism to obtain Cr concentration profiles. To address more complex sink
morphologies, we formulated and solved the governing diffusion equations for 1D, 2D, and 3D planar sinks
using FD methods following the same methodology with appropriate symmetry and boundary conditions.
In parallel, we performed KMC simulations for Fe–3 at.% Cr across a range of temperatures, domain sizes,
and sink configurations, explicitly capturing defect migration and stochastic transport processes. These
simulations serve both to validate the continuum and FD models. A consistent segregation metric has also
been implemented to quantify chromium redistribution across all geometries.
Results
At low temperatures (e.g., 500 K), we observe pronounced Cr enrichment at sink locations across all planar
geometries (1D, 2D, and 3D). The extent of enrichment increases with system size and is highest at regions
of sink intersection, such as grain boundary triple junctions. FD simulations show smooth vacancy and
solute concentration gradients (Figure 1), while kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) results confirm these trends at
the atomistic level, with consistent steady-state segregation profiles (Figure 2). As temperatures increase,
segregation behavior transitions. At intermediate temperatures (~700 K), Cr enrichment diminishes, and at
higher temperatures (e.g., 900 K), Cr depletion near sinks is observed (Figure 2). This crossover is
attributed to a shift in the dominant transport mechanism, from interstitial-driven enrichment at low
temperatures to vacancy-driven depletion at higher temperatures, driven by inverse Kirkendall effects. Sink
dimensionality influences the magnitude, but not the overall trend, of segregation in planar systems.
Segregation scales approximately linearly with interface density for all planar geometries, although the
absolute magnitude decreases from 1D to 3D due to flux redistribution across multiple directions. In
contrast, spherical sinks exhibit qualitatively different behavior, with a nonlinear dependence on interface
density and an additional sensitivity to irradiation dose rate (Figure 3).

 210

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

concentration (II) in the presence of 2D and 3D GBs showing the effect of planar sinks oriented normal to
the x and y directions at 500 K under an irradiation dose rate of 10−6 dpa/s.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 3. Total steady-state Cr segregation with 0.03 nominal Cr concentration for 1D, 2D and 3D planar
ideal sink morphologies at 500, 700 and 900 K computed by KMC, FD, and analytical expression for
spherical sink vs interface density.

Future work will focus on improving the model by incorporating defect recombination, dynamic sink
evolution, and heterogeneous, non-ideal sink behavior. Extensions to more complex alloy systems and
compositions are also planned to enhance applicability to engineering materials. Additionally, efforts will be
directed towards quantitative validation against experimental data to better connect the model with real
irradiated microstructures and improve predictive capability.

9.15 MECHANISMS OF NON-EQUILIBRIUM ION DYNAMICS IN RADIATION TOLERANT ALLOYS —
M. A. Wood, G. Koknat (Sandia National Laboratories)

It is hypothesized that primary recoil damage and defect recombination involve high-energy, non-equilibrium
electronic excitations that alter the potential energy surface and subsequent ion dynamics. This activates
fundamentally different damage accumulation mechanisms and populations than those inferred from simple
equilibrium theories in metals. Downstream phenomena emerging at longer length and time scales
(chemical segregation, mechanical response, and thermal transport) are intimately linked to these initial
transient non-equilibrium behaviors. We will develop the simulation capability to capture these effects.

SUMMARY
Molecular dynamics simulations play an important role in predicting the damage generated from high-
energy neutron collisions in both plasma facing and structural materials. Interatomic potentials are the
backbone of these predictions, with significant variance in Frenkel pair accumulations depending on model
parameterizations. Machine learned interatomic potentials(MLIAP) solve this discrepancy by preserving
quantum-accuracy but still need adaptations to capture non-equilibrium electronic effects that are present
during the primary damage recoil events. Target materials for these advanced interatomic potentials are
WReOs and Yttria-dispersoid strengthened steels. Theoretical advances here are deployed in the widely
used LAMMPS molecular dynamics to ensure broad adoption in our field.

Development of computationally efficient excited state interatomic potentials would be a breakthrough
capability that enables a wealth of new applications in plasma facing materials and beyond. Our efforts to
date have demonstrated how to construct training sets for MLIAP that capture the temperature-dependent
electronic structure changes (Sommerfeld IAP). These Sommerfeld potentials are now able to capture the
inelastic stopping of fast-moving ions, which is key in predicting the damage from neutrons colliding with
the lattice. Code developments in LAMMPS through this work have brought the capability to dynamically
change the interatomic potential, which we use to toggle between ground- and excited-state MLIAP.
Ongoing work to further optimize MLIAP for material properties of Tungsten is underway, we focus on defect
formation energies, elastic constants, and relative phase stability as metrics in this regard. In addition to
the accuracy of developed potentials, we strive to achieve a pareto optimal tradeoff between accuracy and
computational cost.
For all applications of machine learning, deploying a model beyond the content of its’ training set (called
extrapolation) is dangerous due to a loss of accuracy, and scrutiny should be applied to any prediction that
operates in this mode. Our ML models bridge electronic structure (<103 atoms) and atomic (<1010 atoms)
computational scales, which means there are open questions as to how to train MLIAP to emergent
properties at higher length scales. Due to the excessive computational cost of electronic structure methods
such as density functional theory (DFT), directly creating training data with large atom counts is impossible.
However, new software is being developed to solve this key problem by re-creating large scale structures
into quantifiably representative smaller volumes[1]. The method called the Generalized Representative
Structures (GRS) builds off cluster expansion methods to continuous spatial and chemical degrees of
freedom. Current efforts include a refactorization of the code into an object-oriented framework that permits
multiple different code execution paths and enables new contributions without breaking former functionality.
Public release is available at github.com/FitSNAP/structure-generation.

Generation of interatomic potentials begins with a training set of DFT calculations where values of total free
energy and per-atom Hellman-Feynman forces are required. We carry this out using VASP and an initial
set of atomic configurations that were generated from our group in a previous publication[2]. Our main test
is to develop temperature-dependent potentials, which is approximated here as changes in electronic
temperature that DFT is run with. Several thousand configurations are calculated at discrete values of
electronic temperature, where an Atomic Cluster Expansion (ACE) MLIAP is fit to each unique temperature.
This division of potentials is then smoothly interpolated between in LAMMPS using the newly developed

 214

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

pair_style hybrid/scaled feature, more information is available here docs.lammps.org/pair_hybrid.html.
When running a neutron damage simulation, we calculate a per-atom temperature based on the velocities
of atoms in a 6Å spherical cutoff, this atomic temperature thus defines which MLIAP is used. An example
is given in Figure 1 which demonstrates the initial collision and buildup of the thermal spike for a 50keV
recoil energy. High temperature atoms are smoothly toggled onto the excited state IAP, and as they cool
are dynamically switched back to ground-state dynamics. This capability depends on the definition of a
physically motivated switching function, which is the focus of future work.

Figure 1. Rendered images of a 50keV elastic recoil in W showing the development of the thermal spike.
Atoms are colored by their local temperature on a logarithmic scale between 103 K and 106 K, with velocities
above 50Å/ps drawn as vectors. Sample is cut in the (110) plane to show the interior of the thermal spike.
Results
Interatomic potentials for ground- and excited-state dynamics are currently being optimized and will be
made available soon. Demonstration of this novel model construction will look at the effect of the
temperature-dependent dynamics on Frenkel pair generation over the entire D+T neutron energy spectra.
Additionally, code release for the GRS method will be continuously updated on public github repositories.

Completion of Sommerfeld potentials and demonstration of dynamic potential switching for 1-200keV
neutron recoil energies in Tungsten. Publication will be constructed at this initial results stage. Afterward,
continuation of the work will look at chemical segregation effects under these radiation conditions in
WReOs. Again, we expect that excited-state dynamics will significantly affect the retained defect counts as
the addition of inelastic scattering processes of the recoil energy. Adapting trained potentials with GRS
generated data specific to the thermal spike configurations will being much needed validation of our
predictions.

[1] Goff, J. M., Mullen, C., Yang, S., Starovoytov, O. N., & Wood, M. A. (2025). Generalized representative
structures for atomistic systems. Journal of Physics: Condensed Matter, 37(7), 075901. DOI: 0.1088/1361-
648X/ad9791
[2] Wood, M. A., Cusentino, M. A., Wirth, B. D., & Thompson, A. P. (2019). Data-driven material models for
atomistic simulation. Physical Review B, 99(18), 184305. DOI: 10.1103/PhysRevB.99.184305

11.1 INTRODUCING THE FLAME (FIRE LABORATORY FOR ACCELERATED MATERIALS
EVALUATION) FIRE COLLABORATIVE — Zach Hartwig, Sara Ferry, Lou Wainwright (Massachusetts
Institute of Technology), Dave Sprouster (Stony Brook University), Matt Baldwin (University of California
San Diego), Adrien Couet, Lianyi Chen (University of Madison Wisconsin), Sophie Blondel (University of
Tennessee Knoxville)

The principal objective of the FLAME FIRE Collaborative is to advance our understanding of fusion-relevant
radiation damage effects in bulk materials through the use of 10 - 30 MeV proton irradiation at MIT Plasma
Science and Fusion Center’s Schmidt Laboratory for Materials in Nuclear Technologies (LMNT).

SUMMARY
The FLAME FIRE Collaborative will utilize Schmidt Laboratory for Materials in Nuclear Technologies
(LMNT) to. As the lead institution, MIT provides the central experimental proton irradiation and post-
irradiation materials testing capabilities while four collaborating institutions lead dedicated thrusts in fusion
materials science including: Assessing radiation damage impacts on fusion-relevant steel and copper alloy
tensile and thermoelectric properties (Stony Brook University); understanding bulk radiation damage effects
on plasma-material interactions (University of California San Diego); Using machine learning-informed
additive manufacturing to discover and synthesize novel alloys for characterization (University of Madison
Wisconsin); incorporating experimental data on bulk property evolution with fusion-relevant radiation
damage into the validation and verification framework for multiscale materials modeling (University of
Tennessee Knoxville).

OVERVIEW
Deploying fusion energy on climate-relevant timescales requires rapidly achieving solutions for materials
placed in the extreme environment of a deuterium-tritium burning fusion power plant. Such devices expose
materials to neutron fluxes of up to 1014 to 1015 cm−2 s−1, equivalent to ~15 displacements per atom (dpa)
per year while neutrons above ~5 MeV induce (n,α) transmutation reactions leading to helium generation
of up to 150 atomic parts per million per year. The uncertainties around how such changes will impact the
structural, thermal, and electrical property evolution of fusion materials must be minimized to provide
confidence in the design, engineering, and operation of first-of-a-kind fusion pilot plants (FPPs).

The traditional approaches to radiation damage in materials are insufficient to solve this challenge.
Exposing materials within fission test reactors is constrained by high costs, multi-year cycles, a small and
aging fleet of suitable reactors, and an inability to achieve more than a few dpa/year and fusion-relevant
solid and gaseous transmutation. While proton and self-ion beam irradiation have been used successfully,
the induced damage is limited to the near-surface (~0.1 to ~10 μm), restricting analysis to microstructure
imaging and techniques like nano- and micro-hardness testing to infer structural properties, while multiple
accelerators are required to emulate fusion-induced compositions. Recently, however, direct irradiation of
bulk material by a single intense beam of 10-30 MeV protons has been explored for near-prototypic
irradiation of fusion materials with maximum damage rates approaching ~1 dpa/year and with materials in
more complex, fusion-like environments such as expose to plasma or immersed in hot molten salts [1].
This FIRE Collaborative will be centered around a new 10-30 MeV proton irradiation facility that is presently
under construction at the MIT Plasma Science and Fusion Center and shown as a rendering in Figure 1.
The Collaborative seeks three principal objectives: (1) establishing a high throughput, near prototypic, bulk
materials irradiation platform for end users from academia, national laboratories, and private industries; (2)
executing a targeted research program – driven by scientific end users (sub-awardees on this proposal)
with commercial fusion relevance injected by industry advisors (supporters of this proposal) – to evaluate
and de-risk existing materials for FPPs and to discover and down select advanced materials for nth-of a
kind fusion power plants; and (3) fostering education and workforce development in nuclear materials by
siting a major, student-accessible irradiation capability at the heart of a U.S. university with capabilities
enabled by university collaborators.
The research is structured around First Light Experiments (FLEs) and Integrated Irradiation Platforms (IIPs).
FLEs are near-term high-impact research targets that seek to close fundamental gaps in three areas: (1)

 218

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

the evolution of steels and coppers under irradiation; (2) the impact of bulk radiation damage in plasma-
facing components on the dynamics of plasma-material interactions; and (3) validation of the cluster
dynamic code Xolotl with neutron and proton irradiated bulk materials. IIPs are medium-term deployments
of complex irradiation and modeling capabilities, such as providing opportunities for iterative, high
throughput screening of machine learning / artificial intelligence designed materials and bridging of micro-
to macro-scale modeling with first-of-kind experimental validation of multi-scale modeling.

Figure 1. A rendering of the Schmidt Laboratory for Materials in Nuclear Technologies (LMNT) showing the
30 MeV, 0.8 mA proton cyclotron in the center (blue) and four end beamline stations for materials irradiation,
three of which are shown with additional cylindrical concrete shielding.

The LMNT Facility will be commissioned during Summer 2026 followed by initial assessment of the
irradiation target and related hardware. Activities to prepare and characterize materials before irradiation at
LMNT will continue towards the first irradiations. Commissioning of metal additive manufacturing
capabilities will take place, and developing initial Xolotl multiscale materials modeling using high energy
proton irradiation will continue.

[1] S. J. Jepeal, L. L. Snead, Z. S. Hartwig, “Intermediate energy proton irradiation: Rapid, high-fidelity
materials testing for fusion and fission energy systems”, Materials and Design 200 (2021) 109445.

 219

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

11.2 TEMPERATURE-ACTIVATED DISLOCATION AVALANCHES SIGNALING BRITTLE-TO-DUCTILE
TRANSITION IN BCC MICROPILLARS — Giacomo Po, Matthew Maron, Inam Lalani, William Hixson
(University of Miami), Biao Wang (Sun Yat-sen University), Yang Li (Shanghai University), Nasr Ghoniem
(University of California Los Angeles)

ABSTRACT
We carry out strain-controlled in-situ compression experiments of micron-sized tungsten (W) micropillars in
the temperature range 300–900 K, together with simulations of three-dimensional discrete dislocation
dynamics (DDD) at the same scale. Two distinct regimes are observed. At low temperatures, plastic
deformation appears smooth, both temporally and spatially. Stress fluctuations are consistent with a Wiener
stochastic process resulting from uncorrelated dislocation activity within the pillars. However, high-
temperature stress fluctuations are highly correlated and exhibit features of self-organized criticality (SOC),
with deformation located within well-defined slip bands. The high-temperature stress relaxation statistics
are consistent with a thermally activated nucleation process from the surface. The nature of the transition
between the two regimes is a manifestation of the brittle-to-ductile transition in BCC metals.

[1] Li, Yang, Inam Lalani, Matthew Maron, William Hixson, Biao Wang, Nasr Ghoniem, and Giacomo Po.
"Temperature-activated dislocation avalanches signaling brittle-to-ductile transition in BCC
micropillars." Scripta Materialia 277 (2026): 117233.

 220

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

11.3 RADIATION INDUCED SEGREGATION TO DISLOCATION LOOPS IN ULTRA-HIGH PURITY Fe-Cr
ALLOYS — Siwei Chen (The University of Tennessee), Steven John Zinkle (The University of Tennessee
and Oak Ridge National Laboratory), Haixuan Xu (The University of Tennessee)

Fe-Cr based ferritic steels are leading candidate structural materials for key components in fusion and Gen
IV nuclear reactors. Radiation-induced segregation (RIS) is of particular concern, as the localized
partitioning of Cr at defect sinks can lead to severe mechanical embrittlement and degradation of corrosion
resistance. However, RIS at dislocation loops, the most common defect sinks under irradiation, remains
less understood, yet it is believed to contribute to irradiation hardening, inducing ductility loss that limits the
operational lifetime of structural components. The objective of this project is to study the roles of irradiation
temperature and initial Cr concentration on Cr segregation to dislocation loops in ultra-high purity Fe-Cr
alloys.

SUMMARY
Ultra-high purity Fe-Cr alloys, containing 3.05 and 10.10 wt.% Cr, were irradiated by 8 MeV Fe-ions at 300
and 500 °C. Scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) combined with energy dispersive X-ray
spectroscopy (EDS) were conducted to investigate the chemical redistribution. The RIS of Cr at dislocation
loops is minimal at 300 °C but becomes significant at 500 °C. Cr atoms are found to occupy interstitial loop
interior instead of loop periphery. Non-uniformity in Cr segregation is observed across the dislocation loop
population, where some loops are heavily enriched while neighbors remain Cr-poor. Gaussian-like shape
of Cr concentration profile across edge-on loops was observed in Fe-3Cr alloy, while W-shaped Cr
concentration profile was found in Fe-10Cr alloy. We attribute the Cr segregation mechanism at interstitial
loops to vacancy-Cr drag, based on the STEM observations of loosely packed atom columns associated
with Cr-rich regions. Notably, Cr atoms preferentially locate at tension region of the loop structure due to
their association with vacancies and stabilize the interstitial loops.

The ultra-high purity Fe-Cr alloys, containing 3.05 and 10.10 wt.% Cr, namely Fe3Cr and Fe10Cr alloys,
were manufactured within the framework of the EUROfusion Consortium under contract EFDA-06-1901[1].
The alloys were received as pure ferrite with large grain size (>100 µm) and low dislocation density (<1012
m-2). The chemical composition of the as-received materials was measured by glow discharge mass
spectrometry and is given in Table 1. The alloys were irradiated by 8 MeV Fe-ions with 1×10-4 dpa/s dose
rate up to 0.35 dpa in mid-range depth (1 µm) at 300 and 500 °C. The irradiation matrix is shown in Table
2. The dose and injected ion concentration profiles calculated by SRIM Full-Cascade option combining
damage energy method [2-4] and a displacement energy of 40 eV are shown in Fig. 1.
Table 1. Chemical composition of Fe-Cr alloys.
 Cr (wt.%) C (ppm) S (ppm) O (ppm) N (ppm) P (ppm)
 Fe3Cr          3.05            <4             <2              <6               <2            -
 Fe10Cr         10.10           4              4               4                3             <5

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

After irradiation, cross-sectional TEM samples were prepared by an FEI Helios 5 Hydra CX plasma FIB.
The lamellae were thinned down to 100-150 nm with Xe ions at 30 kV, followed by flash electropolishing to
remove the FIB damage on the surfaces. The flash electropolishing was conducted in 3% HClO4-ethanol
electrolyte with 40 and 80V at -65 °C for 60 ms. STEM-EDS characterization of dislocation loops were
performed using a Thermo Fisher Spectra 300 microscope operated at 200 kV.
Figure 2 illustrate dislocation loops and corresponding Cr and Fe elemental maps under Fe-ion irradiation
with 0.35 dpa at 300 °C acquired along the [001] zone axis for the Fe-3Cr (Fig.2a-c) and Fe-10Cr (Fig.2d-
f) alloys. The dark contrast in STEM-BF images show the distribution of dislocation loops, which appear as
black dots in Fe-3Cr alloy and coffee-bean contrast in the Fe-10Cr alloy. The dislocation loops in the Fe-
10Cr alloy are characterized as <100> type loops. Cr segregation to the dislocation loops is not visible in
either Fe-3Cr or Fe-10Cr alloy.

plane [001] dislocation loops. The corresponding EDS mappings (Fig. 3b-c) demonstrate that Cr
preferentially enriches within the loop interiors. The magnitude of Cr accumulation varies between individual
loops. Quantitative concentration profiles across two loops present the peak Cr content reached 9 at. % for
the smaller loop and 6 at. % for the larger one (Fig. 3d-e).

characterized by a prominent central peak with two adjacent valleys where the Cr content remains lower
than the matrix.

Future work will focus on studying Cr segregation under proton irradiation with lower energy and lower dose
rates. Lowering the proton energy will help separate the effects of simple atomic collisions from complex
damage cascades, allowing us to measure segregation caused purely by isolated defect pairs.
Furthermore, reducing the dose rate is crucial for understanding the balance between radiation pushing
solute atoms toward loops and heat causing them to drift away. Investigating these conditions will provide
key data to improve predictions for material behavior in nuclear environments.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

11.4 UCSD PISCES: A PLASMA MATERIALS INTERACTION RESEARCH PROGRAM FOR LONG
PULSE BURNING PLASMAS — G. R. Tynan, M. J. Baldwin (UCSD, Center for Energy Research)

Advancing a decades-long legacy of leadership in plasma–materials interaction (PMI) science, PISCES is
pioneering a next-generation U.S. facility capable of experimentally replicating the coupled effects of PMI
and displacement damage, an unprecedented capability intended to simulate the effects of neutrons. This
facility also incorporates the deployment of state-of-the-art in-situ and in-operando diagnostics, delivering
real-time, high-precision insight into material performance under fusion relevant conditions.

SUMMARY
A summary of accomplishments (at Year 4) under a five-year research program PISCES: A PMI Research
Program for Long-Pulse Burning Plasmas (U.S. DOE-FES Cooperative Agreement DE-SC0022528) is
described. The PISCES program is advancing toward a U.S. capability in burning plasma materials
interaction science through a major upgrade of the PISCES facility, including integration of an energetic ion
beam. Together, these unique capabilities will enable systematic, science-based investigations of plasma-
facing materials under burning plasma–like conditions on a new facility called POSEIDON.

Status of the PISCES-RF Linear Plasma System
In this reporting period, the PISCES-RF linear plasma device was successfully brought back online and
underwent extensive performance testing, prior to integration as a PMI end-station with a new 9SDH-2
accelerator from NEC. Operation has been verified in both deuterium and helium plasmas, with RF power
ranging from 10–20 kW. (ne ∼ 1018–1019 m−3 at the PMI target), demonstrating robust and reliable
performance across all operating regimes [1].

Figure 1. The PISCES-RF linear plasma device: A steady-state, autonomous fusion first-wall and divertor
plasma simulator..
Status of the NEC 9SDH-2 3 MV Accelerator Commissioning for POSEIDON.
The NEC 9SDH-2 3 MV tandem ion accelerator, to be integrated on POSEIDON, passed factory
acceptance testing in March, and is scheduled for delivery to the PISCES lab in June, where it will be fully
assembled, integrated with PISCES-RF, and commissioned by late August this year. It is currently
operational across its full 300 kV–3 MV range with proton and tungsten beams, meeting specified current
targets (≳100 nA of 20 MeV W6+ and ≳1 μA of 3 MeV He+).

 226

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 2. The POSEIDON NEC 9SDH-2 3 MV tandem ion accelerator at the NEC, Wisconsin factory site.
Progress on POSEIDON Diagnostics
In-operando Transient Grating Spectroscopy – Real-time monitoring of the thermo-mechanical properties
of POSEIDON material targets will be performed using an in-operando transient grating spectroscopy
(TGS) system, currently under development on the PISCES-RF linear device. The TGS system
continuously measures thermal diffusivity and Young’s modulus during plasma and ion irradiation via laser
based dual-homodyne setup. The TGS system probes to an effective depth of ~1.6 μm in W, which is close
to the expected POSEIDON damage peak for 20 MeV W6+ ion damage. Designed for flexible, remote
operation with motorized optics and a developing user interface for streamlined data acquisition, this
capability will enable systematic, high-precision characterization of thermo-mechanical response of material
targets undergoing both displacement damage and plasma-surface interaction [2].

Figure 3. The POSEIDON in-operando TGS system. Realtime monitoring of material target thermo-
mechanical evolution during plasma and damaging.
POSEIDON Ion Beam Analysis end station development – In FY24, construction began on an ion beam
analysis (IBA) end-station for ex-situ nuclear reaction analysis (NRA) and Rutherford backscattering
spectrometry (RBS). At present the vacuum end-station is near completion and ready for accelerator
integration in June of this year. This capability is essential for analyzing POSEIDON target material surfaces
for composition, including hydrogen isotope fractions and helium. We acknowledge funding for this upgrade
was by the UC San Diego Dean of Engineering as matching support to our PISCES DOE Cooperative
Agreement.

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Figure 4. The POSEIDON IBA end station for analyzing POSEIDON target material surfaces for
composition, hydrogen isotope fraction and helium.

SUMMARY OF PISCES PMI RESEARCH
D retention in e-beam powder-bed fused (3-D printed) tungsten exposed to high-flux deuterium plasma in
Pisces-RF [3].
Additively manufactured (AM) tungsten targets produced by electron-beam powder-bed fusion (3D printing)
were exposed to high-flux deuterium plasma in the PISCES-RF linear device, with the plasma-facing
surface normal to the AM build direction. Deuterium retention was measured by thermal desorption mass
spectrometry after exposure to ~50 eV D⁺ ion fluxes over fluences of 5×1024–5×1026 m⁻² and temperatures
of 400–1000 K. Total D retention in AM W was comparable to identically exposed conventional sintered W,
but thermal release behavior differed significantly. AM W exhibited reduced trapping in defect sites typical
of sintered W and moderately increased trapping in higher-energy defect states. Its fluence dependence
also differed, showing slower-than-Fickian D uptake, whereas sintered W matched prior polycrystalline W
results from PISCES-B. Hydrogen transport modeling suggests that interconnected pathways enabling D
release to the surface during plasma exposure explains the reduced uptake in AM W. This work
demonstrated that 3D-printed W can withstand fusion-relevant D plasma while exhibiting distinct trapping
and release behavior, providing critical insight for optimizing 3D printed plasma-facing materials in fusion
reactors.
A validated ballistic-He fuzz-growth model for high temperature erosive-plasma regimes [4].
A ballistic helium transport model within tungsten fuzz was previously developed to explain helium-induced
nano-structuring that alters near-surface properties. By including ion reflection from tendril sides and a 5 eV
implantation threshold, the model reproduced fluence-fuzz thickness trends from experiments at a single
temperature and low-ion energy. It has now been expanded to account for material temperature via an
Arrhenius relation and helium-induced sputtering, successfully replicating experimental fuzz thicknesses
across helium fluences of 0.7–110 × 10²⁶ m⁻², temperatures of 993–1300 K, and ion energies of 50–500 eV.
This expanded He transport model enables better prediction of tungsten fuzz growth under fusion-relevant
conditions, that in turn can guide the design and selection of fusion plasma-facing components in regimes
where helium-induced nano-structuring is expected.
Further investigation of ‘Deuterium supersaturated surface layer in tungsten: ion energy dependence [5].
In continuing work, we address the incident ion energy threshold (~20 eV) for deuterium supersaturated
surface layer (DSSL) formation through new D plasma exposure experiments. Additional studies, scanning
ion flux, fluence, and sample temperature showed that non-kinetic (ballistic) processes contribute to DSSL
growth. Plasma impurity effects were also analyzed via post-exposure surface composition. This work was
conducted in collaboration with NIFS, Japan, and the Max-Planck-Institut für Plasmaphysik, Germany,
under the US-Japan Fusion R&D program.

 228

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

Helium line emission spectroscopy to measure plasma parameters using modeling and machine learning
in low-temperature plasmas [6].
A review was produced on helium line emission spectroscopy in low-temperature plasmas (Te ≤ 10 eV),
where He I line intensity ratios (LIRs) are used to determine electron density (ne) and temperature (Te).
Conventional methods compare measured LIRs with collisional-radiative (CR) model predictions,
accounting for effects like radiation trapping via optical escape factors. Recently, machine learning (ML)
approaches have been developed, training models on measured LIRs and known ne or Te, achieving more
accurate predictions than traditional CR-based methods for He and other species. This work advances ML
enhanced He I spectroscopy so that it now delivers faster, accurate measurements of electron density and
temperature in low-temperature plasmas, potentially outperforming conventional collisional-radiative
methods.
Integral X-ray diffuse scattering (XRDS) development for studying irradiation-induced dislocation loops in
single crystals.
Numerical diffuse scattering cross-section calculations were used to establish a rigorous basis for
determining the concentration and size distribution of dislocation loops in irradiated single crystals from
integral XRDS measurements. Differential XRDS intensities for prismatic {111} type, dislocation loops were
numerically calculated as a function of loop radius, R, and wavevectors, q, relative to Bragg reflections in
tungsten. The results showed the well-known 1/q2 Huang scattering form at small-q that transitions to a ∼
1/q4 dependence associated with the Stokes-Wilson approximation for q ≳ 1/R. More importantly, they show
further that the 1/q4 falloff is not the asymptotic large-q form of the diffuse scattering from small loops (R <
200 Å) as has often been assumed. Rather, for loop sizes as small as R ≈ 5 Å with strong curvature, the
calculations show definitively that the scattering transitions to a robust 1/q5 fall-off at larger-q that arises
due to the local strains near the dislocation core defining the circumference of dislocation loops. The
presence of this 1/q5 asymptotic form for both small and large loops was experimentally confirmed using
an integral XRDS measurement around the 110 reflection on self-ion irradiated tungsten combined with
numerically calculated integral XRDS cross-sections. Accordingly, the historical two-region theoretical
treatment of the cross-sections for integral XRDS is extended to a three-region model that has direct
sensitivity to the (first moment) dislocation line lengths of dislocation loops. Importantly, these developments
have now enabled the use of both numerical and analytically modeled cross-sections to make accurate
integral XRDS determinations of dislocation loop sizes and concentrations using modest intensity,
laboratory X-ray sources.
Effect of grain size on defect annealing in displacement-damaged tungsten [7].
In this study, we used tungsten trap evolution to reveal how grain size and heat treatment affect deuterium
retention. Tungsten layers with grain sizes of 1 μm, 100 nm, and 10 nm were self-damaged to 0.23 dpa with
20.3 MeV W ions and vacuum-annealed at 473 K or 673 K before deuterium plasma exposure. Thermal
desorption spectroscopy and rate-equation modeling revealed trap evolution: 10 nm grains retained traps
at 473 K, while 1 μm and 100 nm samples lost 34% and 9% of traps. At 673 K, trap concentrations dropped
strongly across all samples, reducing deuterium retention by 34%, 27%, and 14%, respectively, consistent
with differing grain-boundary densities.
Analytical model for laser-induced transient grating measurements of thermal diffusivity in non-opaque
materials [8].
Laser-induced transient grating spectroscopy (TGS) is commonly used to measure thermal diffusivity, but
standard analysis assumes fully opaque or transparent materials. This work analytically solves the
thermoelastic equations for isotropic materials with finite laser penetration, and benchmarking on single-
crystal silicon shows it significantly improves thermal diffusivity accuracy over traditional opaque-material
assumptions. This model thus provides more precise thermal diffusivity measurements and thus might
better link our studies of plasma and ion induced defects to heat transport in fusion materials.
CONTINUING PISCES COLLABORATIONS AND OTHER RESEARCH
We report continuing PISCES collaborative efforts that support the general US domestic and international
fusion communities. This effort is inclusive of efforts to support ORNL and evaluating emerging fusion
materials studies. To date, PISCES partnered with multiple institutions on fusion PMI research inclusive of:

 229

Fusion Reactor Materials Program Jun 30, 2025 DOE/ER-0313/78 – Volume 78

 • ORNL (MPEX / PISCES-RF impurity transport): Studied impurity production, transport, and
 deposition in high-density helicon plasmas, supporting proto-MPEX observations of downstream
 impurity deposition.
 • ORNL (UHTC materials): Exposed ultra-high-temperature boride ceramics to fusion-relevant
 plasmas to assess boundary material performance.
 • NIFS Japan (US-Japan collaboration): Conducted TEM studies on deuterium-supersaturated
 tungsten layers formed in PISCES-A, providing new insight into deuterium super-saturated layer
 structure.
 • Shimane University (US-Japan collaboration): Initiated installation of in-situ spectroscopic
 ellipsometry on PISCES-RF.
 • North Carolina State University & Radia-beam Inc: Evaluated additively manufactured tungsten,
 examining deuterium retention and thermal diffusivity.
 • UCSD (Vechio Lab): Began PMI testing of radiation-tolerant high-entropy alloys discovered by
 LLNL.
